Schematic of the proof of Lemma
III.6.15
.

Part IV
Path Lifting

This part gives the main technical tool needed to prove the Concordance Implies Regular Homotopy Theorem
VI.1.1
,
namely the Path Lifting Theorem
IV.5.1
. First we need some more advanced setup, namely
the lifting lemmas from Sections
IV.1
,
IV.2
,
IV.3
, and
IV.4
.

The Path Lifting Theorem is one of the key novelties of this paper. To state it, suppose that

N
is a compact manifold, and let
f_{\tau}\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
be an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path of functions. The precise definition of an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path was given in Definition
III.1.1
.
Let
G\colon N\to\Omega
be a generic immersion
and suppose
F_{0}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is such that
F_{0}\circ G=f_{0}
. We ask whether there exists a regular path of immersions
G_{\tau}\colon N\to\Omega
with
G_{0}=G
,
and an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path functions
F_{\tau}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}=f_{\tau}
and
F_{\tau}
has no new critical points away from
G_{\tau}(N)
.
The goal of this part is to prove Path Lifting Theorem
IV.5.1
, which states that this is possible. An important step in the proof is the
finger move
. We delay a detailed description of the finger move until Part
V
, since this is a significant detour which takes around 30 pages.

Throughout Part
III
, we assume that
N
has all
connected components of the same dimension. Necessary changes if this is not the case are given in Section
IV.6
at the end of this part.

IV.1.
Generalities on path lifting

In this section we
make precise the notion of lifting a path of functions, and
address the problem of lifting elementary paths.

Definition IV.1.1
.

Suppose
f_{\tau}\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
is an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path of functions on
N
, as in Definition
III.1.1
. Let
G\colon N\looparrowright\Omega
be a generic immersion
with
M=G(N)
.
Finally, let
F\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be an immersed Morse function such that
F\circ G=f_{0}
.

Suppose we are given a path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
with
\tau\in[0,1]
,
F_{\tau}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and
G_{\tau}\colon N\to\Omega
such that
F_{0}=F
,
G_{0}=G
, and
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
is a regular double path (see Definition
III.1.27
) with the property that
F_{\tau}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is Morse
for all
\tau\in[0,1]
(not necessarily immersed Morse).
Set
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}=F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
.

(1)

We say that the pair
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})

lifts the family
f_{\tau}
if
f_{\tau}=\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
.

(2)

We say that
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})

weakly lifts the family
f_{\tau}
if
f_{1}=\widetilde{f}_{1}
and
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
is
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-homotopic to
f_{\tau}
.

It is a key property of the lift that the function
F_{\tau}
is Morse for all
\tau\in[0,1]
, that is, it
does not acquire any critical points in the top stratum. It is not hard to lift a path
f_{\tau}
at the cost of creating critical points away from
N
: take
G_{\tau}=G
for all
\tau
, choose a path of functions on
\Omega
extending the given function on
N
, and perturb it to be generic
using the methods of Subsection
III.1.3
. But lifting this way, we would not have
any control on the critical points which might appear on the zeroth stratum, so this would not be a very useful approach.

We begin with Lemma
IV.1.2
, which lifts a path of Morse functions with no rearrangements.
Then we prove lemmas on lifting rearrangements, births, and deaths. These lemmas give us an inductive argument to prove the main theorem, namely the
Path Lifting Theorem
IV.5.1
.

Lemma IV.1.2

(Lifting paths of excellent Morse functions)
.

(a)

Assume
N
and
\Omega
are closed.
Suppose
f_{\tau}
is a path of excellent Morse functions on

N
,
G_{0}\colon N\to\Omega
is a generic immersion, and
F_{0}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is an immersed
Morse function for
M=G_{0}(N)
, such that
F_{0}\circ G_{0}=f_{0}
.

Then there exists a family
\Psi_{\tau}
of self-diffeomorphisms of
N
, and a family
\Upsilon_{\tau}
of orientation preserving self-diffeomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}
,
such that the composition

F_{\tau}=\Upsilon_{\tau}\circ F_{0}\circ G_{0}\circ\Psi_{\tau}

defines a lift of the path
f_{\tau}
, that is, for
G_{\tau}=G_{0}\circ\Psi_{\tau}
we have
f_{\tau}=F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
.

(b)

If
N
and
\Omega
are compact,
f_{\tau}
is a neat path,
F_{0}
is neat and
G_{0}
is neat, then the statement holds, with

(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
also being neat.

Proof.

Assume first that
N
and
\Omega
are closed.
By stability of Morse functions
[
GG73
, Proposition III.2.2]
, there is a family
\Psi_{\tau}
of self-diffeomorphisms of
N

and a family
\Upsilon_{\tau}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
of strictly increasing functions such that
f_{\tau}=\Upsilon_{\tau}\circ f_{0}\circ\Psi_{\tau}
. We define
G_{\tau}=G_{0}\circ\Psi_{\tau}

and
F_{\tau}=\Upsilon_{\tau}\circ F_{0}
. It is elementary to check that
F_{\tau}
satisfies all the needed assumptions.

If
N
and
\Omega
have boundary, the argument of
[
GG73
]
provides us with
\Psi_{\tau}
being the identity near the boundary of
N

and
\Upsilon_{\tau}
being the identity near
0
. This means that the path
G_{\tau}
is independent of
\tau
near the boundary of
N
,
while
F_{\tau}
is independent of
\tau
near the boundary of
\Omega
. That is to say, the double path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau)}
is neat.
∎

Remark IV.1.3
.

Lemma
IV.1.2
not only says that the path
f_{\tau}
can be lifted, but also gives a very precise recipe for a lift. For example, it follows
from the form of
F_{\tau}
and
G_{\tau}
that the image
M=G_{\tau}(N)
is independent of
\tau
, only the parametrisation changes. In particular,

G_{\tau}
is a generic immersion for all
\tau
and
F_{\tau}
is a path of immersed Morse functions. In particular
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})

is a regular double path. Moreover,
if
\xi
is a grim vector field for
F_{0}
, then one readily checks that
\xi
is a grim vector field for the whole family
F_{\tau}
.

The following corollary
will be useful.

Corollary IV.1.4
.

Suppose
f_{\tau}
is an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path of functions, and
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
are such that
f_{\tau}
and
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}:=F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
are left-homotopic.
Then there exists a regular double path
(F^{\prime}_{\tau},G^{\prime}_{\tau})
that weakly lifts
f_{\tau}
.

Proof.

For technical reasons, we apply a small perturbation to
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
, denoted
by
(\widetilde{F}_{\tau},\widetilde{G}_{\tau})
so that the latter forms a regular double path; see Corollary
III.1.28
.
As
G_{0}
is a generic immersion, and
F_{0}
is an immersed Morse function with respect to
G_{0}(N)
, we may assume that
the perturbation fixes
(F_{0},G_{0})
. The condition that
(\widetilde{F}_{\tau},\widetilde{G}_{\tau})
is a
small perturbation
means that
we insist that
\widetilde{F}_{\tau}\circ\widetilde{G}_{\tau}
and
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
be left-homotopic. From this it follows that
\widetilde{F}_{\tau}\circ\widetilde{G}_{\tau}
and
f_{\tau}
are left-homotopic. Let
\widetilde{h}_{\sigma,\tau}
be such left-homotopy. That is to say,
\widetilde{h}_{0,\tau}=\widetilde{F}_{\tau}\circ\widetilde{G}_{\tau}
,
while
\widetilde{h}_{1,\tau}=f_{\tau}
. Denote
g_{\tau}=\widetilde{h}_{\tau,1}
;
see Figure
20
.

perturbation
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
(\widetilde{F}_{\tau},\widetilde{G}_{\tau})
f_{\tau}
g_{\tau}
left-homotopy
\mathcal{F}^{1}

Figure 20.
Schematic of the proof of Corollary
IV.1.4
.

Since
(\widetilde{F}_{\tau},\widetilde{G}_{\tau})
is a regular double path,

\widetilde{G}_{1}
is a generic immersion and
\widetilde{F}_{1}
is an immersed Morse function with respect to
\widetilde{G}_{1}
. Lemma
IV.1.2

allows us to find a lift of
g_{\tau}
starting from
(\widetilde{F}_{1},\widetilde{G}_{1})
. We denote this lift by
(\widetilde{F}_{1+\tau},\widetilde{G}_{1+\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,1]
. By construction,
\widetilde{G}_{1+\tau}
is a generic immersion for all
\tau
, and
\widetilde{F}_{1+\tau}
is an immersed Morse function. That is,

(\widetilde{F}_{1+\tau},\widetilde{G}_{1+\tau})
is a regular double path. We concatenate now the paths
(\widetilde{F}_{\tau},\widetilde{G}_{\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,1]

and
(\widetilde{F}_{1+\tau},\widetilde{G}_{1+\tau})
, to obtain a path
(\widetilde{F}_{\tau},\widetilde{G}_{\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,2]
.
The path is a regular double path (as a concatenation of two regular double paths), with
\widetilde{F}_{2}\circ\widetilde{G}_{2}=g_{1}=f_{1}
.

After rescaling the time, we obtain a path
(F^{\prime}_{\tau},G^{\prime}_{\tau})
, with
F^{\prime}_{\tau}=\widetilde{F}_{2\tau}
,
G^{\prime}_{\tau}=\widetilde{G}_{2\tau}
. We claim that
f^{\prime}_{\tau}:=F^{\prime}_{\tau}\circ G^{\prime}_{\tau}
is
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-homotopic to
f_{\tau}
. The homotopy is quite easy to construct explicitly. We define it as a concatenation
of the homotopy
\widetilde{h}_{\sigma,\tau}
and the path
g_{\sigma}
.

h_{\sigma,\tau}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{h}_{\sigma,2\tau}&\tau\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
g_{\sigma+(2\tau-1)(1-\sigma)}&\tau\geq\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

It is routine to check that
\widetilde{h}_{\sigma,\tau}
being a left-homotopy implies that
h_{\sigma,\tau}
is an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-homotopy.
∎

Motivated by Corollary
IV.1.4
we introduce a piece of terminology.

Definition IV.1.5
.

A path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,\ell]
can be
promoted to a weak lift of
f_{\tau}
, if (up to a possible perturbation
of
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
rel. starting point, there exists a regular double path
(F_{\ell+\tau},G_{\ell+\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,1]

such that the whole path
(F_{(\ell+1)\tau},G_{(\ell+1)\tau})
is a weak lift of
f_{\tau}
.

Corollary
IV.1.4
admits the following generalisation.

Lemma IV.1.6
.

Suppose
f_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
is an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
path, while
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
, for
\tau\in[0,\ell]
and
\ell>1
, is a regular double path such that:

•

g_{\sigma}:=F_{\sigma}\circ G_{\sigma}
,
\sigma\in[0,\ell-1]
is an
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-path;

•

\widetilde{f}_{\tau}:=F_{\ell-1+\tau}\circ G_{\ell-1+\tau}
is lax homotopic to
f_{\tau}
over
g_{\tau}
.

Then there exists a path
(F_{\ell+\tau},G_{\ell+\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,1]
, such that
f_{\tau}
and
F_{(\ell+1)\tau}\circ G_{(\ell+1)\tau}
are homotopic rel. endpoints as
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-paths. That is,
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
can be promoted to a weak lift of
f_{\tau}
.

Proof.

For simplicity, assume that
\ell=2
. Let
h_{\sigma,\tau}
be a lax homotopy over
g_{\tau}
between
f_{\tau}
and
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
. Set
\widetilde{g}_{\sigma}=h_{1-\sigma,1}
. This is an
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-path, moreover,
\widetilde{g}_{0}=\widetilde{f}_{1}=F_{2}\circ G_{2}
and
\widetilde{g}_{1}=f_{1}
. Lift this path using Lemma
IV.1.2
to a regular double path
(F_{2+\tau},G_{2+\tau})
with
F_{2+\tau}\circ G_{2+\tau}=\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
. We claim that
F_{3\tau}\circ G_{3\tau}
is homotopic to
f_{\tau}
.
This is routine. We set

\widetilde{h}_{\sigma,\tau}=\begin{cases}g_{3\sigma\cdot\tau}&\tau\in[0,1/3]\\
h_{\sigma,3\tau-1}&\tau\in[1/3,2/3]\\
\widetilde{g}_{3\sigma\tau-3\tau+1}&\tau\in[2/3,1].\end{cases}

With this choice
h_{0,\tau}
is a reparametrisation of
f_{\tau}
, while
h_{1,\tau}
is a concatenation of
g_{\tau}
,
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
and
\widetilde{g}_{\tau}
. That is,
h_{1,\tau}=F_{3\tau}\circ G_{3\tau}
up to reparametrisation.
∎

IV.2.
Lifting paths of birth

Throughout Section
IV.2
, we assume that
N
and
\Omega
are closed. The proof can be easily adapted to the case that

N
and
\Omega
have nonempty boundary, provided that
f_{\tau}
is a neat path,
F_{0}
is neat,
and
G_{0}
is neat.
The goal of this section is to prove the following result.

Lemma IV.2.1

(Lifting paths of birth)
.

Suppose
f_{\tau}\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
is a path of birth of index
h
.
Let
G_{0}\colon N\to\Omega
be a generic immersion and let
F_{0}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be an immersed Morse function such that

F_{0}\circ G_{0}=f_{0}
.

Then there exists a path
F_{\tau}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and
a path
G_{\tau}
of generic immersions, such that:

•

the path
F_{\tau}
is excellent Morse except for
\tau=1/2
;

•

F_{\tau}
has a birth at the first stratum for
\tau=1/2
;

•

(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
weakly lifts the path
f_{\tau}
;

•

G_{\tau}
is as close to
G_{0}
as we please
(
for positive distance
)
.

Proof.

The idea of the proof is to perturb the map
G
, if needed, so that the point at which the birth occurs is on the first stratum.
This constructs the path
G_{\tau}
. The next part creates the birth for the immersed Morse function
F
. Finally, we invoke Uniqueness of Birth
Proposition
III.5.6
to show that the path we construct indeed lifts the original path. Except for Lemma
IV.2.2
below, the result is straightforward. The details follow.

Without loss of generality we may assume that the birth occurs at
\tau=1/2
. Let
q\in N
be the point at which birth occurs and let
c=f_{1/2}(q)
be the level set of
q
. Suppose
\delta>0
is such that,
for
\tau\in[1/2-\delta,1/2)
, no critical point of
f_{\tau}
has critical value
c
. Such a
\delta
exists, because
f_{1/2}
has
q
as the only critical point at the level set
c
. The path
f_{\tau}
for
\tau\in[0,1/2-\delta]
is a path of excellent Morse functions, which we can lift by Lemma
IV.1.2
. It is enough to lift the restricted
path
f_{\tau}
for
\tau\geq 1/2-\delta
. The interval
[1/2-\delta,1/2]
can be stretched to
[0,1/2]
, so the path

f_{\tau}
for
\tau\in[1/2-\delta,1]
can be reparametrised to a path on
[0,1]
agreeing with the old path on
[1/2,1]
.
Therefore, without losing generality, we can assume that
f_{\tau}
has no critical points at the level set
c
for
\tau<1/2
.

The subset of
C^{\infty}(N,\Omega)
consisting of maps that take
q
to the first stratum is clearly open-dense. Hence, we perturb
G_{0}
in such a way that
q
is mapped to the first stratum.
This perturbation can be done via a family of generic immersions. That is, let

G_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,1/4]
, be a path of generic immersions, supported near
p
and
such that
p:=G_{1/4}(q)
belongs to the first stratum. By Lemma
III.1.23
, we may and will assume that
G_{\tau}
is
F_{0}
-regular (see Definition
III.1.20
).
Extend the path
G_{\tau}
by setting
G_{\tau}=G_{1/4}
for
\tau>1/4
.

Choose a neighbourhood
U
of
p
with coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{k}
such that
G_{1/4}(N)\cap U=\{y_{1}=\dots=y_{k}=0\}
. Note that
F_{0}
restricted to
G_{1/4}(N)
does not have a critical point at
p
. Therefore there is an index
i=1,\dots,n
, such that
\frac{\partial F_{0}}{\partial x_{i}}(p)\neq 0
. Without loss of generality we assume that
i=n
.

Consider the map

\psi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{k})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},F_{0}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{k}),y_{1},\dots,y_{k}).

This map is a local diffeomorphism since the derivative is invertible. By composing the coordinate chart with the local inverse map, and shrinking
U
if necessary to where the inverse is defined,
we obtain new coordinates on
U
. By a mild abuse of notation we still denote these coordinates by
(x_{1},\dots,y_{k})
, but we have that
F_{0}\equiv x_{n}
.

Set
F_{\tau}=F_{0}
for
\tau\leq 1/4
, so that for the time
\tau\in[0,1/4]
we modify (perturb)
G
and for
\tau>1/4
we modify
F
.
Choose a bump function
\phi
supported on
U
, with
\phi\equiv 1

in a smaller neighbourhood
U^{\prime}
of
p
.
For
\tau\in[1/4,3/4]
, define

F_{\tau}=F_{0}+\mathfrak{u}_{2\tau-1/2}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})-x_{n}+(\tau-1/4)\varepsilon\phi y_{1},

where
\mathfrak{u}_{\tau}
is the elementary path of birth of index
h

as in Definition
III.5.2
such that
\mathfrak{u}_{\tau}=\mathfrak{u}_{0}
away from
U^{\prime}
. The rôle of the last
term in the definition of
F_{\tau}
is to make sure that the function
F_{\tau}
has no new critical points when regarded as a function on

\Omega
. The real number
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently
small and will be determined shortly.

Lemma IV.2.2
.

For sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta>0
, the function
F_{\tau}
for
\tau\in[0,1/2+\delta]
has no critical
points on the zeroth stratum in
U^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
U^{\prime\prime}\subseteq U^{\prime}
be an open subset containing
p
such that
\phi\equiv 1
on
U^{\prime\prime}
. We choose
\delta>0
by the condition
that all the critical points of the function
\mathfrak{u}_{2\tau-1/2}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
belong to
U^{\prime\prime}
for
1/4\leq\tau\leq 1/2+\delta
. Such

\delta
exists, because
\mathfrak{u}_{2\tau-1/2}
creates a critical point at
p\in U^{\prime\prime}
for
\tau=1/2
and the critical points
p_{-},p_{+}

of
\mathfrak{u}_{\tau}
are at points
(0,\dots,0,\pm\alpha(\tau))
, where
\alpha(\tau)
depends smoothly on
\tau
.

This means that for
1/4\leq\tau\leq 1/2+\delta
,

\mathfrak{u}_{2\tau-1/2}
has no critical points in
\overline{U^{\prime}}\setminus U^{\prime\prime}
. Choose a Riemannian metric. Then there is a constant
c>0

such that
\|D\mathfrak{u}_{\tau}(z)\|>c
for all
z\in\overline{U^{\prime}}\setminus U^{\prime\prime}
. Set
C
to be the upper bound on
\overline{U^{\prime}}\setminus U^{\prime\prime}

for
\|D\phi y_{1}\|
. If
\varepsilon<\frac{c}{2C}
, then

\|DF_{\tau}\|>c-\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}$}\varepsilon C>c-\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{3cC}{8C}$}=\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{5}{8}$}c>\mbox{\footnotesize$\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}$}c

on
\overline{U^{\prime}}\setminus U^{\prime\prime}
, so the derivative is nonzero there. Next, on
U^{\prime\prime}
,
the derivative with respect to
y_{1}
is positive, so there are no critical points on
U^{\prime\prime}
. Thus there are no critical points on the zeroth stratum of
U^{\prime}
, as desired.
∎

Corollary IV.2.3
.

The function
F_{\tau}
is immersed Morse for
\tau\in[0,1/2+\delta]\setminus\{1/2\}
. For
\tau>1/2
it has two more critical
points on the first stratum than for
\tau<1/2
.

We continue with the proof of Lemma
IV.2.1
.
Reparametrise
F_{\tau}
to
F_{\vartheta(\tau)}
where the diffeomorphism
\vartheta\colon[0,1/2+\delta]\to[0,1]
has positive derivative
and maps
1/4
to
1/4
and
1/2
to
1/2
. The reparametrized path still acquires the new critical points at
\tau=1/2
, but the path is extended up to
[0,1]
(and not
on
[0,1/2+\delta]
).

Define
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}=F_{\vartheta(\tau)}\circ G_{\tau}
. Note that
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
, for
\tau\in[0,1/4]
, is arbitrary close to
f_{0}
, in particular
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}

belongs to the same stratum of
\mathcal{F}^{0}
as
f_{0}
. Next,
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
for
\tau\in[1/4,1]
acquires a critical point at
\tau=1/2
, that is,
it crosses the stratum
\mathcal{F}^{1}
at
\tau=1/2
. The crossing is transverse, the path
\mathfrak{u}_{\tau}
crosses the stratum transversely and
the reparametrisation
\vartheta
we used has non-vanishing derivative at
\tau=1/2
. In particular,
\tau\mapsto\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
is a path of birth.

Now the birth on
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
occurs at the same point of
N
as the birth for
f_{\tau}
. Moreover, the indices of the critical points created
by
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
and
f_{\tau}
are the same. We invoke Proposition
III.5.6
to see that
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
and
f_{\tau}
are
left-homotopic. By Corollary
IV.1.4
, the double path
(F_{\vartheta(\tau)},G_{\tau})
can be promoted to a weak lift of
f_{\tau}
.
∎

IV.3.
Lifting paths of rearrangement

Next, we lift paths of rearrangement. Throughout Section
IV.3
we
will assume that
k=\dim\Omega-\dim N>1
.
Remark
IV.3.3
explains the obstruction to lifting if
k=1
, so the assumption is necessary.

Lemma IV.3.1

(Lifting paths of rearrangement)
.

Let
f_{0}\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
be a Morse function and let
\eta
be a gradient-like vector field for
f_{0}
. Assume
\eta
is Morse–Smale.
Let
q_{-},q_{+}
be two critical points of
f_{0}
with
\ind q_{-}\geq\ind q_{+}
and
f_{0}(q_{-})=a<b=f_{0}(q_{+})
. Suppose
there are no other critical points of
f_{0}
in
f_{0}^{-1}[a,b]
.
Assume that there exists a generic immersion
G_{0}\colon N\to\Omega
and an immersed excellent Morse function
F_{0}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}

such that
f_{0}=F_{0}\circ G_{0}
.

If
k>1
, then an elementary
\eta
-path
f_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
, moving the critical point
q_{-}
above the critical point
q_{+}
,
can be weakly lifted to a regular double path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
such that
F_{\tau}
is Morse on the zeroth and first stratum for all
\tau
, and
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
is an
\eta
-path
(
Definition
III.3.9
)
.

The path
F_{\tau}
is supported on
F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
, while
G_{\tau}
is supported on
f_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
, where
\varepsilon>0
can be chosen as small as we please. Moreover, if
f_{\tau}
is neat,
G_{0}
is very neat, and
F_{0}
is neat,
then
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
is neat.

We recall that the condition that
f_{\tau}
is elementary means
that
\tau\mapsto f_{\tau}(q_{-})-f_{\tau}(q_{+})
has precisely one zero in the interval
[0,1]
and this is a simple zero; compare Definition
III.3.6
. This amounts to saying that
f_{\tau}
intersects
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\beta}
transversely at a single point.

In general, not all maps
G_{\tau}
will be generic immersions, in particular, some new self-intersections of the components of
N
can be created.
In the follow-up to Lemma
IV.3.1
, we control the double points created by
G_{\tau}
.

Addendum IV.3.2
.

The path
G_{\tau}
of Lemma
IV.3.1
can be constructed to have the following properties.

(IR-1)

if
G_{0}
is an embedding, then
G_{\tau}
is an embedding for all
\tau
;

(IR-2)

if
N=N_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup N_{\ell}
and the images
N_{i}
and
N_{j}

(
for all
i\neq j)
under
G_{0}
are disjoint, then
G_{\tau}(N_{i})\cap G_{\tau}(N_{j})=\emptyset
for all
\tau
whenever
i\neq j
.

(IR-3)

if
k=2
and
n\leq 3
, then
G_{\tau}
is a generic immersion for all
\tau
.

The proof of Lemma
IV.3.1
takes the remainder of Section
IV.3
.
Addendum
IV.3.2
is proved alongside Lemma
IV.3.1
.
We first introduce the notation, and list two extra conditions
(LR-1)
and
(LR-2)
. Then we prove the lemma assuming
(LR-2)
.
Next, we arrange the function
F_{0}
and the embedding
G_{0}

in such a way that
(LR-1)
holds. Then we show how to improve a pair
(F_{0},G_{0})
satisfying
(LR-1)

so that
(LR-2)
is satisfied. A summary of the entire proof is given in Subsection
IV.3.8
.

IV.3.1.
Notation of the proof of Lemma
IV.3.1

Let
h_{-}:=\ind q_{-}
,
h_{+}:=\ind q_{+}
. Set
p_{-}:=G_{0}(q_{-})
and
p_{+}:=G_{0}(q_{+})
.
Then
p_{-},p_{+}
are critical points of
F_{0}
of indices
h_{-},h_{+}
, respectively. Both of
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
are at
depth
1
. In particular, there are neighbourhoods of
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
, such that
G_{0}
maps these neighbourhoods
to the first stratum of
M=G_{0}(N)
. For
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small,

G_{0}
maps

\mathcal{K}_{\eta,a-\varepsilon,a+\varepsilon}(q_{-})
and
\mathcal{K}_{\eta,b-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon}(q_{+})
(refer to Definition
II.7.2
for
the construction of
\mathcal{K}
)
to the first stratum. Shrink
\varepsilon
if necessary to ensure that
F_{0}
has no critical points
with critical values in
[a-\varepsilon,a+\varepsilon]\cup[b-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
other
than
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
.

Denote
\mathcal{K}_{-}:=\mathcal{K}_{\eta,a,b+\varepsilon}(q_{-})
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}:=\mathcal{K}_{\eta,a-\varepsilon,b}(q_{+})
. With this notation,
\mathcal{K}_{-}
is
the unstable manifold of
q_{-}
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}
is the stable manifold of
q_{+}
. The dimensions are
\dim\mathcal{K}_{-}=n-h_{-}
,
\dim\mathcal{K}_{+}=h_{+}
.
By the Morse–Smale condition on
\eta
, and since

h_{-}\geq h_{+}
, it follows that
\mathcal{K}_{-}
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}
are disjoint.
Set

(IV.3.1)

\mathcal{L}_{-}=G_{0}(\mathcal{K}_{-})\text{ and }\mathcal{L}_{+}=G_{0}(\mathcal{K}_{+}).

p_{2}
p_{1}
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{1})

Figure 21.
Lifting paths of rearrangements. Here
M
is a union of two circles in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Two critical points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}

are connected by a trajectory staying in the zeroth stratum. The point
p_{2}
cannot be moved below
p_{1}
.

Remark IV.3.3
.

If
k=1
and
\ind p_{+}=\ind p_{-}
, there can be a trajectory of
\xi
from
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
in the zeroth stratum, even
though there are no trajectories in the first. Such a zero-stratum trajectory is an obstruction to lifting rearrangements in codimension one. Hence our assumption that
k\geq 2
; compare Figure
21
.

IV.3.2.
Proof of Lemma
IV.3.1
under extra conditions

We first list extra conditions
which simplify the proof of Lemma
IV.3.1
.

(LR-1)

The points
p_{-}=G_{0}(q_{-})
and
p_{+}=G_{0}(q_{+})
are the only critical points of
F_{0}
at depth
0
or
1
in
F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
;

(LR-2)

Either
\mathcal{L}_{-}
or
\mathcal{L}_{+}
belong to the first stratum of
G_{0}(N)
, i.e. at least one of
\mathcal{L}_{-}
or
\mathcal{L}_{+}
misses the singular image of
G_{0}
.

Condition
(LR-2)
will be used to construct a lift, while Condition
(LR-1)
is used to guarantee Condition
(LR-2)
.

Lemma IV.3.4
.

Suppose
(F_{0},G_{0})
satisfies
(LR-2)
.
Then there exists a path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
with
G_{\tau}=G_{0}
for all
\tau\in[0,1]

that weakly lifts
f_{\tau}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}_{-}
belongs to the first stratum. As the first stratum is open, it follows that there exists an open set
V\subseteq N

containing
\mathcal{K}_{-}
such that
G_{0}(V)
belongs to the first stratum. Choose a grim vector field
\xi
on
\Omega
for
F_{0}
,
which is equal to
DG_{0}(\eta)
in a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{L}_{-}
in
M=G_{0}(N)
; compare Proposition
II.5.3
.
Assume that
\xi
is immersed Morse–Smale. Let
\widetilde{\eta}
be the pull-back of
\xi
.

There might be critical points of
F_{0}
in
F_{0}^{-1}[a,b]
other than
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. If that is the case, take a critical point
p\in F_{0}^{-1}[a,b]
distinct from
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. Assume it has
h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{-}+1
and it has the smallest value of
F_{0}
among critical points with this property. The Morse–Smale condition implies that there are no broken trajectories in
F_{0}^{-1}[a,F_{0}(p)]

connecting
p_{-}
with
p
, so we rearrange using
\xi
, applying Theorem
III.2.1
, so that the critical value of
p
is smaller than
a
. Using this procedure,
we move all critical points with
h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{-}+1
below the level set
a
. We denote by
(F_{\sigma},G_{\sigma})
,
\sigma\in[0,1]
,
the corresponding
\xi
-path,
noting that it is supported away from
\mathcal{L}_{-}
and away from all critical points of
F_{0}\circ G_{0}
. By construction,
G_{\sigma}=G_{0}
. On shrinking
V
if needed, we may and will assume that the path is supported away from
G_{0}(V)
.

We claim that for
F_{1}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\xi,a,b}(p_{-})
and
\mathcal{K}_{\xi,a,b}(p_{+})
are disjoint. Clearly,
By Corollary
II.6.6
, as
h_{p_{+}}\leq h_{p_{-}}
,
implies that there are no broken trajectories from
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
. But also, if there exists a critical point
p
in
F_{1}^{-1}[a,b]

and a trajectory from
p
to
p_{+}
, then
h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{p_{+}}+1
, also by Corollary
II.6.6
. But
h_{p_{+}}+1\leq h_{p_{-}}+1

and all critical points with
h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{p_{-}}+1
have been moved away from
F_{1}^{-1}[a,b]
. Thus, no critical point is connected
with
p_{+}
, so indeed,
\mathcal{K}_{\xi,a,b}(p_{-})\cap\mathcal{K}_{\xi,a,b}(p_{+})=\emptyset
.

We use the Rearrangement Theorem
III.2.1
to find
an elementary
\xi
-path
F_{1+\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
of Morse functions that lifts
p_{-}
above
p_{+}
. This path can be chosen to be
supported on an open set
U\subseteq\Omega
such that
U\cap M\subseteq G_{0}(V)
. In particular,
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}:=F_{1+\tau}\circ G_{1+\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,1]

can be supported on
V
.

The path
\sigma\mapsto g_{\sigma}:=F_{\sigma}\circ G_{\sigma}
,
\sigma\in[0,1]
is a path of excellent Morse functions.
We are in position to use Lemma
III.3.12
. As an outcome,
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
and
f_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
, are lax homotopic
over
h_{\sigma,0}
.
This is precisely the situation of Lemma
IV.1.6
with
\ell=2
. That is, we can promote
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})

to a weak lift of
f_{\tau}
.

If
\mathcal{L}_{+}
belongs to the first stratum, the proof is analogous. The vector field
\xi
is assumed to extend
DG_{0}(\eta)
from an open subset
of
M
containing
G_{0}(\mathcal{K}_{+})
and we construct a
\xi
-path that moves the critical point
p_{+}
below
p_{-}
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
IV.3.1
.
∎

Suppose
G_{0}
satisfies
(LR-2)
. The lift
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
we have constructed
has the following properties:

•

G_{\tau}
is a generic immersion for all
\tau
;

•

the image
G_{\tau}(N)
does not change; in fact, the only moment we change
G_{\tau}
is when we complete the path
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}

to a weak lift. Corollary
IV.1.4
constructs this completion in such a way that
G_{\tau}(N)
changes by reparametrisation of
N
;

•

the function
F_{\tau}
is immersed Morse for all
\tau
. The vector field
\xi
used in the construction of the rearrangement path
is grim for all
F_{\tau}
; compare Remark
IV.1.3
.

IV.3.3.
Enforcing condition
(LR-1)

We return to assuming the hypotheses of Lemma
IV.3.1
, and we arrange for
(LR-1)
to hold.

Lemma IV.3.5
.

There exists a path
F_{\tau}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
of immersed Morse functions such that:

•

F_{\tau}(z)=F_{0}(z)
if
F_{0}(z)<a-2\varepsilon
or
F_{0}(z)>b+2\varepsilon
;

•

F_{\tau}=F_{0}
in a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{L}_{-}
and
\mathcal{L}_{+}
;

•

the only critical points of
F_{1}
with critical values in
[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
are
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
, except if
k=2
and
h_{-}=h_{+}
, in which case there is also potentially a finite number of critical points at depth
d>1
, for which the sum of the index and the depth is equal to
h_{-}+1
;

•

Suppose that
N=N_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{\ell}
and the images
G_{0}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{0}(N_{\ell})
are pairwise disjoint.
If
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
belong to different connected components, then all the other critical points can be moved so that their critical values do not lie in
[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
.
If
p_{+}
and
p_{-}
belong to the same component of
G_{0}(N)
, then the only critical
points from the previous item that cannot in general be moved away from
[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]

are those that lie on the same component of
G_{0}(N)
as
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
;

•

The composition
F_{\tau}\circ G_{0}
is a path of excellent Morse functions on
N
.

Note that this lemma does indeed arrange for
(LR-1)
to hold. In the case
k=2
and
h_{-}=h_{+}
, the critical points that can occur with critical values in
[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
have depth at least 2, and so are not relevant to
(LR-1)
.

Proof of Lemma
IV.3.5
.

Note that
\mathcal{L}_{-}
and
\mathcal{L}_{+}
(defined in (
IV.3.1
)) are stratified manifolds of dimensions
n-h_{-}
and
h_{+}
, respectively. Assuming that
G_{0}
is generic,
\mathcal{L}_{-}\cap\mathcal{L}_{+}=\emptyset
.
Choose a grim vector field
\xi
for
F_{0}
which is Morse–Smale and also satisfies the following extra condition.

Condition IV.3.6
.

The membranes of all the critical points of
\xi
in
F_{0}^{-1}[a,b]
different from
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
are transverse to
\mathcal{L}_{-}
and
\mathcal{L}_{+}
.

This can be achieved in the same way as the Morse–Smale condition; see Subsection
II.6.2
.
Using this condition we prove the following result.

Lemma IV.3.7
.

Let
p\neq p_{-},p_{+}
be a critical point of
F_{0}
in
F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
of index
h_{p}
and depth
d_{p}>0
. If
h_{p}+d_{p}<h_{-}+1
, then the descending membrane of
p

is disjoint from
\mathcal{L}_{-}
. If
h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)>h_{+}
, then the ascending membrane is disjoint from
\mathcal{L}_{+}
.

Remark
.

If
d_{p}=0
, the membranes of
p
belong to the zeroth stratum, so they are automatically disjoint from
\mathcal{L}_{-}
and
\mathcal{L}_{+}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
II.6.2
,

\dim\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p)\cap\Omega[j]=h_{p}+d_{p}-j
and
\dim\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p)\cap\Omega[j]=n-k(d_{p}-1)-h_{p}+d_{p}-j
.
For each
j>0
, (
\mathcal{L}_{-}\cap\Omega[0]=\emptyset
by construction), we have

\dim\mathcal{L}_{-}\cap\Omega[j]=n-h_{-}-k(j-1)
. Then

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p)\cap\mathcal{L}_{-}\cap\Omega[j]
is empty if:

(IV.3.2)

h_{p}+d_{p}-j+n-h_{-}-k(j-1)<n-k(j-1)=\dim\Omega[j].

Transforming this formula yields

h_{p}+d_{p}<h_{-}+j.

This holds for all
j>0
, since we assume that

h_{p}+d_{p}<h_{-}+1
.
Thus
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p)\cap\mathcal{L}_{-}\cap\Omega[j]=\emptyset
for all
j\geq 0
.
Hence, since
M=\cup_{j\geq 0}\Omega[j]
, we have that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p)\cap\mathcal{L}_{-}=\emptyset
, as required.

The argument for the
\mathcal{L}_{+}
is analogous. Again,
\mathcal{L}_{+}\cap\Omega[0]=\emptyset
. As
\dim\mathcal{L}_{+}\cap\Omega[j]=h_{+}-k(j-1)
,

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p)\cap\mathcal{L}_{+}\cap\Omega[j]=\emptyset
if

(IV.3.3)

n-k(d_{p}-1)-h_{p}+d_{p}-j+h_{+}-k(j-1)<n-k(j-1).

This translates into
h_{+}<h_{p}+k(d_{p}-1)-(d_{p}-j)
. This holds for all
j>0

if
h_{+}<h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)
. Thus under this condition
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p)\cap\mathcal{L}_{+}\cap\Omega[j]=\emptyset
for all
j\geq 0
, and hence
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p)\cap\mathcal{L}_{+}=\emptyset
.
∎

Remark IV.3.8
.

The intersection of
\mathcal{L}_{-}
with the descending membrane
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p)
need not consist of trajectories of
\xi
, so it can be nonempty even if the expected
dimension is zero. Therefore, we get strict inequalities in Lemma
IV.3.7
.

Continuing the proof of Lemma
IV.3.5
, we introduce a useful piece of terminology.

Definition IV.3.9
.

A
\xi
-path of rearrangement
F_{\tau}
is
safe
if the support of
F_{\tau}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{L}_{-}\cup\mathcal{L}_{+}
.

Continuing the proof of Lemma
IV.3.5
, we state and prove the following result.

Lemma IV.3.10
.

There exists an
F_{\tau}
path of rearrangements that is a concatenation of safe
\xi
-path of rearrangements, supported on
F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
such that for any two pairs of critical points of
F_{\tau}
,
p,p^{\prime}
with
p,p^{\prime}\in F_{0}^{-1}[a+\varepsilon,b-\varepsilon]
,
we have
h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}
whenever
F_{1}(p)<F_{1}(p^{\prime})
.

Furthermore, the only critical points between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
necessarily have
h_{-}+d_{-}\leq h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{+}+d_{+}
.

Remark
.

If
h_{-}+d_{-}>h_{+}+d_{+}
, then the statement of the lemma means that after the rearrangements are made,
there are no critical points between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}

Proof.

We present an algorithm for performing the necessary rearrangements. We take two consecutive critical points
p,p^{\prime}
, that is points such that
F_{0}(p)<F_{0}(p^{\prime})
and such that there are no critical points between
p
and
p^{\prime}
.
We claim that at least one of the situations occur.

(SR-1)

h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}
;

(SR-2)

h_{p}+d_{p}>h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}
and at least one of
d_{p},d_{p^{\prime}}
is zero;

(SR-3)

h_{p}+d_{p}>h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}
and
h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}<h_{-}+1
;

(SR-4)

h_{p}+d_{p}>h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}
and
h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)>h_{+}
.

The claim is proved by contradiction. If none of the four cases occurs, we have (i)
h_{p}+d_{p}>h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}
, (ii)
h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}\geq h_{-}+1
, and (iii)
h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)\leq h_{+}
. We also have that (iv)
h_{-}\geq h_{+}
, by the assumptions of Lemma
IV.3.1
, and
d_{p}>0
,
d_{p^{\prime}}>0
by
(SR-2)
. Combining these yields:

h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{p}+(d_{p}-1)+1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(k\geq 2)}}{{\leq}}h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)+1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}h_{+}+1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{\leq}}h_{-}+1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{<}}h_{p}+d_{p}.

The first inequality used that
d_{p}\geq 1
.
Since this is absurd, we deduce that the above list is indeed exhaustive, as asserted.

We describe now a local algorithm deciding on a case-by-case basis which type of rearrangement should be performed for two consecutive points.

In case
(SR-1)
, there is no need to rearrange.
In case
(SR-2)
, the membranes of the critical point at depth zero are disjoint from
\mathcal{L}_{-}
and
\mathcal{L}_{+}
, so we can rearrange by moving
p
above
p^{\prime}
(if
d_{p}=0
), or
p^{\prime}
below
p
(if
d_{p^{\prime}}=0
). If
d_{p}=d_{p^{\prime}}=0
, we can perform either of the two moves. Note that in this move, we rearrange only a depth zero critical point, so that if
p
or
p^{\prime}
happen to be either of
p_{-}
or
p_{+}
, then they are not moved.

In case
(SR-3)
, the dimension counting argument of Lemma
IV.3.7
ensures that the descending membrane of
p^{\prime}
is disjoint from the set
\mathcal{L}_{-}
. Therefore, we can perform safe rearrangements moving
p^{\prime}
below
p
, unless
p^{\prime}=p_{-}
or
p^{\prime}=p_{+}
.

If
p^{\prime}=p_{-}
, then there is a critical point below
p_{-}
, with
h_{p}+d_{p}>h_{p_{-}}+d_{p_{-}}
. Originally, the function
F_{0}
has no critical points in
F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,a]
. If during the procedure we are now describing, some critical point lands below
p
, then it must have

h_{p}+d_{p}<h_{p_{-}}+d_{p_{-}}
. Therefore, the only situation where the move is forbidden is if
p^{\prime}=p_{+}
is the top critical point.

Similarly, in case
(SR-4)
, the ascending membrane of
p
is disjoint from
\mathcal{L}_{+}
, so we can safely move
p
above
p^{\prime}
. The discussion
of special cases is analogous: we cannot perform the move of case
(SR-4)
only if
p=p_{-}
.

Having specified the local algorithm, we apply it first for all the critical points between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
.
In this way, using a bubble sort, we arrange critical points in ascending order of
h_{p}+d_{p}
. Next, we move to the case when
p=p_{-}
. If the critical point
p^{\prime}
directly above
p_{-}
has
h_{p}+d_{p}>h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}
, then, as
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1
, we land in case
(SR-3)
. Therefore, we can move
p^{\prime}
below
p_{-}
. Inductively,
we move all critical points below
p_{-}
, for which
h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}<h_{-}+1
.

Next, suppose that
p^{\prime}=p_{+}
and
p
is immediately below
p_{+}
with
h_{p}+d_{p}>h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}
, and
p\neq p_{-}
. If
d_{p}=0
, we fit into case
(SR-2)
, we lift
p
above
p^{\prime}
. If
d_{p}\neq 0
, then, as
k\geq 2
,
h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)\geq h_{p}+d_{p}-1
, so we are in case
(SR-4)
. We can lift the critical point
p
above
p_{+}
.

Therefore, the only critical points that can remain between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
are those with
h_{p}+d_{p}\geq h_{-}+d_{-}
(because all others can
be safely pushed below
p_{-}
or above
p_{+}
) and, simultaneously,
h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{+}+d_{+}
.
∎

We study the critical points that remain between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
in greater detail. These are precisely the points at which the local algorithm
fails to move away from the region between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. The only situation when this can happen
is if
h_{-}+d_{-}=h_{+}+d_{+}
.

Corollary IV.3.11
.

Suppose
h_{-}+d_{-}=h_{+}+d_{+}
. Then all the critical points
p
with
d_{p}=0
can be moved above the critical point
p_{+}
. Moreover, if
k\geq 3
,
all the critical points can be moved.

Proof.

We refine the part of the proof of Lemma
IV.3.10
. First take two critical points
p
and
p^{\prime}
between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}

such that
p^{\prime}
is right above
p
and
p^{\prime}
is allowed to be
p_{+}
. Necessarily,
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{p^{\prime}}+d_{p^{\prime}}
(now all critical points between
p
and
p^{\prime}
satisfy this). If
d_{p}=0
and
d_{p^{\prime}}>0
, then we safely move
p
above
p^{\prime}
as in case
(SR-2)
of the proof of Lemma
IV.3.10
. Iterating this process, we safely rearrange all critical points with
d_{p}=0
by moving them above
p_{+}
. This completes the first part of the corollary.

Next, suppose
k\geq 3
and that we have a critical point
p
right below
p_{+}
. Suppose it is not
p_{-}
.
By the assumptions,
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{+}+d_{+}
. As all critical points in the zeroth stratum have been moved above
p_{+}
, we necessarily have
d_{p}>0
.
Since
p\neq p_{-}
, we have
d_{p}\neq 1
too, so
d_{p}\geq 2
. Then

h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)=h_{p}+(k-2)(d_{p}-1)+d_{p}-1>h_{p}+d_{p}-1=h_{+}+d_{+}-1>h_{+}.

Thus, we are in case
(SR-4)
, so we can safely lift
p
above
p_{+}
.
∎

Corollary
IV.3.11
takes care of the first three items of Lemma
IV.3.5
.

We now refine the above procedure to prove the fourth item of Lemma
IV.3.5
. Suppose
G_{0}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{0}(N_{\ell})
are disjoint, and
p_{-}:=G_{0}(q_{-})

belongs to
G_{0}(N_{1})
. This is relevant only if
h_{-}=h_{+}
, because this condition is part of the statement of the fourth item. Suppose
p
is a critical point in
F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
at depth
d_{p}\geq 2
and with

h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1
, and suppose there are no critical points with critical values in
(F(p_{-}),F(p))
.
Assume that
p
belongs, say, to
G_{0}(N_{2})
. By the dimension condition, there are no trajectories between
p_{-}
and
p

that belong to the zeroth stratum. On the other hand,
\mathcal{L}_{-}
belongs to a different component of
G_{0}(N)
than the part of
the membrane of
p
belonging to the first and deeper strata (here we use the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{-}\subseteq G_{0}(N_{1})
and
p\in G_{0}(N_{2})
).
Hence, the descending membrane of
p
is disjoint from
\mathcal{L}_{-}
, so we can safely move
p
below
p_{-}
. This proves the fourth item.

Concatenating all the
\xi
-paths of rearrangement used in these two procedures (first organizing all the critical points between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
and then second moving most of their critical values out of the region between the values of
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
) creates a
\xi
-path of
rearrangement
F_{\tau}
with support disjoint from
\mathcal{L}_{-}\cup\mathcal{L}_{+}
, i.e. a safe path. The only critical points of
F_{1}
in
F_{1}^{-1}(a,b)
are those with
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1=h_{+}+1
and
d_{p}>0
.
If
\varepsilon>0
is small,
F_{1}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]

also contains only these points.
We have not moved any of the critical points at depth 1, and so
F_{\tau}\circ G_{0}
is a path of excellent Morse functions, as required by the last item of the lemma.
This concludes the proof of Lemma
IV.3.5
.
∎

The path constructed in Lemma
IV.3.5
does not change
G_{0}
, so in particular no new double points of
M=G_{0}(N)
are
created. Moreover,
F_{\tau}
is a path of immersed Morse functions, without births or deaths on the deeper strata.

We have done enough to obtain
(IR-1)
of Addendum
IV.3.2
.
If
G_{0}
is an embedding then
(LR-2)
is automatically satisfied, and so no further operations are necessary. The procedure described in Subsection
IV.3.8
will give rise to a regular double path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
with
G_{\tau}
an embedding for all
\tau
.

IV.3.4.
Enforcing condition
(LR-2)
: the regular homotopy

The next result, ensuring that it is possible to change the map
G_{0}
by a path of immersions
in such a way that
G_{1}
maps
\mathcal{K}_{-}
to the first stratum, is the most difficult step
in the proof of Lifting Rearrangement Lemma
IV.3.1
. The precise construction of the isotopy
G_{\tau}
stretches across Subsections
IV.3.4
,
IV.3.5
,
IV.3.6
, and
IV.3.7
. The main part of the proof occurs in this subsection. In Subsection
IV.3.5
we show that we can arrange for
G_{\tau}
to be an immersion for all
\tau
. In Subsection
IV.3.6
we show how to modify the construction to arrange for
(IR-2)
to hold. In Subsection
IV.3.7
we address
(IR-3)
. Then in Subsection
IV.3.8
we summarise the entire proof.

Proposition IV.3.12
.

Suppose
(F_{0},G_{0})
satisfies condition
(LR-1)
, and
\eta
is gradient-like for
F_{0}\circ G_{0}
.
There exists a generic path of immersions
G_{\tau}\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
,

\tau\in[0,1]
, such that
G_{\tau}
is a regular
F_{0}
-path
(
see Definition
III.1.24
)
,

F_{0}\circ G_{\tau}
is an
\eta
-path
(
Definition
III.3.9
)
of
excellent Morse functions, and
G_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{-})\cap F_{0}^{-1}([a,b])
is contained in the first stratum of
G_{1}(N)
.

Proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
modulo Subsection
IV.3.5
.

We will prove the lemma modulo a key technical lemma, that will be proven in the following subsection.
Define

(IV.3.4)

\Omega^{\prime}=F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]\text{ and }N^{\prime}=G_{0}^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime})=f_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon].

The idea is to push the set
G_{0}(\mathcal{K}_{-})\cap\Omega[2]
above the level set of
F_{0}^{-1}(b)
,
so that
G_{0}(\mathcal{K}_{-})
does not intersect
\Omega[2]
below
F_{0}^{-1}(b)
. Hence also
G_{0}(\mathcal{K}_{-})
will not intersect
\Omega[d]
for
d\geq 3
, because
\Omega[d]\subseteq\overline{\Omega[2]}
.
The push will be guided by a vector field which is
not necessarily tangent to
G_{0}(N)
.
Choose a vector field on
\Omega^{\prime}
, which is a gradient vector field for
F_{0}
as
an ordinary Morse function
. This means that
the vector field need not be tangent to
M
. To emphasise the difference between grim vector fields and this vector field,
we denote the latter by
\nabla F_{0}
. Note that
F_{0}
, regarded as an ordinary Morse function,
has no critical points on
\Omega^{\prime}
(by Condition
(LR-1)
), therefore
\nabla F_{0}
does not vanish in
\Omega^{\prime}
.

We will assume that
\nabla F_{0}
is generic. The precise meaning of the genericity
condition will be specified in few places, especially in Subsection
IV.3.6
.
The next result, Lemma
IV.3.13
, gives a suitable framework for genericity: instead of perturbing
the Riemannian metric defining
\nabla F_{0}
, we can perturb the induced projection. This allows us to use transversality
arguments in a more flexible way.

A gradient-like vector field
\nabla F_{0}
induces a projection from
\Omega^{\prime}
onto
Y:=F_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
:
a point
z\in\Omega^{\prime}
is mapped to the unique point
y\in Y
lying on the same trajectory of
\nabla F_{0}
.
We denote this projection by
\Pi\colon\Omega^{\prime}\to Y
. For future use we give the following standard result.

Lemma IV.3.13
.

Suppose
\Pi^{\prime}\colon\Omega^{\prime}\to Y
is another projection, and
\Pi^{\prime}
is sufficiently close
(
in the
C^{1}
-norm
)
to
\Pi
.
Then there is a gradient-like vector field for
F_{0}
inducing
\Pi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For any
c\in[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
the restriction
\Pi|_{F_{0}^{-1}(c)}
is a local diffeomorphism, because
\nabla F_{0}
is transverse to the level sets of
F_{0}
. This property is open, hence
if
\Pi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime},Y)
is sufficiently close to
\Pi
, then
\Pi^{\prime}
restricted to
F_{0}^{-1}(c)
, for
c\in[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
, is a local diffeomorphism as well.

Choose a metric on
\Omega^{\prime}
. For each point
z\in\Omega^{\prime}
, let
\xi(z)\in\ker\Pi^{\prime}
be the unit vector such that
\partial_{\xi(z)}F_{0}(z)>0
. (We know that
\partial_{\xi(z)}F_{0}(z)\neq 0
because
\xi(z)
is transverse to the level set
F_{0}^{-1}(F_{0}(z))
.) We choose
the orientation of
\xi(z)
in such a way that
\partial_{\xi(z)}F_{0}(z)>0
. By construction,
\xi(z)
is a gradient-like vector field
for
F_{0}
. Moreover, since
\xi(z)\in\ker D\Pi^{\prime}(z)
for all
z
, the trajectories of
\xi(z)
are contained in the fibres of
\Pi^{\prime}
.
∎

We use this lemma to specify the first of several genericity conditions imposed on
\nabla F_{0}
.

Lemma IV.3.14
.

The set of maps
\Pi
such that for each sign
\pm
,
(\Pi\circ G_{0})^{-1}(\Pi\circ G_{0}(q_{\pm}))=\{q_{\pm}\}
,
is open dense in the set of all smooth maps from
\Omega^{\prime}
to
Y
.

Proof.

The double point set of
\Pi\circ G_{0}
is of codimension
k-1
, hence it may be avoided generically by the zero-dimensional manifold
\{q_{-},q_{+}\}
.
It follows from transversality that the given set of maps is open-dense.
∎

It follows that there exists a nonempty open neighbourhood
U\subseteq N
of
q_{-}

with the property that a trajectory of
\nabla F_{0}
through any point in
G_{0}(u)
for
u\in U
, does not hit
G_{0}(N)
. Decrease
\varepsilon
, if necessary to ensure that

(IV.3.5)

\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f^{-1}[a,a+2\varepsilon]\subseteq U.

Define
Z\subseteq f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
to be that subset of
z\in f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
that satisfy Condition
IV.3.15
below.

Condition IV.3.15
.

Whenever
w\in N
lies on a trajectory of
\eta
through
z
and
a+\varepsilon\leq f(w)<a+2\varepsilon
, then the trajectory of
\nabla F_{0}
through
G_{0}(w)
only intersects
G_{0}(N)
in
\Omega^{\prime}
at
G_{0}(w)
.

We refer to the schematic of
Z
in Figure
22
.
The conditions defining
Z
are open, hence
Z
is an open subset of
f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
. Note that (
IV.3.5
) implies also
that
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f^{-1}[a+\varepsilon]\subseteq Z
. In particular
Z
is nonempty.

As
\mathcal{K}_{-}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{K}_{+}
, there exists an open subset
Z_{0}\subseteq Z
(‘open’ meaning open in
f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
)
containing
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
and disjoint from
\mathcal{K}_{+}
.

q_{-}
f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
f^{-1}(a+2\varepsilon)
f^{-1}(b)
\gamma_{z}
w
z
F_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
F_{0}^{-1}(a+2\varepsilon)
F_{0}^{-1}(b)

\nabla F_{0}
p_{-}

second stratum

Figure 22.
The point
z
does not belong to
Z
. In fact, the trajectory
\gamma_{z}
of
\eta
, in
N
passes through
w
(left picture), and the trajectory of
\nabla F_{0}
through
G_{0}(w)
, in
G_{0}(N)\subseteq\Omega
, reaches a point on
G_{0}(N)
(right picture).

Define
R\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
to be a smooth increasing function such that
R(x)=x
for
x\notin[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
and such that

R
maps:

•

[a+\varepsilon,a+2\varepsilon]
onto
[a+\varepsilon,b]
;

•

[a+2\varepsilon,b]
onto
[b,b+\varepsilon/2]
;

•

[b,b+\varepsilon]
onto
[b+\varepsilon/2,b+\varepsilon]
.

The function
R
will be our
rescaling function
, denoting the height of the lift. Note that, by construction

(IV.3.6)

R(x)\geq x\textrm{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

We define a new function, which is called the
lift function

P\colon\Omega^{\prime}\times[0,1]\to\Omega^{\prime}
.
For
\theta\in[0,1]
, and
z\in\Omega^{\prime}
, we define
P(z,\theta)
to be the unique point in
\Omega^{\prime}
belonging to the
same trajectory of
\nabla F_{0}
as
z
, such that

(IV.3.7)

F_{0}(P(z,\theta))=F_{0}(z)+\theta(R(F_{0}(z))-F_{0}(z)).

Note that the function
P\colon\Omega^{\prime}\times[0,1]\to\Omega^{\prime}
is a smooth function, because of the smooth dependence of solutions of an ODE
on initial conditions.

We aim to define
G_{\tau}
in such a way that a point
u\in\mathcal{K}_{-}
is mapped to
P(G_{0}(u),\tau)
, i.e. it is moved up
from the level set
f(u)
to the level set of
(1-\tau)f(u)+\tau R(f(u))
.
For the map to be smooth, we need to also lift points near
\mathcal{K}_{-}
. To this end, we need
to define a cut-off function

\mu\colon f^{-1}[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]\to[0,1].

In the construction, we will adjust the function
\mu
in such a way that

(i)

\eta
is a gradient-like vector field for
F_{0}\circ G_{\tau}
for all
\tau\in[0,1]
; and

(ii)

G_{\tau}
is an immersion for all
\tau\in[0,1]
.

We address the first property next. The second property will be recorded below as Lemma
IV.3.19
, the proof of which we will defer to Subsection
IV.3.5
.

To prove that
\eta
is gradient-like for
F_{0}\circ G_{\tau}
, we will need the following properties
of
\mu
:

(
\mu
-1)

\partial_{\eta}\mu\geq 0
, and equality holds only at critical points of
f
and at places where the value of
\mu
lies in
\{0,1\}
;

(
\mu
-2)

the support of
\mu
intersected with
f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
is contained in
Z_{0}
;

(
\mu
-3)

\mu
is equal to
1
on
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f^{-1}[a+2\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
and vanishes on
\mathcal{K}_{+}
.

The next lemma shows that such a function exists.

Lemma IV.3.16
.

There exists a smooth function
\mu\colon f^{-1}[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]\to[0,1]
satisfying
(

μ

-1)
,
(

μ

-2)
, and
(

μ

-3)
.

Proof.

Take a smooth function
\mu_{0}\colon f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)\to[0,1]
supported on
Z_{0}

and equal to
1
on
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)\subseteq Z_{0}
. Define
\Omega_{0}^{\prime}:=F_{0}^{-1}[a+2\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
and
N_{0}^{\prime}:=G_{0}^{-1}(\Omega_{0}^{\prime})
.

Let
\nu
be a smooth non-decreasing function from
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
to
[0,1]

equal to
0
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
close to
0
and equal to
1
for
t>t_{0}
,
where
t_{0}
is the minimal time needed to reach
f_{0}^{-1}(a+2\varepsilon)

by going from
Z_{0}
via
\eta
.

Then define
\mu\colon N^{\prime}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
via
\mu(z)=\nu(t)\mu_{0}(z_{0})

for any
z\in N^{\prime}_{0}
if the trajectory of
\eta
through
z

hits the level set
a+\varepsilon
at the point
z_{0}
and the time to go from
z_{0}
to
z
is equal to
t
.
With this construction,
\mu
has all the required properties.
∎

We will impose one more condition on
\mu
in Subsection
IV.3.5
, and show that
\mu
can be assumed to also satisfy this.

Now we define a path of maps
G_{\tau}\colon N\to\Omega
. The definition is as follows. Take
u\in N
. Set
G_{\tau}(u)=G_{0}(u)
if
f_{0}(u)\notin N^{\prime}_{0}
.
If
f_{0}(u)\in N^{\prime}_{0}
, we set

(IV.3.8)

G_{\tau}(u)=P(G_{0}(u),\tau\mu(u)).

Compare Figure
23
.

F_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
F_{0}^{-1}(a+2\varepsilon)
F_{0}^{-1}(b)
Z

Figure 23.
A schematic of
G_{\tau}
. Points on a trajectory through
Z
are lifted up by flowing along
\nabla F_{0}
.

We list the properties of
G_{\tau}
.

•

G_{\tau}(u)
depends smoothly on
\tau
and
u
, in particular
G_{\tau}
is a homotopy between the maps
G_{0}
and
G_{1}
;

•

G_{\tau}(u)=G_{0}(u)
on
\mathcal{K}_{+}
by
(

μ

-3)
. Moreover, if a trajectory of
\eta
through
u
misses
Z
, then
G_{\tau}(u)=G_{0}(u)
;

•

G_{\tau}=G_{0}
above the level set
f_{0}^{-1}(b+\varepsilon)
and
below the level set
f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
.

Set

\widetilde{f}_{\tau}=F_{0}\circ G_{\tau}\colon N\to\mathbb{R}.

Lemma IV.3.17
.

The family
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
is an
\eta
-path of excellent Morse functions.

Proof.

It is clear from the construction that
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
does not depend on
\tau
for any of the critical points of
f_{0}
. In fact, the only
critical point of
f_{0}
in
N^{\prime}_{0}
is
q_{+}
, and we have that
\mu(q_{+})=0
.

We prove that
\partial_{\eta}f_{\tau}\geq 0
with equality only at critical points of
f_{\tau}
.
Given a point
u\in N
, (
IV.3.7
) implies that
P(G_{0}(u),\tau\mu(u))
is at the level set

F_{0}(G_{0}(u))+\tau\mu(u)(R(F_{0}(G_{0}(u)))-F_{0}(G_{0}(u)))
.
Noting that
F_{0}(G_{0}(u))=f_{0}(u)
and
f_{\tau}(u)=F_{0}(G_{\tau}(u))
we see that
(
IV.3.7
) and (
IV.3.8
) imply:

(IV.3.9)

f_{\tau}(u)=f_{0}(u)+\tau\mu(u)(R(f_{0}(u))-f_{0}(u)).

Applying
\partial_{\eta}
to both sides of (
IV.3.9
) we obtain.

(IV.3.10)

\begin{split}\partial_{\eta}f_{\tau}(u)=&\partial_{\eta}f_{0}(u)+\\
&+\tau(R(f_{0}(u))-f_{0}(u))\partial_{\eta}\mu+(R^{\prime}(f_{0}(u))\partial_{\eta}f_{0}(u)-\partial_{\eta}f_{0}(u))\tau\mu=\\
=&\underbrace{(1-\tau\mu)\partial_{\eta}f_{0}(u)}_{(1)}+\underbrace{\tau(R(f_{0}(u))-f_{0}(u))\partial_{\eta}\mu}_{(2)}+\underbrace{\tau\mu R^{\prime}(f_{0}(u))\partial_{\eta}f_{0}(u)}_{(3)}.\end{split}

We study the terms (1), (2), and (3).

(1)

As
\eta
is a gradient-like vector field and
\mu(u)\in[0,1]
, term (1) is non-negative. It is zero only if
\mu(u)=\tau=1

or
u
is a critical point of
f
.

(2)

By construction,
\partial_{\eta}\mu\geq 0
, with equality at the critical points, and also
R(f_{0}(u))-f_{0}(u)\geq 0
(compare (
IV.3.6
)), so (2) is non-negative.

(3)

The derivative
R^{\prime}
is positive. The term (3) is non-negative. It is positive unless
\tau\mu=0
or
u
is a
critical point of
f_{0}
.

From the list, since
\tau\mu
cannot be
0
and
1
simultaneously, it is clear that
\partial f_{\tau}(u)\geq 0
with equality exactly at the critical points of
f_{0}
.
∎

Continuing the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
, we have the
following result.

Lemma IV.3.18
.

G_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{-})\cap F_{0}^{-1}[a,b]
is disjoint from the set of the double points of
G_{1}(N)
.

Proof.

Take
w\in\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f_{0}^{-1}[a,b]
. Consider the following three cases.

•

If
f_{0}(w)\leq a+\varepsilon
, then
G_{1}(w)=G_{0}(w)
belongs to the first stratum, because
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f_{0}^{-1}[a,a+\varepsilon]\subseteq U
.

•

Suppose
f_{0}(w)\in[a+\varepsilon,a+2\varepsilon]
. Let
u\in f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
belong to the same trajectory of
\eta

as
w
. Then
u\in Z
and by Condition
IV.3.15
,
G_{1}(w)
belongs to the first stratum.

•

If
f_{0}(w)>a+2\varepsilon
, then
\mu(w)=1
, so by (
IV.3.9
) we have
f_{\tau}(w)=(1-\tau)f_{0}(w)+R(f_{0}(w))>b
.
So
G_{1}(w)\notin F_{0}^{-1}[a,b]
. ∎

We will need one more result, whose proof is deferred to later subsections.

Lemma IV.3.19
.

There exists a choice of
\mu
such that
G_{\tau}
is an immersion for all
\tau\in[0,1]
.

We will prove Lemma
IV.3.19
in Subsection
IV.3.5
. Modulo this, Lemmas
IV.3.17
,
IV.3.18
, and
IV.3.19
conclude the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
.
∎

In the next subsection, we will check that
G_{\tau}
is an immersion for all
\tau
, proving Lemma
IV.3.19
. This done, we
will perturb
G_{\tau}
in such a way that
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
becomes a regular double path (see Definition
III.1.27
).
The construction will be further tweaked in Subsections
IV.3.6
and
IV.3.7
, in order to arrange for
(IR-2)
and
(IR-3)
to hold.
A summary of the entire proof of Lemma
IV.3.1
will be given in Subsection
IV.3.8
.

IV.3.5.
Enforcing condition
(LR-2)
: proving that
G_{\tau}
is an immersion

We will now prove that if
\nabla F_{0}
is sufficiently generic, and for a careful choice of
\mu
, then
G_{\tau}
is an immersion for all
\tau
.

Recall that
\Omega^{\prime}=F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
. Set
Y=F_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
. As explained in Subsection
IV.3.4
, the
vector field
\nabla F_{0}
induces a projection
\Pi\colon\Omega^{\prime}\to Y
.
We begin with the following observation.

Lemma IV.3.20
.

The map
\Pi\circ G_{\tau}\colon N\to Y
does not depend on
\tau
.

Proof.

For any point
u\in N
, by the construction of
G_{\tau}
, the points
G_{\tau}(u)
,
\tau\in[0,1]
belong to the same trajectory of
\nabla F_{0}
. Therefore, they are mapped by

\Pi
to the same point.
∎

Corollary IV.3.21
.

Let
u\in N
and let
z=G_{0}(u)
. Suppose that
\nabla F_{0}
is not
tangent at
z
to the branch of
G_{0}(N)
containing
G_{0}(u)
. Then
G_{\tau}
is an immersion near
u
for all
\tau
.

Proof.

By the chain rule,
D(\Pi\circ G_{0})=D\Pi(G_{0}(u))\circ DG_{0}(u)
.
As
G_{0}
is an immersion,
DG_{0}(u)
has trivial kernel.
The differential
D\Pi(z)
has kernel spanned by
\nabla F_{0}(z)
. By assumption, it follows that the kernel of
D\Pi(z)
intersects the image
of
DG_{0}(u)
only at
0
. Hence
D(\Pi\circ G_{0})(u)
has trivial kernel. By Lemma
IV.3.20
,
D(\Pi\circ G_{\tau})(u)

has trivial kernel for all
\tau
. Since
D(\Pi\circ G_{\tau})=D\Pi(G_{\tau}(u))\circ DG_{\tau}(u)
, it follows that
DG_{\tau}(u)
has trivial kernel for all
\tau
, and hence by continuity of the derivative,
G_{\tau}
is an immersion near
u
.
∎

Thus we only need to study points at which
\nabla F_{0}
is tangent to
G_{0}(N)
at
z
. We show that generically, the set of such points
z\in G_{0}(N)
is itself a manifold.
In proving genericity, it is most convenient to perturb the immersion
G_{0}
.

Lemma IV.3.22
.

There is an open-dense subset of maps
G_{0}
such that the set of points
u\in N
, with the property that
\nabla F_{0}
is tangent to the branch
of
M
with tangent vectors
DG_{0}(u)(T_{u}N)\subseteq T_{G_{0}(u)}\Omega
, forms a smooth manifold of dimension
n-k
, missing
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
.

Proof.

The condition
\nabla F_{0}(G_{0}(u))\subseteq\Iim DG_{0}(u)
defines a smooth submanifold in the 1-fold jet space
J^{1}(N,\Omega)

of maps from
N
to
\Omega
; compare Subsection
III.1.2
for the relevant definitions. The codimension is equal to
k
. This can be seen as follows:
DG_{0}(u)
is a codimension
k
subspace and
\nabla F_{0}(G_{0}(u))
is a 1-dimensional vector. The condition that a vector
fits into a codimension
k
space is of codimension
k
.
By the Thom transversality theorem (compare Theorem
II.3.4
for
s=1
) the set of maps
G_{0}
transverse to this stratum is open-dense.
If, possibly after a perturbation,
G_{0}
is transverse to this stratum, then the set of points in
N
such that the jet extension of
G_{0}
hits the stratum,
is a smooth submanifold of the same codimension, that is,
k
.
∎

Remark
.

We can understand Lemma
IV.3.22
in the broader context of the Thom-Boardman stratification of Subsection
II.3.2
.
The set of points
u
where
\nabla F_{0}(G_{0}(u))
is tangent to
G_{0}(N)
at
G_{0}(u)
actually corresponds to the Thom-Boardman stratum
\beta^{1}
of the map
\Pi\circ G_{0}
. In particular, the dimension of this set can be alternatively computed using e.g.
[
AGZV12
, Section I.2]
.

\Sigma
\Pi
G

Figure 24.
The set
\Sigma
and its stratification. The thickened points on
\Sigma
represent the second level of the Thom-Boardman stratification with respect to the map
\Pi\circ G
.

Perturb
G_{0}
so that it satisfies the genericity condition spelled out in Lemma
IV.3.22
. Let
\Sigma\subseteq N

be the set of points
u
such that the image of
DG_{0}(u)
contains
\nabla F_{0}(G_{0}(u))
; see Figure
24
. As critical points of
f_{0}
form a finite set, by further perturbing
G_{0}
, we may and shall assume that

\Sigma
misses all critical points of
f_{0}
.
For each point
u
in
\Sigma
, there is a unique
vector
\upsilon_{u}\in T_{u}N
such that
DG_{0}(u)(\upsilon_{u})=\nabla F_{0}
. Hence we obtain a section
\upsilon\colon\Sigma\to TN
of
TN|_{\Sigma}
.
We make a trivial observation for future use.

Lemma IV.3.23
.

If
\upsilon_{u}
is parallel to
\eta(u)
,
then
\upsilon_{u}
is not a negative multiple of
\eta(u)
.

Proof.

We have
f_{0}=F_{0}\circ G_{0}
,
\upsilon_{u}\in T_{u}N
, and
DG_{0}(\upsilon_{u})=\nabla F_{0}
.
Thus

(IV.3.11)

\partial_{\upsilon}f_{0}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}\partial_{DG_{0}(\upsilon)}F_{0}(G_{0}(u))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}\partial_{\nabla F_{0}}F_{0}(G_{0}(u))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{=}}\|(\nabla F_{0})(G_{0}(u))\|^{2}\geq 0.

Here, (1) is functoriality of the differential. The second equality (2) is the definition of
\upsilon_{u}
. The third equality (3) is the definition of the directional
derivative as the scalar product with the gradient.
Hence
\partial_{\upsilon}f_{0}\geq 0
.
Moreover,
\partial_{\eta}f_{0}\geq 0
, because the vector field
\eta
is gradient-like for
f_{0}
. Notice that we assumed that
\Sigma
does not pass through any of the critical points. Hence,
\partial_{\eta}f_{0}
is actually positive.
If
\upsilon_{u}
is a negative multiple of
\eta
, we must have
\partial_{\upsilon}f_{0}=\partial_{\eta}f_{0}=0
, which
can hold only if
u
is a critical point of
f_{0}
. But
u\in\Sigma
is not a critical point.
∎

The key property of
\upsilon_{u}
is that we can translate the condition on
G_{\tau}
being an immersion into a condition involving
\mu

and
\upsilon
.

Lemma IV.3.24
.

Suppose
u\in\Sigma
. Assume that the function
\mu
satisfies

(IV.3.12)

\partial_{\upsilon}\mu(u)\geq 0.

Then
G_{\tau}
is an immersion near
u
for all
\tau
.

Proof.

We know that
\dim\ker D(\Pi\circ G_{0})(u)=1
is spanned by
\upsilon_{u}
. Therefore,
the kernel of
DG_{\tau}(u)
can either be trivial, or it can be a one-dimensional subspace spanned by
\upsilon_{u}
.
To prove the lemma, we need to make sure
that
\upsilon_{u}
is not in the kernel of
DG_{0}(u)
. Since
f_{\tau}=F_{0}\circ G_{\tau}
, if
\upsilon_{u}\in\ker DG_{\tau}(u)
, then

\partial_{\upsilon}f_{\tau}(u)=0
. Hence, it is enough to show that (
IV.3.12
) implies

\partial_{\upsilon}f_{\tau}(u)>0
.

To this end, we use (
IV.3.9
). Differentiating both sides of (
IV.3.9
) with respect to
\upsilon
, we obtain
a formula analogous to (
IV.3.10
).

\partial_{\upsilon}f_{\tau}(u)=(1-\tau\mu)\partial_{\upsilon}f_{0}(u)+\tau(R(f_{0}(u))-f_{0}(u))\partial_{\upsilon}\mu+\tau\mu R^{\prime}(f_{0}(u))\partial_{\upsilon}f_{0}(u).

Note that
\partial_{\upsilon}f_{0}
is positive, since it is
\partial_{\nabla F_{0}}F_{0}
by (
IV.3.11
). An analysis akin to the one following (
IV.3.9
) leads to the statement that
\partial_{\upsilon}\mu(u)\geq 0
implies that
\partial_{\upsilon}f_{\tau}>0
.
∎

In light of Lemma
IV.3.24
(and keeping in mind Corollary
IV.3.21
), we proceed to the main technical result
of this subsection.

Proposition IV.3.25
.

There exists a smooth function
\mu\colon f_{0}^{-1}[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]\to[0,1]
satisfying
items
(

μ

-1)
,
(

μ

-2)
, and
(

μ

-3)
above and such that for any
u\in\Sigma
,
\partial_{\upsilon}\mu(u)\geq 0

with equality only at points where
\mu(u)=0,1
.

Proof.

One might expect this proof to start with a function
\mu

satisfying items
(

μ

-1)
,
(

μ

-2)
, and
(

μ

-3)
, and then improving it to satisfy the statement of Proposition

. However, there are technical problems near
\mu^{-1}(\{0,1\})
. To avoid them, we will proceed in a slightly indirect way.

Let
\kappa>0
be an integer: we will describe how it is determined at the end of the proof. Choose a family
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{\kappa}

of open subsets of
f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
with the properties that
\overline{S}_{1}\subseteq Z_{0}
,
\overline{S}_{i+1}\subseteq S_{i}
and
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)\subseteq S_{\kappa}
. For

, let

be the subset of

consisting of the points that are reached from

by a trajectory of
\eta
.
By construction

\overline{T}_{i+1}\subseteq T_{i}.

We note that here we assume
\eta
has no critical points other than

q_{+}
in
f^{-1}[a+\varepsilon,b+\frac{5}{4}\varepsilon]
, and not just in
f^{-1}[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
. But this does not pose any problems to arrange, e.g. by decreasing
\varepsilon
.

By Condition
IV.3.15
, no trajectory of
\eta
starting from
Z
hits
\Sigma
before reaching the level set
f^{-1}(a+2\varepsilon)
. This is because of Condition
IV.3.15
. We assume that the set
S_{1}
is chosen in such a way that no trajectory of
\eta

starting from
S_{1}
hits
\Sigma
before reaching the level set
f^{-1}(a+\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon)
.

We construct a function
\zeta\colon T_{1}\to[0,\frac{5}{4}]
with the following properties:

(
\zeta
-1)

\partial_{\eta}\zeta>0
on
T_{1}
;

(
\zeta
-2)

Suppose
u\in T_{1}
is connected to
S_{1}\setminus S_{\kappa}

by a trajectory of
\eta
. Then
\zeta(u)\in[0,\frac{1}{4}]
;

(
\zeta
-3)

\zeta
is greater or equal to
1
on
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f^{-1}[a+2\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
.

There is a correspondence between
(

μ

-1)
and
(

ζ

-1)
,
(

μ

-2)
and
(

ζ

-2)
, as well as
between
(

-3)
and
(

ζ

-3)
.
The precise connection will be made precise in due course, at the end of the proof; see (
IV.3.14
) for the final definition of

and the proof, using the conditions
(

-1)
,
(

ζ

-2)
, and
(

ζ

-3)
, that the conditions
(

-1)
,
(

μ

-2)
, and
(

μ

-3)
are satisfied for this definition (recall that we proved in Lemma
IV.3.16
that a function

satisfying these criteria exists, but we need a refined version to guarantee that
G_{\tau}
is an immersion for all

).

Lemma IV.3.26
.

There exists
\zeta
exists satisfying
(

ζ

-1)
,
(

ζ

-2)
, and
(

ζ

-3)
.

Sketch of proof.

As these conditions resemble strongly the conditions for
\mu
, the proof that
\zeta
exists satisfying these properties is a straightforward
adaptation of the proof of Lemma
IV.3.16
; we therefore leave the details to the reader.
∎

Our goal is to improve the function
\zeta
so that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0
for all
u\in\Sigma\cap T_{\kappa}
, and hence since the
T_{i}
are nested this proves that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0
for all points
u
in
\Sigma\cap T_{i}
, for all
i
.
We will need the following technical result.

Lemma IV.3.27
.

Suppose
X
is a closed submanifold of
\Sigma
. Suppose
U
is an open subset of
X

such that
neither
\upsilon
, nor
\eta
is tangent to
X
at any point of
\overline{U}
.
Then there exists a regular index triple
V\subseteq N

(
see Definition
II.7.5
)
such that
V\cap X=U
and
if a trajectory of
\eta
exits
\overline{V}
at
v_{+}
and then re-enters
\overline{V}
at
v_{-}
, then
\zeta(v_{-})>\zeta(v_{+})
.

Proof.

Choose a Riemannian metric on
\Omega
. Let
E
be the normal bundle for
X
in
N
.
Since neither
\upsilon
nor
\eta
is tangent to
\overline{U}
, we may and will assume that
\eta
and
\upsilon
are orthogonal to
T\overline{U}
.
As
\upsilon
is not parallel to
\eta
either,
we choose a metric in which
\eta
is orthogonal to
\upsilon
. In particular,
\eta
and
\upsilon

can be regarded as sections of the normal bundle to
\overline{U}
.
Let
L
be the rank 1 subbundle of
E|_{\overline{U}}
generated by
\eta
.
Let
L^{\perp}
be the orthogonal complement to
L
in
E|_{\overline{U}}
.
We have that
\upsilon_{u}\in L^{\perp}_{u}
, a fact which we will not use until
Lemma
IV.3.28
.
For
\rho>0
, we let
\widetilde{D}(\rho)
denote the disc
bundle of
L^{\perp}
consisting of closed discs of radius
\rho
.
By compactness of
\overline{U}
, we may and will choose
\rho
sufficiently small so that
\exp\colon\widetilde{D}(\rho)\to N
is a diffeomorphism onto its
image. Let
\overline{D}(\rho)\subseteq N
be the image of
\widetilde{D}(\rho)
under the
\exp
map. Clearly,
\overline{D}(\rho)\cap X=\overline{U}
. We define
D(\rho)
as the interior of
\overline{D}(\rho)
. Note that
\overline{D}(\rho)
is a codimension one submanifold of
N
.

We have chosen
\eta
to be orthogonal to
L^{\perp}
. This means that
\eta
is transverse to
\overline{D}(\rho)
at least at the points in
\overline{U}
. By openness of the transversality condition, we may and will decrease
\rho
in such a way that
\eta
is transverse to
\overline{D}(\rho)
at every point of
\overline{D}(\rho)
.

We now thicken
\overline{D}(\rho)
in the direction of
\eta
. Choose
\delta>0
. By compactness of
\overline{D}(\rho)
we may and will assume that
any trajectory of
\eta
connecting two distinct points of
\overline{U}

takes at least
3\delta
to travel.
Define
\overline{D}(\rho,\delta)
as the set of points on trajectories of
\eta
that can be reached from a point
u\in\overline{D}(\rho)
in time in
[-\delta,\delta]
. With that choice of
\delta
,
the flow of
\eta
induces a diffeomorphism
\overline{D}(\rho,\delta)\cong\overline{D}(\rho)\times[-\delta,\delta]
.

We declare
V
to be the interior of
\overline{D}(\rho,\delta)
.
The entry set
\partial_{\textrm{in}}V
is the set of points that reach
\overline{D}(\rho)
in time
\delta
. The exit set
\partial_{\textrm{out}}V
is the set of points that are reached from
\overline{D}(\rho)
in time
\delta
.

Note that if a trajectory of
\eta
exits
\partial_{\textrm{out}}V
and then hits
\partial_{\textrm{in}}V
, it has to travel for time at least
\delta
(otherwise there would be a trajectory connecting two points of
\overline{D}(\rho)
in time less than
3\delta
).
As
\zeta
increases along the trajectories,
if a trajectory leaves
\overline{V}
at
v_{+}
and then hits
\overline{V}
at
v_{-}
,
we have
\zeta(v_{-})>\zeta(v_{+})
.
∎

Remark
.

By compactness of
N^{\prime}
, the condition
\partial_{\eta}\zeta>0
can quickly lead to a universal constant
C_{\eta\zeta}
such that
\zeta(v_{-})>\zeta(v_{+})+C_{\eta\zeta}
.

The next lemma is the key step in the proof of Proposition
IV.3.25
.

Lemma IV.3.28
.

.

;

.

We continue proving Proposition
IV.3.25
.
First, consider the map
\Pi\circ G_{0}\colon T_{1}\to Y
(here
\Pi
is the projection along
\nabla F_{0}
). As
\dim\ker D\Pi=1
,

; see Subsection

. We denote

\Sigma_{m}

. This is a smooth manifold with boundary. There is an index
m_{0}
such that
\Sigma_{m_{0}}
is nonempty and all subsequent
\Sigma_{i}

(there is a universal bound of

in terms of the dimensions

and

By definition,

\Pi\circ G_{0}
restricted to any
\Sigma_{m}\setminus\Sigma_{m+1}

\upsilon_{u}

\Sigma_{m}\setminus\Sigma_{m+1}
. However,
\eta
might be tangent to
\Sigma_{m}\setminus\Sigma_{m+1}
.
Therefore, consider the second projection
\Pi_{\eta}\colon N^{\prime}\to f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
along the trajectories of
\eta
.
Let
\Sigma_{m,s}
stand for the
s
th Thom–Boardman stratum
\beta^{\mathbf{s}}\Pi_{\eta}|_{\Sigma_{m}\setminus\Sigma_{m+1}}
.
By Lemma

,

\Sigma_{m,s}

, there is

such that

is nonempty and

. We can now finally define

\kappa=1+\sum_{m}s_{m}.

We proceed to prove that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0
by induction on

. Consider
\Sigma_{m_{0},s_{m_{0}}}
. This is a closed submanifold
of

. Let

be the set of points for which

\eta
is parallel to

. By Lemma

,

,

is a positive

\upsilon_{u}
. In particular, there is a neighbourhood
