of
R

such that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta>0
everywhere on
\overline{U}_{R}
. We consider

\overline{U}:=\Sigma_{m_{0},s_{m_{0}}}\setminus\Int U_{R}
, letting
U
be the interior of
\overline{U}
. We let
U^{\prime}=U\cap T_{2}

and
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{4\kappa}
.

V
\overline{U}
W
\Sigma_{m,s+1}

Figure 27.
Proof of Proposition
IV.3.25
. Constructing
\overline{U}
.

Using Lemma
IV.3.28
we improve
\zeta
on
U
in such a way that

\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0
for all
u\in\overline{U}^{\prime}
. This means that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0

everywhere on
\Sigma_{m_{0},s_{m_{0}}}\cap\overline{T}_{2}
.

We proceed with the induction step, inducting on
i
in
T_{i}
. Suppose that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0

for
u\in\Sigma_{m,s+1}\cap\overline{T_{i}}
. We aim to improve
\zeta
so that this also holds on
\Sigma_{m,s}\cap\overline{T}_{i+1}
.
The procedure is analogous to the first step.
Let
R
be the subset of
\Sigma_{m,s}
such that
\eta
is parallel to
\upsilon
. For
u
in the compact set

(R\cup\Sigma_{m,s+1})\cap\overline{T_{i}}
, we already know that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0
. In fact, on
R\cap\overline{T_{i}}
this follows from Lemma
IV.3.23
,
while on
\Sigma_{m,s+1}\cap\overline{T_{i}}
this is the induction assumption. There exists an open subset
W\subseteq\Sigma_{m,s}

containing
R\cup\Sigma_{m,s+1}
such that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0
on the whole of
\overline{W}
.
We set
U=\Sigma_{m,s}\setminus W
and
U^{\prime}=U\cap T_{i+1}
; see Figure
27
.
We apply Lemma
IV.3.28
to obtain that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0
for
u\in\overline{U^{\prime}}
. This means
that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta(u)>0
on
\overline{W}\cup\overline{U^{\prime}}
which contains
\Sigma_{m,s}\cap\overline{T_{i+1}}
.
This completes the induction step.

The inductive argument proves the statement until we reach
\Sigma_{m,0}
, that is, the statement
holds for the whole of
\Sigma_{m}
. We declare
\Sigma_{m-1,s_{m}+1}
to be
\Sigma_{m}
,
and we proceed with induction.
The procedure continues until we conclude that
\partial_{\upsilon}\zeta>0
everywhere
on
\Sigma\cap T_{\kappa}
.

The final function
\zeta
has been constructed. Now, as the final step in the proof, we construct the function
\mu
using the function
\zeta
. We then show that
\mu
satisfies the required conditions.
Recall from Lemma
IV.3.16
that constructing a function satisfying
(

μ

-1)
,
(

μ

-2)
, and
(

μ

-3)
was relatively straightforward. The key here is that we also need
\partial_{\upsilon}\mu(u)\geq 0
.

To this end, choose a smooth function
\chi\colon[0,\frac{5}{4}]\to[0,1]
with the following properties.

(CH-1)

\chi
maps
[0,\frac{1}{2}]
to
0
;

(CH-2)

\chi
maps
[\frac{3}{4},\frac{5}{4}]
to
1
;

(CH-3)

\chi^{\prime}(x)>0
for
x\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4})
.

We set

(IV.3.14)

\mu(u)=\chi(\zeta(u)).

To prove
(

μ

-1)
we use the property of
(

ζ

-1)
together with
(CH-3)
. The chain rule yields that

\partial_{\eta}\mu>0
except at the locus where
\chi=\{0,1\}
. At these latter points,
\partial_{\eta}\mu=0
.

The same argument applies
for the derivative along
\upsilon_{u}
, proving the last property for
\mu
in the statement of Proposition
IV.3.25
.

Next, note that subsequent applications of Lemma
IV.3.28
will not increase the value of
\zeta

by more than
\frac{1}{4}
(we apply this lemma
\kappa
times, and we choose
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{4\kappa}
).
It follows from
(

ζ

-2)
that
\zeta
is less than
\frac{1}{2}

at all points that are connected with
S_{1}\setminus S_{\kappa}

by a trajectory of
\eta
. Therefore,
\mu=0
on that set by
(CH-1)
. Hence we can extend
\mu
to the whole of
f^{-1}(a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon)

by declaring it to be zero away from
S_{1}
. This proves
(

μ

-2)
and the second part of
(

μ

-3)
.
The fact
(

ζ

-3)
that initially
\zeta\geq 1
on
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f^{-1}[a+2\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
implies that after all the alterations we have

\zeta\geq\frac{3}{4}
on that set. Hence by
(CH-2)
, we have that
\chi\circ\zeta=1
, proving
(

μ

-3)
. We have proven that
(

μ

-1)
,
(

μ

-2)
, and
(

μ

-3)
hold. Also, we argued that
\partial_{\upsilon}(\mu)\geq 0
. So
\mu
satisfies all the required conditions.
∎

IV.3.6.
Enforcing condition
(LR-2)
: arranging
(IR-2)

We have shown that for any
\nabla F_{0}
, the function
\mu
can be changed in such a way that
G_{\tau}
is a regular homotopy.
Now, we will alter
\nabla F_{0}
to make sure that item
(IR-2)
of Addendum
IV.3.2
is satisfied. Recall that this says that
if
N=N_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup N_{\ell}
and the images
N_{i}
and
N_{j}

(
for all
i\neq j)
under
G_{0}
are disjoint, then
G_{\tau}(N_{i})\cap G_{\tau}(N_{j})=\emptyset
for all
\tau
whenever
i\neq j
.

While pushing a point on
G_{0}(N)
via the lift map
P
of Subsection
IV.3.4
we might create new double points. In fact,
we create a self-intersection of
G_{\tau}
each time that
P(G_{0}(u),\tau\mu(u))
hits another component of
G_{0}(N)
. In particular,
self-intersections are created only among points that are identified under the map
\Pi
. We will use this principle to prove the next result.

Proposition IV.3.29
.

Suppose
N=N_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup N_{\ell}
and
G_{0}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{0}(N_{\ell})
are pairwise disjoint. Then there exists
a vector field
\nabla F_{0}
such that
G_{\tau}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{\tau}(N_{\ell})
are pairwise disjoint for every
\tau\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Choose a grim vector field
\xi
for
F_{0}
.
It is usually not generic, in the sense that the map
\Pi
induced by
\xi

is not Thom-Boardman (see Definition
II.3.6
), in the sense that the Thom-Boardman strata are not smooth of the expected dimension. However,
\xi
has the following important property.

Lemma IV.3.30
.

No two points
z_{i},z_{j}
with
z_{i}\in G_{0}(N_{i})\cap\Omega^{\prime}
and
z_{j}\in G_{0}(N_{j})\cap\Omega^{\prime}
,
i\neq j
, are connected by a trajectory of
\xi
.

Recall that
\Omega^{\prime}=F_{0}^{-1}(a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon)
as in (
IV.3.4
).

Proof.

The result relies on property
(LR-1)
. If
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
are in distinct connected components of
G_{0}(N)
, the only trajectory of
\xi
that could possibly connect them has to lie in the zeroth stratum. As
z_{i},z_{j}
are both on the first stratum or deeper, the only possibility
that they can be connected by a trajectory in a shallower stratum is that both
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
are critical points of
F_{0}
(this follows from the definition of the grim vector field; see Definition
II.5.1
).

However, the fourth item of Lemma
IV.3.5
implies that all critical points of
F_{0}
in
\Omega^{\prime}
belong to the same component
G_{0}(N_{i})
.
This means that even if there is a trajectory in the zeroth stratum, it connects only critical points on the same connected component.
∎

Remark
.

We could also argue that there are no trajectories on the zeroth stratum for dimensional reasons. However, the present argument
will also be used in proving an analogous result when lifting paths of death, where a dimension counting argument does not work.

Now suppose that
p_{1},\dots,p_{r}
are critical points of
F_{0}
in
\Omega^{\prime}
(
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
being in this set). The critical point
p_{i}
is assumed to belong to
G_{0}(N_{j_{i}})
. Choose balls
B_{1},\dots,B_{r}
around these points.

Lemma IV.3.31
.

For sufficiently small balls
B_{1},\dots,B_{r}
, there is no trajectory of
\xi
that passes through
B_{i}
and a component
G_{0}(N_{j})
with
j\neq j_{i}
. Also,
there are no trajectories that connect two balls
B_{i}
and
B_{i^{\prime}}
whenever
j_{i}\neq j_{i^{\prime}}
.

Sketch of proof.

If for every choice of balls there were a trajectory violating one of these conditions, on taking smaller and smaller balls, and passing to a limit we would conclude that
there is a broken trajectory between two different components of
G_{0}(N)
. But such a broken trajectory would be composed
of actual trajectories connecting components of
G_{0}(N)
(no such trajectory can terminate at the zeroth stratum, because there are no critical points at depth
0
). Therefore, there would be at least one unbroken trajectory connecting components of
G_{0}(N)
, contradicting Lemma
IV.3.30
.∎

Now modify the vector field
\xi
inside
B_{1},\dots,B_{p}
so that
\xi
has no critical points in
\Omega^{\prime}
.
That is, replace the coordinate of
y_{11}
in (
II.5.1
) from
\sum y_{1j}^{2}
with
\phi+(1-\phi)\sum y_{1j}^{2}
,
where
\phi
is a bump function supported at
B_{-}
and equal to
1
at
p_{-}
(respectively supported on
B_{+}
and equal to
1

at
p_{+}
). Let
\xi^{\prime}
be the replaced vector field. It clearly satisfies the statement of Lemma
IV.3.31
,
so it also satisfies the statement of Lemma
IV.3.30
.

Now note that the condition in the statement of Lemma
IV.3.30
is open. Hence, we may perturb
\xi^{\prime}
to
\xi^{\prime\prime}
,
in such a way that
\xi^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the genericity condition of Lemma
IV.3.14
and such that no trajectory of
\xi

connects two different components of
G_{0}(N)
. Now
\xi^{\prime\prime}
is gradient-like for
F_{0}
in
\Omega^{\prime}
.
We choose a metric in such a way that
\nabla F_{0}=\xi^{\prime\prime}
(compare Theorem
II.5.2
).
∎

By Proposition
IV.3.29
, the previous proof with this choice of
\nabla F_{0}
gives the desired statement, with the addition that
(IR-2)
holds: if
G_{0}(N_{i})\cap G_{0}(N_{j})
for
i\neq j
, then no new intersections are created between the different components.

IV.3.7.
Condition
(LR-2)
for small dimensions: arranging
(IR-3)

In this subsection,
we focus on the case
k=2
. The first lemma holds for
k>2
as well, however, so we state it in general.

Lemma IV.3.32
.

Let
k\geq 2
.
Suppose
h_{-}>n-k
or
h_{+}<k
. Then, for generic
G_{0}
, Condition
(LR-2)
is automatically satisfied.

Proof.

If
h_{-}>n-k
, the manifold
\mathcal{K}_{-}
has dimension strictly less than
k
. The set of points in
N
that are mapped to the second stratum
by
G_{0}
has codimension
k
, so if
h_{-}>n-k
, this set is generically avoided by
\mathcal{K}_{-}
.
If
h_{+}<k
, then
\dim\mathcal{K}_{+}<k
, and we repeat the argument.
∎

Corollary IV.3.33
.

Suppose
k=2
and
n\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then condition
(LR-2)
is satisfied for generic
G_{0}
.

Proof.

Suppose
n=3
. The only situation when
\dim\mathcal{K}_{+}\geq k
is that
h_{+}=2,3
. In both cases, we know that
h_{-}\geq 2,3
, because
h_{-}\geq h_{+}
.
Then
h_{-}>n-k=1
, so we apply Lemma
IV.3.32
. The argument for
n=1,2
is analogous.
∎

Remark
.

If
n=4
, Condition
(LR-2)
is satisfied for generic
G_{0}
unless
h_{-}=h_{+}=2
. Indeed, for
n=4
,
\dim\mathcal{K}_{+}\geq 2
,
\dim\mathcal{K}_{-}\leq 2
and
\dim\mathcal{K}_{+}=\dim\mathcal{K}_{-}
can hold only when
h_{-}=h_{+}
.

IV.3.8.
Proof of Lifting Rearrangement Lemma
IV.3.1

We are now in position to prove Lifting of Rearrangement Lemma without assuming
(LR-1)
and
(LR-2)
at the beginning.
The proof consists of improving the initial pair
(F_{0},G_{0})
so as to assert
(LR-2)
. Then, we lift rearrangement. The key
difficulty we address in this subsection is to show that the path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
we construct is indeed a weak lift, that is
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
is left-homotopic to the original path. This relies on patiently controlling each step improving
(F_{0},G_{0})
to satisfy
conditions
(LR-1)
and then
(LR-2)
. A schematic of the proof is given in Figure
28
.

\mathcal{F}^{1}
f_{\tau}
\widetilde{\eta}
-path
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
\widetilde{\eta}
-path
\widetilde{f}_{1+\tau}
\widetilde{\eta}
-path
g_{\tau}
\widetilde{f}_{2+\tau}
f_{0}
(LR-1)
(LR-2)

Figure 28.
Paths created in the proof of Lifting Rearrangment Lemma
IV.3.1
.

Consider an immersed Morse function
F_{0}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and a vector field
\eta
on
N
that is gradient-like for
f_{0}=F_{0}\circ G_{0}
.
We denote the original
\eta
-path of rearrangement by
f_{\tau}
.

At first, we guarantee condition
(LR-1)
.
Lemma
IV.3.5
creates a double path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,1]
,
such that
F_{\tau}
is a
\xi
-path of rearrangement,
F_{\tau}
is supported
away from
\mathcal{L}_{-}\cup\mathcal{L}_{+}
. Set
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}:=F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
. Note that
F_{\tau}
is not moving any critical points at depth
1
,
hence
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
is actually a path of excellent Morse functions.
As
\xi
was constructed independently from
\eta
, we cannot expect that
\eta
is gradient-like for
\widetilde{f}_{1}
.
However, it is so near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
, which is enough for our purpose, as we now explain.

Lemma IV.3.34
.

There is a vector field
\widetilde{\eta}
on
N
that agrees with
\eta
near
\mathcal{K}_{-}
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}
and such that
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}

is a
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of excellent Morse functions.

Proof.

Choose
U_{1},U_{2}\subseteq N
to be two open subsets with the property that
U_{1}\supset\overline{U_{2}}\supset(\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+})
and such that
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}

is supported on
N\setminus U_{1}
. This is possible because Lemma
IV.3.5
uses only safe rearrangements. Shrink
U_{1}

so that
U_{1}
contains only
q_{-},q_{+}
as critical points of
f_{0}
.

Let
\phi
be a bump function equal to
1
on
U_{2}
and supported on
U_{1}
. Let
\eta_{\xi}
be the pull-back of
\xi
via
G_{1}
, as in Subsection
II.5.2
. Then
\eta_{\xi}
is a gradient-like vector field for
g_{\tau}
. Also, restricted to
U_{1}
,

\eta
is gradient-like vector field for
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
, because
{\widetilde{f}_{\tau}}|_{U_{1}}={\widetilde{f}_{0}}|_{U_{1}}
. Set
\widetilde{\eta}=\phi\eta+(1-\phi)\eta_{\xi}
. Then
\widetilde{\eta}
is gradient-like for
\widetilde{f}_{1}
and agrees with
\eta
on
U_{2}
.
∎

Next, Proposition
IV.3.12
applied to
(F_{1},G_{1},\widetilde{\eta})
creates another double path, which we now denote
(F_{1+\tau},G_{1+\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,1]
,
such that
\widetilde{f}_{1+\tau}:=F_{1+\tau}\circ G_{1+\tau}
is a path of excellent Morse functions,
\widetilde{\eta}
is gradient-like
for all
\widetilde{f}_{1+\tau}
, and
\widetilde{f}_{1+\tau}
does not depend on
\tau
near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
. The main outcome of Proposition
IV.3.12

is that
G_{2}
satisfies
(LR-2)
.

Let
\widetilde{f}_{2+\tau}
be the
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of rearrangement starting from
\widetilde{f}_{2}=F_{2}\circ G_{2}
.
The pair of functions
(F_{2},G_{2})
satisfies
(LR-2)
. Consider the
\widetilde{\eta}
-path
g_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,1]

that starts at
g_{0}:=\widetilde{f}_{2}
and lifts the critical point
q_{-}
above the critical point
q_{+}
. By Lemma
IV.3.4
,
the path can be weakly lifted. That is, there exists a double path
(F_{2+\tau},G_{2+\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,1]
, such that
\widetilde{f}_{2+\tau}:=F_{2+\tau}\circ G_{2+\tau}
is weakly homotopic to
g_{2+\tau}
.

Lemma IV.3.35
.

The path
\widetilde{f}_{2+\tau}
is lax homotopic to
f_{\tau}
over
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,2]
.

Proof.

As
\widetilde{f}_{2+\tau}
is left-homotopic to
g_{\tau}
, it is enough to show that
g_{\tau}
is lax homotopic to
f_{\tau}
over
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
.
This is precisely the statement of the lemma. We have set
h_{\sigma,0}=\widetilde{f}_{2\sigma,0}
is a path of excellent Morse functions
supported away from a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{K}_{-}
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}
. The path
f_{\tau}
is an
\eta
-path, while the path
g_{\tau}
is a
\widetilde{\eta}
-path, and the two vector fields agree in a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
.
∎

Corollary IV.3.36
.

The path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,3]
can be promoted to a weak lift of
f_{\tau}
.

Proof.

Lemma
IV.3.35
implies that the paths
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,3]
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
IV.1.6
.
∎

To conclude the proof of Lifting Rearrangment Lemma
IV.3.1
, we need to show that
G_{\tau}

has the properties stated in Addendum
IV.3.2
.
This is done with a case-by-case analysis.

•

If
G_{0}
is an embedding, then
(LR-2)
is always satisfied. Therefore, we do not create self-intersections by
changing
G_{0}
in such a way that
(IR-1)
holds;

•

If
N
is a disjoint union
N_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup N_{\ell}
with
G_{0}(N_{i})
and
G_{0}(N_{j})
disjoint for each
i\neq j
, then
we use the methods from Subsection
IV.3.6
to choose an appropriate vector field
\xi
so that condition
(IR-2)
is guaranteed.

•

If
k=2
and
\dim N\leq 3
, then we use Subsection
IV.3.7
to show that
(LR-2)
is automatically satisfied.

We have constructed a weak lift of the rearrangement by a regular double path, that does not introduce critical points on the zeroth or first stratum, and we have shown that the conditions in Addendum
IV.3.2
can be arranged, when appropriate. This completes the proof of Lemma
IV.3.1
.

IV.4.
Lifting paths of death

We now pass to the most important, and technically most difficult question, namely lifting paths of death.
In the proof of Lemma
IV.4.1
, we are going to use the finger move, described in detail in Part
V
.

Lemma IV.4.1

(Lifting paths of death)
.

Let
G\colon N\looparrowright\Omega
be a generic immersion with
\dim N=n
,
\dim\Omega=n+k
, and
k\geq 2
.
Suppose
F\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is an immersed Morse function and
f_{\tau}\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
, for
\tau\in[0,1]
, is an
elementary path of death such that
f_{0}=F\circ G
.

Then there exists a regular double path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
that weakly lifts
f_{\tau}
.

Suppose moreover that the level sets of the critical points that are cancelled are
a,b
.
The path
F_{\tau}
is supported on
F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
, while
G_{\tau}
is supported on
f_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
, where
\varepsilon>0
can be chosen as small as we please. In particular, if
f_{\tau}
is neat,
G_{0}
is neat and
F_{0}
is neat,
then
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
is neat.

As in the case of the Lifting Paths of Rearrangement Lemma
IV.3.1
, we give the follow-up result, which specifies additional
properties of
G_{\tau}
under certain conditions;

Addendum IV.4.2
.

The map
G_{\tau}
has the following properties.

•

If
k>2
, and
G_{0}
is an embedding, then
G_{\tau}
is an embedding for all
\tau
.

•

If
k=2
, and
G_{0}
is an embedding, then any new self-intersections are only added via a finite number of finger moves.

•

If
k=2
and
n\in\{1,2\}
, then any new self-intersections are only added via a finite number of finger moves.
If
n=1
self-intersections only occur at finitely many values of
\tau\in(0,1)
.

•

If
N=N_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup N_{\ell}
and
G_{0}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{0}(N_{\ell})
are pairwise disjoint, then for all
\tau\in[0,1]
, we also have that
G_{\tau}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{\tau}(N_{\ell})
are pairwise disjoint.

The proof of Lemma
IV.4.1
occupies the whole of Section
IV.4
, except for the finger move, which
occupies the whole of Part
V
.
Schematically, the proof is similar to the proof of the Lifting Paths of Rearrangement Lemma
IV.3.1
. First we set up notation,
then we prove Lemma
IV.4.1
under the conditions
(LD-1)
–
(LD-5)
specified
in Subsection
IV.4.2
. The next subsections,
IV.4.3
,
IV.4.4
,
IV.4.5
,
IV.4.6
, and
IV.4.7
, show how to arrange the function
F_{0}
and the embedding
G_{0}
in such a way that the conditions
(LD-1)
–
(LD-5)

(from Subsection
IV.4.2
) are satisfied. Finally, in Subsection
IV.4.9
,
we give a precise summary of all steps in the proof of Lemma
IV.4.1
.

IV.4.1.
Notation of the proof of Lemma
IV.4.1

We let
q_{-},q_{+}
denote the critical points of
f
that are cancelled along
f_{\tau}
. The indices are
h_{-}
and
h_{+}
(
h_{+}=h_{-}+1
).
By Assertion
III.6.7
,
it is enough to consider
f_{\tau}
that is an elementary path of death from Definition
III.6.6
.
We choose a double neighbourhood for
f
and we let
\eta
be a gradient-like vector field for
f
such
that this neighbourhood is adapted to the pair of good caps
(D_{\eta},A_{\eta})
. (It may help to refer back to Section
III.6
for the terminology.)

Let
\gamma
be the unique trajectory of
\eta
connecting
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
.
As
\dim\gamma=1
and the union of strata
\bigcup_{d\geq 2}\Omega[d]
is of codimension 2, we may perturb
G_{0}
to a generic immersion
such that
G_{0}(\gamma)
is disjoint from the second stratum and all higher strata. Note that a perturbation can be realised as a path

G_{\tau}
of
F_{0}
-regular generic immersions such that
F_{0}\circ G_{\tau}
is a path of an excellent Morse functions.
Hence, from now on, we will be assuming that
G_{0}(\gamma)
is disjoint from
\bigcup_{d\geq 2}\Omega[d]
.

Set
a=f(q_{-})
,
b=f(q_{+})
.
Choose a neighbourhood
U_{0}
of
\gamma

such that
G_{0}
maps
U_{0}
to the first stratum. Write
\mathcal{K}_{-}\subseteq N
, respectively
\mathcal{K}_{+}\subseteq N
for the unstable manifold of
q_{-}
, respectively for the stable manifold of
q_{+}
. We choose
\varepsilon>0
such
that
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f^{-1}[a,a+\varepsilon]
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}\cap f^{-1}[b-\varepsilon,b]
are contained in
U_{0}
.
Note that
\gamma\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap\mathcal{K}_{+}\cap f^{-1}[a,b]
.
Define
\mathcal{L}_{-}=G_{0}(\mathcal{K}_{-})
,

\mathcal{L}_{+}=G_{0}(\mathcal{K}_{+})
. As usual, we set
p_{+}=G_{0}(q_{+})
and
p_{-}=G_{0}(q_{-})
.

IV.4.2.
Lifting paths of death under extra conditions

We will structure the proof using the following conditions on
\eta
,
f
, and
G_{0}
.

(LD-1)

There are no critical points of
F
at depth
0
with critical values in
[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
;

(LD-2)

The unique trajectory
\gamma
on
N
, between
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
, is mapped to the first stratum;

(LD-3)

The subsets
\mathcal{K}_{-}
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}
of
N
are mapped to the first stratum of
G_{0}(N)
;

(LD-4)

There exists a grim vector field
\xi
for
F
such that the pull-back
\widetilde{\eta}
of
\xi
via
G_{0}

gives rise to a path of death that is left-homotopic to a path of death constructed with
\eta
,
and moreover there are no critical points of
F
in
F^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
other than
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
;

(LD-5)

There are no trajectories of
\xi
from
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
other than the image of the trajectory
\gamma
.

Remark
.

(LD-1)
and
(LD-3)
are the analogues of
(LR-1)
and
(LR-2)
respectively; however
(LD-3)
requires
that
both

\mathcal{K}_{-}
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}
are mapped to the first stratum, whereas
(LR-2)
required only one of them.

A condition on the lack of other critical points appears twice, in
(LD-1)
and in
(LD-4)
.
First, before we construct an appropriate
vector field, we are allowed to perform only safe rearrangements (see the discussion in the proof of Lemma
IV.3.7
). The lack of critical points at depth
0

is important in ensuring
(LD-3)
. Once we have a vector field extending
\eta
, we have more control when we perform rearrangements, and this enables us to
move all critical points away from between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
.
Arranging for
(LD-5)
to hold is rather difficult: it will require the finger move, which takes the whole of Part
V
to construct.
Removing the other critical points and arranging that there are no trajectories other than
\gamma
are both needed to apply Cancellation Theorem
III.4.1
.

Lemma IV.4.3

(Conditional lifting of paths of death)
.

Suppose conditions
(LD-4)
and
(LD-5)
are satisfied. Then there exists a path
F_{\tau}
such that
(F_{\tau},G_{0})
weakly lifts
f_{\tau}
.

Proof.

The Cancellation Theorem
III.4.1
uses a vector field
\xi
to create a regular double path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,2]
that cancels the pair of critical points
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. This is possible since
(LD-5)
is satisfied.
Then the composition
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
is an
\widetilde{\eta}
path of death.
By
(LD-4)
, it is left-homotopic to the original path of death constructed with
\eta
. By Corollary
IV.1.4
,
the path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
can be promoted to a weak lift of the original
\eta
-path.
∎

Remark
.

In the proof of Lemma
IV.4.3
we did not use properties
(LD-1)
,
(LD-2)
, and
(LD-3)
. However these properties will
be needed to construct a vector field
\xi
satisfying
(LD-4)
and
(LD-5)
.

IV.4.3.
Removing critical points from
[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]

In this section we arrange that condition
(LD-1)
holds. The procedure uses the rearrangement theorem and the approach is similar to the approach of Subsection
IV.3.3
. In particular, we might need to replace the original vector field
\eta
by another gradient-like vector field,
\widetilde{\eta}
, which agrees with the original one near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
.

Lemma IV.4.4
.

There exists a path of Morse functions
F_{\tau}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
such that
F_{\tau}=F_{0}
on
U_{0}
,
F_{1}
has no critical
points in
[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
at depth
0
and

F_{\tau}\circ G_{0}
is a path of Morse functions on
N
with constant
critical values, and such that the support of
F_{\tau}\circ G_{0}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
.

Proof.

The proof follows the argument of Lemma
IV.3.5
and discovers what are the depths and indices of the critical points that can and cannot be moved out of the region
F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
. The only difference with Lemma
IV.3.5
is that
h_{+}=h_{-}+1
, where we recall that
h_{\pm}
is the index of
q_{\pm}
.

We begin with the case
k=2
, which is the most rigid. Choose a grim vector field
\xi
for
F
. By Lemma
IV.3.7
,
a critical point
p
can be safely (see Definition
IV.3.9
) moved up by a
\xi
-path if
h_{p}+d_{p}>h_{+}+1=h_{-}+2
(or, if
d_{p}=0
), and
p
can be moved down by a
\xi
-path if
h_{p}+d_{p}<h_{-}+1
. Acting as in the proof of Lemma
IV.3.5

we can move all critical points with
h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{-}
below
p_{-}
and all critical points with
h_{p}+d_{p}\geq h_{-}+3
above
p_{+}
. There remain critical points with
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1
or
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+2
. These points cannot be safely rearranged in general.

Suppose a point
p^{\prime}
is between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
and
d_{p^{\prime}}=0
. If
h_{p^{\prime}}=h_{-}+1
, we can safely move it below
p_{-}
; here any rearrangement with other critical points is safe and possible, because the only critical points
have
h_{p}+d_{p}\geq h_{-}+1
. Compare the algorithm for handling
(SR-2)
in the proof of Lemma
IV.3.10
above. On the other hand, if
h_{p^{\prime}}=h_{-}+2
, we move it up.

Concatenating the paths of rearrangement, we construct a
\xi
-path
F_{\tau}
supported away from
\mathcal{L}_{-}\cup\mathcal{L}_{+}
. The only critical points of
F_{1}
in
F_{1}^{-1}(a,b)
are at depth
2
or more (recall that the critical points at depth
1
correspond to critical points of
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
, and since we consider a path of death there are none; see Section
III.6
) and are such that
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1
or
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+2
. This concludes the case
k=2
.

Suppose
k>2
. Lemma
IV.3.7
allows us to safely move a critical point

p
below
p_{-}
as long as
h_{p}+d_{p}<h_{-}+1
. If

h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)>h_{+}=h_{-}+1
, we can safely move a point
p
above

p_{+}
. The only
possibility that a critical point cann be safely moved neither above
p_{+}

nor below
p_{-}
is if
h_{p}+d_{p}\geq h_{-}+1
and
h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)\leq h_{+}=h_{-}+1
simultaneously. These two conditions imply that
h_{p}+d_{p}\geq h_{p}+(k-1)(d_{p}-1)
, that is,
d_{p}\leq\frac{k-1}{k-2}
. Since
d_{p}\geq 2

and
k\geq 3
by assumptions, the only possibility is that
d_{p}=2
and

k=3
. In that case, we must have
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1
. ∎

We remark that the rearrangements performed during the proof of Lemma
IV.4.4
, might result in
\eta

not being a gradient-like vector field for
f_{1}:=F_{1}\circ G_{0}
. However,
f_{1}=f_{0}
near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
. Therefore,
\eta
is a gradient-like vector field for
f_{1}
near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
. By Proposition
II.5.3
, there exists a gradient-like vector field
\widetilde{\eta}
for
f_{1}
agreeing with
\eta
near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
.

We can refine the result if
N
is a union of disjoint components, and
G_{0}
maps all these components to pairwise disjoint stratified manifolds.

Lemma IV.4.5
.

Suppose
N=N_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup N_{\ell}
, the images
G_{0}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{0}(N_{\ell})
are pairwise disjoint, and
p_{+},p_{-}
belong to the component
G_{0}(N_{1})
. Then the path of rearrangement from Lemma
IV.4.4
can be chosen to additionally push all critical points away from
F_{0}^{-1}(a,b)
,
except for those that belong to
N_{1}
.

Remark
.

Unlike in Lemma
IV.3.5
, we need not consider the case where
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
are in separate components; the existence of a trajectory in
G_{0}(N)
connecting
q_{-}
to
q_{+}
implies that
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
belong to the same connected component of
G_{0}(N)
.

Proof of Lemma
IV.4.5
.

Lemma
IV.4.4
allows us to move critical points away from
F_{0}^{-1}(a,b)
under certain conditions
on
h_{p}+d_{p}
depending on the codimension
k
. If
k>3
, we are able to move all critical points, so the statement of Lemma
IV.4.5
follows from Lemma
IV.4.4
.

Assume
k=2
. By Lemma
IV.4.4
, only critical points that cannot be safely moved away from
F_{0}^{-1}(a,b)

are those for which
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1
or
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+2
, and
d_{p}\geq 2
.

The proof in that case follows the same lines as the proof of the fourth item of Lemma
IV.3.5
, so here we only give a quick sketch. If a critical point
p
has
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1
,
its descending membrane is disjoint from the ascending membrane of
p_{-}
for dimensional reasons. Next, if
p
and
p_{-}

belong to different components of
G_{0}(N)
, the membrane of
p
belongs to a different component of
G_{0}(N)
than
\mathcal{L}_{-}=G_{0}(\mathcal{K}_{-})
.
Moreover, for dimensional reasons, the descending membrane of
p
is disjoint from all the ascending membranes of critical points
between
p_{-}
and
p
. Hence,
p
can be safely moved below the level set of
p_{-}
.

In this way, we can push all critical points with
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1
below the level set of
p_{-}
, except for those that belong
to the same component of
G_{0}(N)
as
p_{-}
. Reversing this construction, we push all critical points with
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+2
above
the level set of
p_{+}
, with the exception of critical points belonging to the same component as
p_{+}
.

For
k=3
we essentially repeat the argument, except that we only need to move critical points with
h_{p}+d_{p}=h_{-}+1

and
d_{p}=2
.
This completes the proof that
(LD-1)
can be arranged, and moreover if the
G_{0}(N_{i})
are pairwise disjoint, the only critical points remaining in
F^{-1}(a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon)
are on the connected component
G_{0}(N_{1})
.
∎

IV.4.4.
Proving
(LD-2)

In order to prove
(LD-3)
(the analogue of
(LR-2)
), we need an intermediate step. This is the place where the present situation differs from the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
used for lifting rearrangements in the previous section. As in Subsection
IV.3.4
,
we will be working with a gradient-like vector field for
F_{0}
as an ordinary Morse function. Again, to distinguish these gradient-like
vector fields from grim vector fields, we use the notation
\nabla F_{0}
. Note that any gradient-like vector field is a gradient for some choice of Riemannian metric, so this is not a significant abuse of notation.

Lemma IV.4.6
.

There exists a vector field
\nabla F_{0}
, gradient-like for
F_{0}
, such that with
\Omega^{\prime}=F_{0}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
we have:

•

the projection
\Pi
from
\Omega^{\prime}
to the level set
F^{-1}(a-\varepsilon)
is a Thom-Boardman map
(
see Definition
II.3.6
)
;

•

suppose
N=N_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup N_{\ell}
and that
G_{0}(N_{i})
are pairwise disjoint for
i=1\dots,\ell
. Then there are open balls near critical points of
F_{0}
, which we can choose to be as small as we please, such that
there are no trajectories of
\nabla F_{0}
that connect two distinct components
G_{0}(N_{i})
and
G_{0}(N_{j})
.

Proof.

The first item follows from the arguments that have already been used in the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
.
Namely, the map
\Pi
is defned as the projection onto
F^{-1}(a-\varepsilon)
along the trajectories of
\nabla F_{0}
. By Lemma
IV.3.13
, a small perturbation of
\Pi
can be realized by a perturbation of
\nabla F_{0}
within the family of gradient-like
vector fields for
F_{0}
. Furthermore, Proposition
II.3.7
guarantees, that any
\Pi
can be perturbed to a Thom–Boardman map.
That is, any gradient-like vector field
\nabla F_{0}
can be perturbed to a gradient-like vector field satisfying the first condition.
The second part is proved in the same way as Lemmas
IV.3.30
and
IV.3.31
.
∎

We need to add an extra condition regarding the vector field
\nabla F_{0}
.

Definition IV.4.7
.

We say that
\gamma
is in
good position
with respect to
G_{0}
if for every
w\in G_{0}(\gamma)
the trajectory
of
\nabla F_{0}
through
w
does not hit
G_{0}(N)\cap\Omega^{\prime}
, except at
w
.

Lemma IV.4.8
.

•

If
k>2
, then for a generic gradient-like vector field
\nabla F_{0}
,
\gamma
is in a good position with respect to
G_{0}
.

•

If
k=2
, and
F_{0}
satisfies
(LD-1)
, then
there exists a regular
F_{0}
-path of immersions
G_{\tau}

such that
G_{\tau}
is fixed on
\gamma
,
\eta
is a gradient-like vector field
for
F_{0}\circ G_{\tau}
, for all
\tau\in[0,1]
, and
\gamma
is in good position with respect to
G_{1}
.

•

If
N=N_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup N_{\ell}
and
G_{0}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{0}(N_{\ell})
are pairwise disjoint, then
G_{\tau}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{\tau}(N_{\ell})
are also pairwise disjoint for all
\tau\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

For each point
z
in
\gamma
, let
\nu_{z}
denote the set of points in
\Omega^{\prime}
that lie on the trajectory
of
\nabla F_{0}
through
G_{0}(z)
.
The set
C=\bigcup_{z\in\gamma}\nu_{z}
is two-dimensional and contains
G_{0}(\gamma)
by construction.
This set can be reinterpreted as follows: the flow of
\nabla F_{0}
induces a projection
\Pi
of
\Omega^{\prime}

onto the level set
F_{0}^{-1}(a-\varepsilon)
; see Lemma
IV.3.13
above and the discussion
in Subsection
IV.3.4
. Suppose
\Pi
is generic with respect to
\gamma
, that is, it satisfies the following two conditions.

(1)

First
\beta^{\mathbf{\ell}}\Pi|_{\gamma}
is smooth
of expected dimension (where
\beta^{\mathbf{i}}\gamma
denotes the Thom–Boardman stratification, see Subsection
II.3.2
).
As
\dim\gamma=1
, the expected dimension of
\beta^{1}\gamma
is negative. This means that
\beta^{\mathbf{1}}\gamma
is empty, so that
\Pi|_{\gamma}
is a generic immersion.

(2)

Secondly, we assume that the set of double points of the image is of expected dimension. This set is of codimension
(n+k-3)
in
\Pi(\gamma)
. As we assumed that
k\geq 2
, the set of double points is empty unless
n=1
and
k=2
. This means that
\Pi^{-1}\Pi(\gamma)
is either a smooth surface (if
n>1
or
k>3
) or a surface with
1
-dimensional set of singularities: each such singularity is a product of an ordinary double point of a curve and a segment.

These two conditions imply that in both cases,
\Pi^{-1}\Pi(\gamma)
is Whitney stratified (Definition
II.1.1
). The set
C
is an open subset of
\Pi^{-1}\Pi(\gamma)
, so it inherits Whitney stratification. In particular, stratified general position arguments can be applied to
C
as follows.

If
k>2
, the set
C
can be made disjoint from
G_{0}(N)\setminus G_{0}(\gamma)
(which is of codimension
k>\dim C
)
by perturbing
\Pi
. This proves
the first item.

For the remaining part of the proof, we will assume that
k=2
.
As
C
is stratified of dimension
2
, a general position argument guarantees that
C
intersects
G_{0}(N)\setminus G_{0}(\gamma)
at finitely many points,
and each such point is a smooth point of
C
(because the singular set of
C
has dimension
1<\codim G_{0}(N)\setminus G_{0}(\gamma)
).
Moreover,
C
avoids the second stratum of
G_{0}(N)
. Let

w_{1},\dots,w_{t}\in N
be such that
C\cap(G_{0}(N)\setminus G_{0}(\gamma))=\{G_{0}(w_{1}),\dots,G_{0}(w_{t})\}
;
see Figure
29
.

\gamma
q_{-}
q_{+}
\gamma_{1}
\gamma_{2}
G_{0}(\gamma)
p_{1}
p_{2}
\nabla F_{0}
G_{0}(w_{1})
G_{0}(w_{2})
G_{0}(\gamma_{1})
G_{0}(\gamma_{2})

Figure 29.
Notation of proof of Lemma
IV.4.8
.

Similarly to Subsection
IV.3.5
, define
\Sigma\subseteq N
to be the subset of points
u\in N
such
that
\nabla F_{0}
is tangent at
G_{0}(u)
to the branch of
G_{0}(N)
.
Denote the collection of trajectories of
\eta
through points on
w_{1},\dots,w_{t}
by
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{t}
.
The curves
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{t}
are one-dimensional, while
\Sigma\subseteq N
is of codimension
k
.
By general position arguments (choosing appropriate
\Pi
) we may and will assume that all the curves
\gamma_{i}
are disjoint from
\Sigma
.

The original trajectory
\gamma
is not any of the
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{t}
, therefore, each of the
\gamma_{i}

either does not terminate at
q_{+}
,
or does not start at
q_{-}
(or both).
Suppose
\gamma_{1}
does not terminate at
q_{+}
. As
\gamma_{1}
is a trajectory of
\eta
, the fact that
\gamma_{1}
does not terminate at
q_{+}
implies that
\gamma_{1}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{K}_{+}
.

Our aim is to lift
\gamma_{1}
so that the point
w_{1}
lands above the level
f^{-1}(b+\varepsilon)
, and then to apply induction. To accomplish the first goal, we use the procedure of the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
. Set
c_{1}=f(w_{1})
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
such that
c_{1}-\varepsilon^{\prime}>a
.

Choose a neighbourhood
U_{1}
of
\gamma_{1}\cap f^{-1}(c_{1}-\varepsilon^{\prime},b+\varepsilon)
disjoint from
\mathcal{K}_{+}
and all the other curves
\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{t}
. We assume that all trajectories
of
\eta
passing through
U_{1}
avoid
\Sigma
in
f^{-1}([c_{1}-\varepsilon^{\prime},b+\varepsilon])
. Such a choice of
U_{1}

is possible, since
\gamma_{1}
avoids
\Sigma
itself. Pick a function

\mu\colon f^{-1}[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]\to[0,1]

satisfying analogues of
items
(

μ

-1)
,
(

μ

-2)
, and
(

μ

-3)
. Namely:

•

\partial_{\eta}\mu\geq 0
;

•

\mu
is supported on
U_{1}
;

•

\mu=1
on
\gamma_{1}
.

Existence of such
\mu
follows from the same arguments as in Lemma
IV.3.16
.
Note that in this specific case, we need not care about the condition
\partial_{\upsilon}\mu\geq 0
as in Lemma
IV.3.24
.
In fact,
\mu
is supported on
U_{1}
and we have chosen
U_{1}
so as to avoid
\Sigma
. The vector field
\upsilon
was defined
only at points on
\Sigma
.

This function, together with the choice of the rescaling function
R
and the lift function
P
as in the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
, allows us to create a regular homotopy lifting
the trajectory
\gamma_{1}
and its neighbourhood
along the trajectories of
\nabla F_{0}
, to
arrange that no point of
\gamma_{1}
belongs to the same trajectory of
\nabla F_{0}
as a point on
\gamma
.
We have the following properties.

•

As we already mentioned, the procedure is needed only for
k=2
.

•

If
N
is presented as a disjoint sum of
N_{i}
with
G_{0}(N_{i})
pairwise disjoint, then
\gamma_{1}
belongs to the same component as
\gamma
. While we might create some new self-intersections, the only new self-intersections we create are within
G(N_{1})
.

If
\gamma_{1}
terminates at
q_{+}
, then it cannot start at
q_{-}
. We push it down instead of up, by the same procedure. An inductive argument constructs the required regular homotopy.
∎

IV.4.5.
From
(LD-1)
and
(LD-2)
to
(LD-3)

Lemma IV.4.9
.

Suppose
(F_{0},G_{0},\eta)
satisfies
(LD-1)
and
(LD-2)
.
There exists a regular homotopy
G_{\tau}
such that
\eta

is a gradient-like vector field for
F_{0}\circ G_{\tau}
for all
\tau
and
G_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{-})
and such that
G_{1}(\mathcal{K}_{+})
belong to the first stratum
of
G_{1}(N)
, i.e. such that
(LD-3)
is satisfied. The path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
with
F_{\tau}=F_{0}
is a regular double path.

Moreover:

•

if
G_{0}(N)
is an embedding, then
G_{\tau}=G_{0}
.

•

if
G_{0}(N)
is a disjoint union of
G_{0}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{0}(N_{\ell})

(
with
N=N_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup N_{\ell})
,
then
G_{\tau}(N)
is a disjoint union of
G_{\tau}(N_{1}),\dots,G_{\tau}(N_{\ell})
for all
\tau\in[0,1]
.

Remark
.

The condition
(LD-3)
is stronger than
(LD-2)
, that is
G_{1}
satisfies
(LD-2)
as well. However, the procedure of arranging for
(LD-3)
might create extra self-intersections, hence we do not control critical points at deeper strata. That is,
(F_{1},G_{1})
need not satisfy
(LD-1)
. These critical points will be dealt later, with
(LD-4)
, which in turn implies
(LD-1)
.

Proof.

If
G_{0}(N)
is an embedding then it automatically satisfies
(LD-3)
.

The proof of Lemma
IV.4.9
uses the whole machinery of the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
. The presence of the trajectory
\gamma
prevents us from applying that proposition directly, because in the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
,
we were assuming that
\mathcal{K}_{+}
and
\mathcal{K}_{-}
are disjoint. However, thanks to
(LD-2)
,
we can apply these arguments with
only minor modifications.

Choose
\varepsilon>0
. As in the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
, define
Z
by Condition
IV.3.15
.
We note that
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
belongs to
Z
. Recall that
Z
is an open subset of the level set
f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
.

Let
U_{0}\subseteq f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
be
a neighbourhood of
\gamma\cap f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
such that if
z\in U_{0}
, if
\lambda_{z}
is a trajectory
of
\eta
through
z
(its part in
N^{\prime}:=G_{0}^{-1}(\Omega^{\prime})
; see (
IV.3.4
)), and if
w\in G_{0}(\lambda_{z})
, then the trajectory of
\nabla F_{0}
through
w

does not hit
G_{0}(N)
at any point in
\Omega^{\prime}
except at
w
itself. This is a condition analogous to Definition
IV.4.7
.
Note that
\gamma
is in good position, so
\gamma\cap f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
indeed belongs to
U_{0}
. In particular
U_{0}
is not empty. The condition defining
U_{0}
is open, so we may indeed choose
U_{0}
to be open.

Let
V
be an open set of
f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
such that
\overline{V}\subseteq Z
,
V
contains
(\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon))\setminus U_{0}
,
and
V
is disjoint from some open subset
U_{1}
containing
\mathcal{K}_{+}
. Notice that the only point on
f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)

that belongs to
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap\mathcal{K}_{+}
is the intersection point of
\gamma
with the level set (and that belongs to
U_{0}
). Therefore,
such a
V
exists. Let
Z_{\eta}
and
V_{\eta}
be
the sets of points
in
f^{-1}[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
that are reached from
Z
, respectively
V
, by a trajectory of
\eta
; compare Figure
30
.

f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
\mathcal{K}_{-}
\mathcal{K}_{+}
\gamma
U_{0}
Z
V
V
U_{1}

Figure 30.
Notation of the proof of Lemma
IV.4.9
. The figure represents the level set
f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
.

To ensure property
(LD-3)
holds, we construct yet another cut-off function
\mu
, with similar properties to the function with the same name from Section
IV.3
, but different from it.
Let

\mu\colon f_{0}^{-1}[a+\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]\to[0,1]

be a smooth function such that

•

\mu
is supported on
Z_{\eta}
;

•

\mu\equiv 1
on
V_{\eta}
;

•

\partial_{\eta}\mu\geq 0
with equality only at points where
\mu
is zero or one;

•

\partial_{\upsilon}\mu(u)\geq 0
for all
u\in\Sigma\cap Z_{\eta}
with equality only for when
\mu
is zero or one, here

\upsilon
is as in Subsection
IV.3.5
.

The construction of
\mu
is analogous to the construction in Lemma
IV.3.16
. Using
\mu
, and constructing an appropriate scaling function
R
and a lift function
P
as in Subsection
IV.3.4
, we define the isotopy
G_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[1,2]
,
that lifts all the points
w
with
f_{1}(w)\in[a+2\varepsilon,b]
whose trajectory passes through
V
above the level set
F_{0}^{-1}(b)
.
The construction of
G_{\tau}
is via the same formula as in the proof of Proposition
IV.3.12
; see especially (
IV.3.8
).

The definition of
G_{\tau}
implies that the arguments of Lemma
IV.3.17
apply to show that
F_{0}\circ G_{\tau}
is an
\eta
-path of functions.
To show that
G_{1}
maps
\mathcal{K}_{-}
to the first stratum, we use arguments analogous to the proof of Lemma
IV.3.18
.
Take
w\in\mathcal{K}_{-}
, and let
w^{\prime}\in f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
be the point on the same trajectory of
\eta
as
\mathcal{K}_{-}
. Consider the following
two cases.

•

If
w^{\prime}\in V
, then

–

either
f_{0}(w)>a+\varepsilon
and so
F_{0}\circ G_{1}(w)>b+\varepsilon/2
, and we are done;

–

or
f_{0}(w)<a+\varepsilon
and then
G_{\tau}(w)
belongs to the first stratum by construction.

•

If
w^{\prime}\notin V
, then, as
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}_{-}
, we infer that
w^{\prime}\in U_{0}
(by construction,
V\cup U_{0}
contains
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap f_{0}^{-1}(a+\varepsilon)
). Then, by definition of
U_{0}
, the trajectory of
\nabla F_{0}
through
G_{0}(w)

does not hit
G_{0}(N^{\prime})
, so
G_{\tau}(w^{\prime})
belongs to the first stratum.

After this isotopy
\mathcal{K}_{-}
is mapped to the first stratum.

An analogous operation improves
G_{0}
in such a way that
\mathcal{K}_{+}
is
eventually mapped to the first stratum. The concatenation of the two paths (one making
\mathcal{K}_{-}
map to the first stratum, the other
making
\mathcal{K}_{+}
map to the first stratum) is the desired path of immersions
G_{\tau}
. We perturb it rel
G_{0}
to be a regular
F_{0}
-path in the sense of Definition
III.1.20
, so that
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
, with
F_{\tau}=F_{0}
is a regular double path.

To prove the second item of the lemma,
we use the same method as in the proof of Lemma
IV.4.5
. The conditions
on
\nabla F_{0}
, guaranteed by Lemma
IV.4.6
, ensure that we do not create intersections between different components of
N
.
∎

IV.4.6.
Condition
(LD-4)
and the vector field
\xi

Throughout Subsection
IV.4.6
we assume that
(F_{0},G_{0})
satisfies
(LD-3)
. Recall that the condition
(LD-4)

tells about the existence of a suitable vector field
\xi
and the lack of critical points in
F^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]

other than
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. The next lemma constructs the vector field. That vector field will be used to move all the unnecessary critical
points away from the interval
F^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
.

Lemma IV.4.10
.

Suppose
F
is immersed Morse,
G\colon N\hookrightarrow\Omega
is a generic immersion,
f=F\circ G
and
\eta
is gradient-like for
f
.
Assume
G
maps
\mathcal{K}_{-}
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}
to the first stratum, i.e.
(LD-3)
holds.
There exists a grim vector field
\xi
for
F

such that the pull-back
\widetilde{\eta}
of
\xi
to
N
agrees with
\eta
in an open subset of
N
containing
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
.
If
G
is an embedding, we can guarantee that
\eta^{\prime}=\eta
.

As the pull-back
\widetilde{\eta}
of
\xi
agrees with
\eta
in an open subset of
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
, Lemma
III.6.15
gives us
a left-homotopy of the
\eta
-path and the
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of death.

Proof of Lemma
IV.4.10
.

As
(F,G)
satisfies
(LD-3)
, the map
G
takes a neighbourhood
U_{1}\subseteq N
of
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
to the first stratum.
Take
U_{2}\subseteq N
open such that
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}\subseteq U_{2}\subseteq\overline{U}_{2}\subseteq U_{1}
.
By Proposition
II.5.3
, applied to
U=G(U_{1})
there exists a grim vector field
\xi
for
F
, which is equal to
DG(\eta)

on the whole of
G(U_{2})
. We perturb
\xi
so that it is Morse–Smale.

By definition, the pull-back
\widetilde{\eta}
of
\xi
agrees with
\eta
on
U
.
∎

To guarantee
(LD-4)
, we need to rearrange critical points to move them away from between
F(p_{-})=a
and
F(p_{+})=b
.
This includes the critical points that have not been rearranged before and the critical points that might have been created while enforcing
(LD-3)
.

Lemma IV.4.11
.

Suppose
(F,G)
satisfies
(LD-3)
and
\xi
is as constructed in Lemma
IV.4.10
. Then, there exists
a path of functions
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
such that
F_{\tau}
is a
\xi
-path,
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
is a path
of excellent Morse functions supported away from
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
,
G_{\tau}=G

(
in particular,
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
is a regular double path
)

and moreover,
F_{1}
has no critical
points in
F_{1}^{-1}[a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon]
other than
p-
and
p_{+}
.

Proof.

We proceed in a standard way. That is, we take the first critical point above
p_{-}
with
h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{-}+1
.
We move it below
p
using Rearrangement Theorem
III.2.1
(compare the proof of Lemma
IV.3.4
).
Inductively, we construct a special
\xi
-path that moves all critical points with
h_{p}+d_{p}\leq h_{-}+1
below
p_{-}
. We can do it
in such a way that the path is supported away from
\mathcal{L}_{-}
. Next,
the same argument allows us to move all critical points
p
with
h_{p}+d_{p}\geq h_{+}+1
above the level set
p_{+}
. This done,
since
h_{+}=h_{-}+1
, there are no more critical points between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. We let
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
, for
\tau\in[0,1]
,
denote the resulting path. This is a
\xi
-path, a concatenation of
\xi
-paths of rearrangments. Therefore
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}

is an
\widetilde{\eta}
-path. Notice that we do not rearrange pairs of critical points at depth
1
, that is,
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}

is a path of excellent functions.
∎

Remark
.

Unlike in item
(LD-1)
, our vector field
\xi
extends
\widetilde{\eta}
. That is, the condition that the membranes of points being rearranged miss
\mathcal{L}_{-}
and
\mathcal{L}_{+}
is included in the Morse–Smale condition, and need not be additionally guaranteed by the condition on a safe rearrangement.

For the reader’s convenience we summarise the properties of the functions we have constructed.

Corollary IV.4.12
.

The pair
(F_{1},G_{1})
that is the outcome of Lemma
IV.4.11
satisfies conditions
(LD-1)
,
(LD-2)
,
(LD-3)
,
and
(LD-4)
.

Proof.

The vector field
\xi
from Lemma
IV.4.10
is grim for
F_{1}
, because Lemma
IV.4.11
constructed
F_{1}
as the end of a
\xi
-path. Next, Lemma
IV.4.11
does not change the immersion
G
, in particular
(LD-3)
is preserved and
(LD-4)
is arranged to hold. Next, condition
(LD-3)
generalises (and implies) the condition
(LD-2)
: the former one tells about the behaviour of
G
on
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
, the latter
controls
G
on
\gamma=\mathcal{K}_{-}\cap\mathcal{K}_{+}
. Finally, condition
(LD-4)
controls all critical points between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
, while
condition
(LD-1)
controls only some of them. That is,
(LD-4)
implies
(LD-1)
.
∎

IV.4.7.
The Finger Move Theorem

Before we complete the proof of Lifting Paths of Death Lemma
IV.4.1
, we need the most important ingredient, namely the Finger Move Theorem, which we now state. The proof is deferred to Part
V
.

Theorem IV.4.13

(Finger Move)
.

Let
G\colon N\looparrowright\Omega
be a generic immersion
with
\dim N=n
and
\dim\Omega=n+2
. Let
F\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be an immersed Morse function with respect to
M=G(N)
and let
\xi

be a grim vector field for
F
satisfying the Morse–Smale condition.
Set
f=F\circ G\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
and let
\eta
be the pull-back of
\xi
as in Section
II.5.2
, a gradient-like vector field on
N
. Assume that
\xi
and
\eta
are Morse–Smale.

Suppose that there exist critical points
q_{-},q_{+}\in N
of indices
h
and
h+1
respectively, such that there is a single trajectory
\gamma
of
\eta

connecting
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
, and
G
maps the following subsets to the first stratum of
M=G(N)
, where
a:=F(p_{-})
,
b:=F(p_{+})
: (i)
\gamma
; (ii) the part of the stable manifold of
q_{+}
in
F^{-1}(a,b)
; and (iii) the part of the unstable manifold of
q_{-}
in
F^{-1}(a,b)
.
Suppose also there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\xi

has no critical points in the region
F^{-1}(a-\varepsilon,b+\varepsilon)
other than
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. Let
r>0
be the number of trajectories of
\xi
connecting
p_{-}=G(q_{-})
to
p_{+}=G(q_{+})
that do not lie on
M=G(N)
.

Then there exists a path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
, for
\tau\in[0,2]
, and a grim vector field
\overline{\xi}
for
F_{2}
,
such that
F_{\tau}
for
\tau\in[0,1]
is an arbitrarily small perturbation of
F_{0}
,
F_{1+\tau}=F_{1}
for
\tau\in[0,1]
, and
G_{\tau}=G_{0}
for
\tau\in[0,1]
. The path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
and
\overline{\xi}
satisfy the following properties.

(FM-1)

The Morse function
F_{2}
has at most four more critical points than
F_{0}
and these critical points belong to the second stratum of
G_{2}(N)
. Also they lie in the image of a single connected component of
N
under
G_{2}
.

(FM-2)

F_{2}=F_{0}
near all critical points of
F_{0}
.

(FM-3)

The trajectories of
\overline{\xi}
connecting
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
are precisely the trajectories of
\xi
connecting
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
, except that one trajectory outside of
G_{0}(N)

(
and outside of
G_{2}(N))
is removed.

(FM-4)

Set
f_{\tau}=F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
. The path
f_{1+\tau}
is a constant path for
\tau\in[0,1]
. Moreover,
\eta
is a gradient-like vector field for all
f_{\tau}
.

(FM-5)

There is a pull-back
\overline{\eta}
of
\overline{\xi}
via
G_{2}
such that the paths of death starting from
F_{2}\circ G_{2}
and
constructed with
\eta
and
\overline{\eta}
are left-homotopic.

(FM-6)

The map
G_{\tau}(N)
fails to be a generic immersion only for one time value of
\tau

(
but still it is an immersion
)
.
The double points that are created are within the same connected component of
G_{0}(N)
.

IV.4.8.
Condition
(LD-5)

The next result uses the finger move for its proof, which will be the topic of Part
V
.

Lemma IV.4.14
.

Suppose
(F,G)
satisfy
(LD-1)
–
(LD-4)
. Then.

•

If
k>2
, then
\xi
automatically satisfies
(LD-5)
;

•

If
k=2
, then there exists a regular homotopy
G_{\tau}
and a family of functions
F_{\tau}
, with
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
a regular double path, as well as a grim vector field
\widetilde{\xi}

for the Morse function
F_{2}
such that

–

\xi
satisfies
(LD-3)
,
(LD-4)
, and
(LD-5)
;

–

with
\widetilde{\eta}
the pullback of
\widetilde{\xi}
, the paths of death starting from
F\circ G
with
\eta
and the path
of death starting from
F_{2}\circ G_{2}
with
\widetilde{\eta}
are lax homotopic over
F_{2\tau}\circ G_{2\tau}
.

Proof.

Suppose first
k>2
. Then, by Lemma
II.6.5
applied to
p_{-}=G_{0}(q_{-})
and
p_{+}=G_{0}(q_{+})
, it follows
that
\dim\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})\cap\Omega[0]\cap F^{-1}(c)<0
. In particular, the only trajectory of
\xi
connecting

p_{-}
to
p_{+}
is the image of the trajectory
\gamma
of
\eta
connecting
G_{0}
. This means that
(LD-5)

is automatically satisfied.

From now on, suppose that
k=2
. Lemma
II.6.5
implies that the number of trajectories of
\xi

from
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
that lie in
\Omega\setminus G_{0}(N)
is finite. Let
\ell
be this number. If
\ell=0
, nothing needs to be done.

Assume
\ell>0
.
We first check that the properties
(LD-1)
—
(LD-4)
imply that the assumption of the Finger Move Theorem
IV.4.13
are satisfied. The vector field
\xi
is Morse–Smale as well as its pull-back
\widetilde{\eta}
.
The curve
\gamma
is mapped to the first stratum by
(LD-2)
, and the parts of the stable/unstable manifolds are of
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
,
that is,
\mathcal{K}_{-}
and
\mathcal{K}_{+}
, are mapped to the first stratum by
(LD-3)
. The assumption of the Finger Move Theorem
IV.4.13
on the lack of critical points is guaranteed by
(LD-4)
.

Apply the finger move. That is, by Theorem
IV.4.13
, we can create a path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,2]
,
and a new grim vector field
\overline{\xi}
for
F_{2}
. The path has
all the properties
(FM-1)
–
(FM-6)
.
By Lemma
V.5.3
,
\eta
is gradient-like for
F_{2}\circ G_{2}
. By Lemma
III.6.15

with
h_{\sigma,0}=F_{2\tau}\circ G_{2\tau}
, the
\eta
-paths
of death constructed from
F_{2}\circ G_{2}
and from
F\circ G
are lax homotopic over
F_{2\tau}\circ G_{2\tau}
. By
(FM-5)
,
the pull-back
\overline{\eta}
of
\overline{\xi}
via
G_{2}
has the property that the
\overline{\eta}
-path of death starting from
F_{2}\circ G_{2}

and the
\eta
-path of death starting from
F_{2}\circ G_{2}
are left-homotopic. That is, to say,
the
\overline{\eta}
path of death starting from
F_{2}\circ G_{2}
and the
\eta
-path of death starting from
F\circ G
are lax homotopic
over
F_{2\tau}\circ G_{2\tau}
.
Moreover, by
(FM-3)
,

\overline{\xi}
has
\ell-1
trajectories outside of
G_{2}(N)
connecting
p_{-}
with
p_{+}
. All this means that
(F,G)
satisfies condition
(LD-3)
.

Now, by
(FM-1)
,
F_{2}
acquires four critical points on the second stratum. We perform rearrangements, using
\overline{\xi}
as the guiding vector field, to move the four new critical points away
from the region
F_{2}^{-1}[a,b]
. The rearrangement is done as in Lemma
IV.4.11
. Call
(F_{3},G_{3})
the resulting function. As the rearrangement is done using the vector field
\overline{\xi}
,
and
\overline{\eta}
is a pull-back, the path
F_{2+\tau}\circ G_{2+\tau}
is an
\overline{\eta}
-path of excellent Morse functions. In particular, by Lemma
III.6.15
, the
\overline{\eta}
-paths of death starting with
F_{3}\circ G_{3}
and starting with
F_{2}\circ G_{2}
are lax homotopic over
F_{2+\tau}\circ G_{2+\tau}
. That is to say, the
\overline{\eta}
-path of death starting from
F_{3}\circ G_{3}
is lax homotopic over
F_{3\tau}\circ G_{3\tau}

to the original
path of death. Note that the pair
(F_{3},G_{3})
satisfies:

•

the condition
(LD-3)
, with
\overline{\xi}
being the corresponding grim vector field. This is because
(F_{2},G_{2})
already satisfied that condition;

•

the condition
(LD-4)
, because we just move all critical points that were created by the finger move away from
F_{2}^{-1}[a,b]
;

•

the conditions
(LD-1)
and
(LD-2)
as consequences of conditions
(LD-3)
and
(LD-4)
.

This places us in a position to use induction. Starting with
(F_{3},G_{3})
, we create further paths by a subsequent use of the finger move. More precisely, the
j
-th path
denoted by
(F_{3j-3+\tau},G_{3j-3+\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,3]
, is constructed using the finger move applied to
(F_{3j-3},G_{3j-3})

and the vector field
\widetilde{\xi}_{j}
, grim for
F_{3j-3}
, with
\widetilde{\xi}_{0}=\xi
,
\widetilde{\xi}_{1}=\overline{\xi}
.
The vector field
\widetilde{\xi}_{j}
has
\ell-j
trajectories connecting
p_{-}
with
p_{+}
. Moreover, the induction argument shows that the grim path
of death starting from
F_{3j-3}\circ G_{3j-3}
, and using the pull-back of
\widetilde{\xi}_{j}
, is lax homotopic to the original one over
F_{(3j-3)\tau}\circ G_{(3j-3)\tau}
.

Each of the vector fields
\widetilde{\xi}_{j}
satisfies
(LD-4)
. Eventually,

(F_{3\ell},G_{3\ell})
and
\widetilde{\xi}_{\ell}
satisfy
(LD-3)
,
(LD-4)
, and
(LD-5)
. We conclude the proof by reparametrizing the path to the interval
[0,1]
.
∎

IV.4.9.
The proof of the Death Lifting Lemma
IV.4.1

Let
F_{0}=F
and
G_{0}=G
. We set
f_{0}=F_{0}\circ G_{0}
,
\eta
a gradient-like vector field for
f_{0}
, and
f_{\tau}
an
\eta
-path of death cancelling
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
. The proof follows the pattern of the proof of the Rearrangement Lifting Lemma
IV.3.1
, but is technically more involved. The reader should keep in mind that throughout the proof a few new vector fields are constructed, like
\widetilde{\eta}
,
\eta^{\prime}
, and
\overline{\eta}
. The key property is that except for
\overline{\eta}
, all these vector fields agree with the original vector field
\eta
near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
, so that all paths of death created with these vector fields are lax-homotopic to the original one. The case of
\overline{\eta}
is a bit special, as it arises from the finger move, but its behaviour is controlled by
(FM-5)
of Finger Move Theorem
IV.4.13
. A schematic of the proof is presented in Figure
31
.

\mathcal{F}^{1}
f_{\tau}
\overline{\eta}
-path
\widetilde{f}_{5+\tau}
\widetilde{\eta}
-path
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
\widetilde{\eta}
-path
\widetilde{f}_{1+\tau}
\widetilde{\eta}

-path

(LD-1) — (LD-5)

(LD-1)

\begin{subarray}{c}\vphantom{2^{3}}\ (LD-1)\\

Figure 31.
A schematic of the proof of Death Lifting Lemma
IV.4.1
.

Lemma
IV.4.4
takes care of
(LD-1)
. It creates a path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})

that replaces
(F_{0},G_{0})
by a
(F_{1},G_{1})
(with
G_{\tau}=G_{0}
) and
(F_{1},G_{1})
satisfying
(LD-1)
.
Set
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}=F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}
. Clearly
\eta
need not be gradient-like for
\widetilde{f}_{1}
.
However, the arguments of Lemma
IV.3.34
from Subsection
IV.3.8
allow us to construct a vector field
\widetilde{\eta}
,
gradient like for
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
and such that
\widetilde{\eta}=\eta
near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
.

If
G_{1}
is an embedding (that is if
G_{0}
is),
(LD-3)
is already satisfied.
Otherwise, we create a path whose destination satisfies that condition. We choose a generic gradient-like vector field
\nabla F_{0}
and, if necessary, replace
G_{1}
by a path of immersions (denoted
G_{\tau}
in Subsection
IV.4.4
, but here we call it
G_{1+\tau}
, for
\tau\in[0,1]
, to avoid a notation clash), with
F_{1+\tau}\equiv F_{1}
such that the trajectory
\gamma
is in good position with respect to
G_{2}
and
F_{1+\tau}\circ G_{1+\tau}
is an
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of Morse functions. This is done in Lemma
IV.4.8
.

With this choice,
(F_{2},G_{2})
satisfies
(LD-2)
. However,
(F_{2},G_{2})
might acquire some critical points on deeper strata,
as the path
(F_{1+\tau},G_{1+\tau})
might potentially create extra self-intersections.
The key point is that no critical points at depth
0
are created, because
F_{1+\tau}
is constant. That is,
(F_{2},G_{2})
again satisfies

(LD-1)
.

Once
(LD-1)
and
(LD-2)
are satisfied, Lemma
IV.4.9
constructs a path of functions, which we here denote
(F_{2+\tau},G_{2+\tau})
, such that
F_{2+\tau}\circ G_{2+\tau}
is a
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of Morse functions. Moreover
(F_{3},G_{3})
satisfies
(LD-3)
.

Lemma
IV.4.10
applied to
(F_{3},G_{3},\widetilde{\eta})
constructs a vector field
\xi
, grim for
F_{3}
, such that the pull-back,
\eta^{\prime}

agrees with
\widetilde{\eta}
near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
. Note that
\eta^{\prime}
agrees also with the original vector field
\eta
in an open subset
containing
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
. In Lemma
IV.4.11
, we construct a path, which we now
denote
(F_{3+\tau},G_{3+\tau})
, that moves all critical points away from between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. This is a path
of rearrangement, such that
F_{3+\tau}\circ G_{3+\tau}
is an
\eta^{\prime}
path.

The resulting functions
(F_{4},G_{4})
satisfy conditions
(LD-3)
and
(LD-4)
, so they also satisfy
(LD-1)
(which is weaker than
(LD-4)
) and
(LD-2)
(which is weaker than
(LD-3)
).

We continue with the proof of Death Lifting Lemma
IV.4.1
by studying
\xi
in detail.
The vector field
\xi
might have more trajectories
connecting
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. We apply finger moves through Lemma
IV.4.14
applied to
(F,G)=(F_{4},G_{4})
, the vector field
\xi

and its pull-back
\eta^{\prime}
. We obtain a path, which we denote
(F_{4+\tau},G_{4+\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,1]
,
and a vector field
\overline{\xi}
, grim for
F_{5}
.

Lemma
IV.4.14
implies that
(F_{5},G_{5},\overline{\xi})
satisfies

(LD-4)
and
(LD-5)
, as well as all the previous conditions.
Lemma
IV.4.3
applied to that system of functions constructs a weak lift of
the
\overline{\eta}
path of death starting from
F_{5}\circ G_{5}
.
Denote it by
(F_{5+\tau},G_{5+\tau})
,
\tau\in[0,1]
. Set
\widetilde{f}_{5+\tau}:=F_{5+\tau}\circ G_{5+\tau}
.

Lemma IV.4.15
.

The path
\widetilde{f}_{5+\tau}
is lax-homotopic to the path
f_{\tau}
over
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,5]
.

Proof.

We make this proof in several steps.

•

An
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{1}
is lax homotopic to an
\eta
-path of death starting from
f_{0}=\widetilde{f}_{0}
over
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
(which is
f_{\tau}
) by Lemma
III.6.14
.

•

An
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{2}
is lax homotopic to an
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{1}
over
\widetilde{f}_{1+\tau}
by Lemma
III.6.15
.

•

An
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{3}
is lax homotopic to an
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{2}
over
\widetilde{f}_{2+\tau}
by Lemma
III.6.15
.

•

An
\eta^{\prime}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{4}
is lax homotopic to an
\widetilde{\eta}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{3}
over
\widetilde{f}_{3+\tau}
. Here we use Lemma
III.6.14
, because we change the vector field, however, we do not change it near
\mathcal{K}_{-}\cup\mathcal{K}_{+}
.

•

An
\overline{\eta}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{5}
is lax homotopic to an
\eta^{\prime}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{4}
over
\widetilde{f}_{4+\tau}
. This is a consequence of Lemma
IV.4.14
, which in turn relies on the property
(FM-5)
of the Finger Move Theorem
IV.4.13
.

•

By construction
\widetilde{f}_{5+\tau}
is a weak lift of an
\overline{\eta}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{5}
. As such,
\widetilde{f}_{5+\tau}
is left-homotopic to an
\overline{\eta}
-path of death starting from
\widetilde{f}_{5}
.

We now concatenate the homotopies. Eventually we get that
\widetilde{f}_{5+\tau}
is lax homotopic to
f_{\tau}
over
\widetilde{f}_{5\tau}
.
∎

Lemma
IV.4.15
will put us in a position to use Lemma
IV.1.6
. The following result is needed to check all the assumptions.

Lemma IV.4.16
.

The path
(F_{6\tau},G_{6\tau})
is a regular double path.

Proof.

The proof requires a case-by-case analysis of the components of the path.

•

The path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
changes only
F_{\tau}
, fixing
G_{\tau}=G_{0}
being a regular immersion. Moreover
F_{\tau}
is an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path, compare Lemma
IV.4.4
,
so
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
is a regular double path.

•

The path
(F_{1+\tau},G_{1+\tau})
created in Lemma
IV.4.8
is such that
F_{1+\tau}
is fixed, and
G_{1+\tau}
is a path of immersions (an
F_{1}
-regular path in the sense of Definition
III.1.20
), in particular
(F_{1+\tau},G_{1+\tau})
is
a regular double path.

•

Likewise, Lemma
IV.4.9
creates a regular double path
(F_{2+\tau},G_{2+\tau})
.

•

The path
(F_{3+\tau},G_{3+\tau})
is a path of rearrangments with
G_{3+\tau}=G_{3}
, see Lemma
IV.4.11
. That is, it is a regular double path.

•

Lemma
IV.4.14
creating the path
(F_{4+\tau},G_{4+\tau})
creates a regular double path.

•

The weak lift
(F_{5+\tau},G_{5+\tau})
is created using Lemma
IV.4.3
creating a regular double path, actually, that lemma does not change
G_{5+\tau}
, only
F_{5+\tau}
.

Concatenation of regular double paths is a regular double path.
∎

Give Lemmata
IV.4.15
and
IV.4.16
, we promote
(F_{6\tau},G_{6\tau})
to a weak lift of
f_{\tau}
using Lemma
IV.1.6
. This completes the proof of the Death Lifting Lemma
IV.4.1
.

IV.5.
The Path Lifting Theorem

We can now combine the results from Section
IV.1
on lifting
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-paths and paths of birth, rearrangement and death, to obtain the following result.

Theorem IV.5.1

(Path Lifting)
.

Let
N
be a closed manifold of dimension
n
and let
f_{\tau}
, for
\tau\in[0,1]
, be an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path of functions such that there are no rearrangements for which
a critical point of higher index goes below a critical point of a lower index.
Let
G_{0}\colon N\to\Omega
be a generic immersion, where
\Omega
is a compact manifold of dimension
n+k
. Suppose
F_{0}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is an immersed Morse function such that
F_{0}\circ G_{0}=f_{0}
.

If
k\geq 2
, then there exists a weak lift
(
Definition
IV.1.1
)
of
f_{\tau}
. That is, there exist:

•

a regular path
(
an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path
)

of functions
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\widetilde{f}_{0}=f_{0}
and
\widetilde{f}_{1}=f_{1}
and the paths
\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
and
f_{\tau}

are
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-homotopic;

•

a regular double path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
such that
F_{\tau}\circ G_{\tau}=\widetilde{f}_{\tau}
with the following properties for
G_{\tau}
.

(i)

If connected components of
N
have disjoint image under
G
, then this also holds for
G_{\tau}
for each
\tau\in[0,1]
.

(ii)

if
G_{0}
is an embedding and either
k\geq 3
or
f_{\tau}
has no deaths, then
G_{\tau}
is an ambient isotopy.

•

The family
F_{\tau}
has no births or deaths on the zeroth stratum, that is to say, for each
\tau\in[0,1]
,
F_{\tau}
is a classical Morse function when
regarded as a function from
\Omega
to
\mathbb{R}
, forgetting the stratification.

If
N
and
\Omega
are compact with nonempty boundary,

G_{0}
is a neat immersion and
f_{\tau}
is neat as a path of functions on
N
, while
F_{0}

is a neat function, then there is a neat regular double path
(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
satisfying all the required properties.

Proof.

Let
T
be the number of ‘events’ on
f_{\tau}
, where by an event we mean a birth, a rearrangement,
or a death.
As
f_{\tau}
is an
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path we make sure that at each of these events, the path
f_{\tau}
is an elementary path, respectively of birth, of rearrangement, or of death.
We may and shall assume that these events occur at distinct times.

We perform induction over
T
. If
T=0
, then
f_{\tau}
is a path of excellent Morse functions. We use Lemma
IV.1.2
.

Otherwise suppose that at
\tau_{0}
there appears one of the events: birth, death or rearrangement, and that
\tau_{0}>0
is minimal with this property.
Let
\delta>0
be such that the path
f_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[\tau_{0}-\delta,\tau_{0}+\delta]
is an elementary path. We split our path
f_{\tau}
into
three parts: the first part, for
\tau\in[0,\tau_{0}-\delta]
, is a
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path of Morse functions, which can be lifted using Lemma
IV.1.2
. For

\tau\in[\tau_{0}-\delta,\tau_{0}+\delta]
we lift the path using Lemma
IV.2.1
(if there is a birth at
\tau_{0}
), Lemma
IV.3.1
(if there
is a rearrangement at
\tau_{0}
), or Lemma
IV.4.1
(if there is a death at
\tau_{0}
). Note that if there is a rearrangement at
\tau_{0}
, a critical point with a smaller index can never go above a critical point of higher index by the assumptions of the theorem. Therefore,
the assumptions of Lemma
IV.3.1
are satisfied.

The path for
\tau\in[\tau_{0}+\delta,1]
has
T-1
events and we use the inductive hypothesis.

Now we provide an argument that the result works for
N
and
\Omega
with nonempty boundary. First, we perturb
f_{\tau}
to a
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-path that is neat.
This is done using methods from Subsection
III.1.6
. Therefore, we only need to prove that we can lift a neat path of Morse functions
to a neat path, and that we can lift an elementary path of neat functions to a neat path on
\Omega
.

The first step, that is, lifting a neat path of Morse functions to a neat path, is done by the last part of Lemma
IV.1.2
. Next,
we note that each kind of elementary path (births, rearrangements and deaths) is lifted in such a way that the initial Morse function
F_{0}

and the initial embedding
G_{0}
are unchanged away from a set of the form
F_{0}^{-1}(c,d)
: for example, for rearrangements, if
a
and
b

are critical values of points that are going to be rearranged, we set
c=a-\varepsilon
,
d=b+\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon\ll 1
. This means
that each time we lift an elementary path, we do not alter the function
F_{0}
near the boundary of
\Omega
, nor do we alter the function
G_{0}
near the boundary of
N
. Each time we lift an elementary path, we might need to shrink the neighbourhoods of
\bd N
and
\bd\Omega
, on which
G_{\tau}
, respectively
F_{\tau}
are independent of
\tau
, but we perform only finitely many such operations. Hence, the resulting regular double path

(F_{\tau},G_{\tau})
is neat.
∎

IV.6.
Path lifting for
N
having components of varying dimensions

Now we discuss the case that
N=N_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup N_{r}
is a union of components of dimensions
n_{1},\dots,n_{r}
, with
n_{i}\leq\dim\Omega-2
for all
i
. We suppose that
G_{0}(N_{i})\cap G_{0}(N_{j})=\emptyset
unless
i=j
. Set
k_{i}=\dim\Omega-\dim N_{i}
to be the codimension of
N_{i}
.

First, the notions of immersed Morse functions and of grim vector fields can be generalised in an obvious way. With the stratification
\Omega[d]
given by points at depth
d
(even if the connected components of
\Omega[d]
might have different codimensions), the local form of a grim vector field is still given by (
II.5.1
),
with the necessary adjustments of the range of indices of the
x
and
y
variables. The ascending and descending membranes of a critical point
p\in G_{0}(N_{i})
have dimensions governed by the
n_{i}
, the depth of
p
, and the codimension
k_{i}
. Existence of Morse functions and of grim vector fields is proved in this situation without changes.

In the Cancellation Theorem
III.4.1
we work with the image of a single connected component of
N
. Indeed, the two critical points that are to be cancelled are connected by a trajectory of the grim vector field that lies in the first stratum. That is, everything happens within a single connected component of
N
. The dimension restriction is not used.

The situation is different with the rearrangement theorem, where we might rearrange critical points belonging to different components. The key property we use is dimension counting. It is routine to check that if
k_{i}\geq 2
for all
i
, then critical points
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
with
\ind p_{-}\geq\ind p_{+}
can be rearranged regardless of their depth and of the dimension of the manifolds they belong to. One way to see this (and to avoid lengthy calculations) is that rearrangement is possible in codimension two, and the dimension of membranes at the same depth does not increase if the codimension
k_{i}
increases. Another way is to check that if
p_{-}\in N_{i}
and
p_{+}\in N_{j}
,
i\neq j
, then the only trajectories connecting
p_{-}
to
p_{+}
are necessarily in the zeroth stratum, since
G_{0}(N_{i})\cap G_{0}(N_{j})=\emptyset
. The calculation of dimensions of the membranes is straightforward.

We pass to a discussion of path lifting. Lifting of births is purely local. In general, we lift a path governed by a vector field
\eta
on
N

(an
\eta
-path of rearrangment or an
\eta
-path of death), this terminology is valid even if the components of
N
have different dimensions.
Going into technical details, the partial rearrangement, i.e. ensuring
(LR-1)
and
(LD-1)
, relies on dimension counting arguments. These arguments go through if the components have various dimensions (the same argument as in the previous paragraph applies). The most difficult case is still if
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
both belong to a single codimension two component.

Arranging for properties
(LR-2)
and
(LD-3)
to hold is one of the most sophisticated technical part of the proof of the Path Lifting Theorem. Recall that we use an extra gradient-like vector field, which we call
\nabla F_{0}
, and a special cut-off function
\mu
supported in a neighbourhood of the ascending manifold
\mathcal{K}_{-}
of
q_{-}
. The function
\mu
is nonzero only on the component of
N
containing
q_{-}
, that is, the whole discussion constructing
\mu
is independent of the dimensions of the components of
N
not containing
q_{-}
. The vector field
\nabla F_{0}
is constructed so as to avoid self-intersections of different components of
G_{0}(N)
.
This holds even if the
N_{i}
have different dimensions.

Finally, we need to discuss the finger move. It is used if the critical points
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
belonging to the
same
component of
N

are connected by a trajectory of a vector field
\xi
that lies in the zero stratum. As
q_{-}
and
q_{+}
belong to the same component, and the component has codimension two, the finger move concerns only that single connected component of
N
. Other components do not interfere, regardless of their dimension.

Part V
The finger move

Throughout Part
V
we assume that the codimension
k=2
.
Our work is focused on the image of
N
. Therefore, unlike in Part
IV
, we will mostly work on
M:=G_{0}(N)
.

Plan of proof of Theorem
IV.4.13
.

The proof of Theorem
IV.4.13
requires Sections
V.1
through
V.5
.
As a guide for the reader through the proof, we present the plan.

The first challenge is to find a suitable coordinate system.
The construction of this coordinate system is done in Sections
V.1
and
V.2
and consists of several steps. In fact,
the coordinate system we define
is not merely a local coordinate system near a critical point, but it is suitably extended along a “guiding curve.” In a sense,
this a semi-local coordinate system.

The actual finger move is described in Section
V.3
. This section begins with an explicit formula
for the finger move in Subsection
V.3.1
. Formally, this induces an isotopy
G_{1+\tau}
of Theorem
IV.4.13
.
One of the key properties is that
G_{1+\tau}
is level-set-preserving, that is
F\circ G_{1+\tau}=F\circ G_{1}=F\circ G_{0}
for all
\tau\in[0,1]
. Condition
(FM-6)
follows from the construction of
G_{\tau}
, and it is addressed at the end
of Subsection
V.3.1
.

In Subsection
V.3.2
we construct a suitable vector field
\widehat{\xi}
, which is tangent to
G_{2}(M)
. In Subsection
V.3.3
we prove
that
\partial_{\widehat{\xi}}F\geq 0
.
Already the vector field
\widehat{\xi}
satisfies the most
important property of Theorem
IV.4.13
, namely
(FM-3)
. However,
\widehat{\xi}
is not a grim vector field on
F
;
moreover,
F
is not an immersed Morse function for
G_{2}(N)
. To remedy this, in Subsection
V.3.4
we construct
an explicit perturbation
F_{\tau}
,
\tau\in[0,1]
, of
F
and an explicit perturbation of the vector field
\xi_{\tau}
and of
the function
F_{\tau}
.

The key property
(FM-3)
is proved in Section
V.4
as Theorem
V.4.1
with
two key lemmas (Lemma
V.2.3
and
V.2.5
) proved in Section
V.2
. Since
F_{1}
is a perturbation
of
F
, the proof of Theorem
V.4.1
holds both for
F
and
\widehat{\xi}
and for
F_{1}
and
\overline{\xi}
.

Finally, we study the pull-back of
\overline{\xi}
, denoted by
\overline{\eta}
, which is a vector field on
N
, gradient-like for
f_{2}=F_{2}\circ G_{2}
.
Properties
(FM-4)
and
(FM-5)
are proved in Section
V.5
.
By a careful analysis of the ascending and descending manifolds of
\overline{\eta}
, in the same section we show that the grim paths of death
constructed via
\overline{\eta}
and via
\eta
are homotopic.
∎

V.1.
The outer shell

As noted above, the proof of Finger Move Theorem
IV.4.13
begins with a construction of a suitable open subset with concrete coordinates, in which the finger move
will be performed. In fact,
we will construct three nested neighbourhoods of one of the critical points.

Subsections
V.1.1
through
V.1.5
construct the outer shell,
V_{\textrm{out}}
. The construction
is rather technical. For the reader who wants to skip the details, we summarise the properties of
V_{\textrm{out}}
in Subsection
V.1.6
.

V.1.1.
The set
V_{\textrm{out}}
.

In this section we construct the biggest (outer) neighbourhood of one of the critical points, which we will denote
V_{\textrm{out}}
.

We set

c_{-}=F(p_{-}),\ c_{+}=F(p_{+})

and we denote by
\Xi_{s}
the flow of the vector field
\xi
.

Suppose that there are no other critical points in
F^{-1}([c_{-},c_{+}])
. Note that this can always be arranged by the Global Rearrangement Theorem
III.2.4
.
Choose local coordinates around
p_{+}
given by
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2})
, so that
p_{+}
is given by
(0,\ldots,0)
,
M
is locally given by
\{y_{1}=y_{2}=0\}
,
and the grim vector field around
p_{+}
is given by
(-x_{1},\ldots,-x_{h+1},x_{h+2},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2},0)
.
Let
U_{\textrm{coor}}
be an open set containing
p_{+}
on which these coordinates are defined.
In these local coordinates
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
is given by

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})=\{x_{h+2}=\dots=x_{n}=y_{2}=0\}\cap\{y_{1}\leq 0\}.

Choose
c_{\textrm{bot}}\in(c_{-},c_{+})
such that for any
c\in[c_{\textrm{bot}},c_{+}]
, the intersection of the descending membrane
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
with the level set
F^{-1}(c)

is contained in the coordinate neighbourhood
U_{\textrm{coor}}
. Likewise, choose a regular value
c_{\textrm{top}}>c_{+}
in such a way that

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{+})\cap F^{-1}(c)\subseteq U_{\textrm{coor}}
for any
c\in[c_{+},c_{\textrm{top}}]
;
see Figure
32
.
It is clear that such
c_{\textrm{bot}}
and
c_{\textrm{top}}
exist.

The set
U_{\textrm{out}}
will be contained inside
