.
To define it choose
\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}>0
and let
Y_{\textrm{out}}
be an open tubular neighbourhood of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
in the level set
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})

consisting of points at distance less than
\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}
from
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
.
We will assume that
\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}>0
is small, and the precise meaning of ‘small’ in this context will be clarified soon. For now define
U_{\textrm{out}}
as

(V.1.1)

\overline{U}_{\textrm{out}}:=\overline{\bigcup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\Xi_{s}(Y_{\textrm{out}})}\cap F^{-1}[c_{\textrm{bot}},c_{\textrm{top}}].\ \ U_{\textrm{out}}=\Int\overline{U}_{\textrm{out}}.

That is,
U_{\textrm{out}}
is the grim neighbourhood of
p_{+}
in the sense of Definition
II.7.7
. Clearly,
\overline{U}_{\textrm{out}}
is the closure of
U_{\textrm{out}}
.

Lemma V.1.1
.

If
Y_{\textrm{out}}
is sufficiently thin
(
that is, if
\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}
is sufficiently small
)
,
then
U_{\textrm{out}}
is contained in the coordinate neighbourhood
U_{\textrm{coor}}
.

Proof.

Let
Y_{n}
be the set
Y_{\textrm{out}}
defined with
\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}=\frac{1}{n}
and let
U_{n}
be the corresponding set
U_{\textrm{out}}
(that is,

U_{n}
is constructed via (
V.1.1
) by taking
Y_{\textrm{out}}=Y_{n}
). By construction,
\overline{U}_{n+1}\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}},c_{\textrm{top}})\subseteq U_{n}
.
It follows from the proof of
Lemma
II.7.9
that
\bigcap U_{n}=(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{+})\cup\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+}))\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}},c_{\textrm{top}})
, so that
\bigcap U_{n}\subseteq U_{\textrm{coor}}
. Since
U_{\textrm{coor}}
is open, it follows
that for large
n
we must have
U_{n}\subseteq U_{\textrm{coor}}
as well.
∎

We have constructed the outer neighbourhood
U_{\textrm{out}}
starting from the neighbourhood
Y_{\textrm{out}}
.
We leave ourselves the possibility of shrinking
Y_{\textrm{out}}
further by decreasing
\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}
,
but henceforth the levels
c_{\textrm{bot}}
and
c_{\textrm{top}}
will remain fixed.

V.1.2.
The position of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})
.

By the Morse–Smale condition, the membranes
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
intersect transversely along a finite number of trajectories. Intersecting

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
with the level set
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
yields a finite number of points
z_{0},z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}
.
Here
z_{0}
is assumed to lie on
M
, that is
z_{0}
corresponds to the trajectory of
\xi
on
M
that connects
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
.

If
Y_{\textrm{out}}
is small enough, the intersection
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap Y_{\textrm{out}}
consists of
r+1
discs of dimension
n-h
.
In fact, each connected component of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap Y_{\textrm{out}}
that does not contain
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})\cap Y_{\textrm{out}}

is separated from it by some distance, and thus shrinking
Y_{\textrm{out}}
(decreasing
\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}
) we can make this component disjoint from
U_{\textrm{out}}
;
see Figure
33
.

The

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
, because
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})

and they intersect

. These discs can be isotoped in such a way that for

around

has the form

(V.1.2)

x_{1}=s_{1,k},\ldots,x_{h+1}=s_{h+1,k}\textrm{ for }k=1,\ldots,r,

for some constants
s_{i,\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
. For one of the points
z_{m}
, after applying the aforementioned isotopy, we can and will assume that all of the constants
s_{i,m}=0
, for
i=1,\dots,h+1
and that

s_{1,j}\neq 0
for all
j\neq m
.
The choice of
m
is arbitrary if
\dim\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})>1
(that is, if
h<n-1
); if
\dim\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
is 1-dimensional, then we take
m
to be the index of the point

nearest
to
M
on the guiding curve, see Subsection
V.1.2
below.

Given (
V.1.2
), the coordinates of the point
z_{\ell}
are

(V.1.3)

(s_{1,\ell},\ldots,s_{h+1,\ell},0,\ldots,0,c_{\textrm{bot}}-s_{1,\ell}^{2}-\ldots-s_{h+1,\ell}^{2},0).

We remark that the
y_{1}
coordinate of
z_{\ell}
is determined by the fact that
F(z_{\ell})=c_{\textrm{bot}}
. We also isotope the disc
D_{0}
within
M
in such a way that it is given by (
V.1.2
),
but with the extra conditions that the
y_{1}
coordinate of
z_{0}
is equal to
0
, and the
y_{2}
coordinate on
D_{0}

is either non-positive or non-negative.

According to the Isotopy Insertion Lemma
II.5.8
, the isotopy of the discs required to arrange the local description above of the intersection of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})
with
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
, can be obtained by locally altering the vector field
\xi
in the set

F^{-1}(c^{\prime},c_{\textrm{bot}})
, for some
c^{\prime}<c_{\textrm{bot}}
close to
c_{\textrm{bot}}
.
From now on we will assume that such an alteration has

).

V.1.3.
The guiding curve.

Consider the point
z_{m}
on the intersection
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, for which we have set
s_{1,m}=\ldots=s_{h+1,m}=0
.
Take an embedded curve
\varpi\colon[0,\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}]\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
given by

(V.1.4)

\varpi(s)=(0,\ldots,0,0,\ldots,0,0,s);

see Figure
34
, in which
m=4
. Since
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2}
is an orthonormal coordinate system and
Y_{\textrm{out}}
is the set
of points at distance less than or equal to
\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}

from
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, we have that
\varpi(\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}})\in\partial Y_{\textrm{out}}
.
Next, we want to extend
\varpi
to a map
\varpi\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
such that

\displaystyle\varpi((\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}},1])\cap Y_{\textrm{out}}

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

\displaystyle\varpi(1)

\displaystyle\in M\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\varpi(1)

\displaystyle\notin\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+}).

That is,
\varpi
is a path in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})
and in the level set
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, from an intersection point in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
, to a point on
M
in the boundary of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
;
see Figure
35
.
The curve
\varpi
will correspond to the guiding curve in Section
VI.3
.

The construction of
\varpi
is as follows. The set
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
is path connected. Its boundary, the unstable manifold of
p_{-}
, consists of at least two points. Precisely one point of the boundary
belongs to
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
: this point corresponds to the unique trajectory on
M
connecting
p_{-}
with
p_{+}
. There exists a point
z_{\textrm{end}}
on
\bd(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}}))
disjoint from
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
.
By path connectedness, we extend the curve
\varpi
to a curve in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
in such a way that
\varpi(1)=z_{\textrm{end}}
and
\varpi
is transverse to
M
at
z_{\textrm{end}}
.

It remains to show that
\varpi
can be chosen in such a way that

\varpi((\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}},1])\cap Y_{\textrm{out}}=\emptyset
. Note that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
intersects
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
along points
z_{0},\dots,z_{r}
and so
Y_{\textrm{out}}
intersects
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})

along open balls near these points. If
\dim\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})>1
, the complement
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})\setminus Y_{\textrm{out}}
is still path connected so the choice of
\varpi
does not pose
any problems.

If
\dim\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})=1
, though,
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
is an arc with end points
z_{0}
and
z_{\textrm{end}}
(there is only one possibility of chosing
z_{\textrm{end}}
). The set
Y_{\textrm{out}}
separates this arc.
In order that
\varpi
exists, we need to choose the point
z_{m}
, from which
\varpi
emerges, to be the
nearest
point (among
z_{1},\dots,z_{r}
) to
z_{\textrm{end}}
on
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, compare
Figure
34
, where
m=4
is the only possible choice. It might also happen that (
V.1.4
) defining
\varpi
points in the other direction, i.e. in Figure
34
, the curve from
z_{4}

goes towards
z_{3}
instead of
z_{\textrm{end}}
as the parameter
y_{2}
increases. If this happens, we globally replace
y_{2}
by
-y_{2}
. Note that
F
does not depend on
y_{2}
and
\xi
depends on
y_{2}
only via
y_{2}^{2}
, so this change takes one coordinate system to another one,
in which both
F
and
\xi
have the same shape.

Y_{\textrm{out}}
\mathbb{M}_{d}(p_{2})

.

We have constructed a guiding curve
\varpi
.
For technical reasons we need to extend
\varpi
slightly across the point
\varpi(1)
, so we will assume, for some
\varepsilon_{\gamma}>0
, that

\varpi\colon[0,1+\varepsilon_{\gamma}]\to F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})

is defined as a small extension of
\varpi\colon[0,1]\to F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, and that
\varpi
is transverse to
M
at
\varpi(1)
. This is why
\varpi
extends slightly beyond
M
in Figures
35
and
36
.

V.1.4.
Coordinates on a neighbourhood of the guiding curve.

Now we are going to extend the coordinate system from
U_{\textrm{out}}
to a neighbourhood of
\varpi
.
First the coordinate system will be constructed in the level set
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, then in later subsections it will be extended to
F^{-1}[c_{\textrm{bot}},c_{\textrm{top}}]
.

X_{\textrm{out}}
Y_{\textrm{out}}
\mathbb{M}_{d}(p_{2})
\mathbb{M}_{a}(p_{1})
z_{0}
z_{\textrm{end}}
\varpi
M

Figure 36.
The set
X_{\textrm{out}}
.

Choose a small neighbourhood
X_{\textrm{out}}
of
\varpi(\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2,1+\varepsilon_{\gamma})
in
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, shown in Figure
36
.
We will assume that
X_{\textrm{out}}
has a product structure, that is
X_{\textrm{out}}\cong\varpi(\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2,1+\varepsilon_{\gamma})\times D

for some
n
-dimensional disc
D
(
\Omega
is
(n+2)
-dimensional so level sets are
(n+1)
-dimensional) and that
X_{\textrm{out}}
intersects the manifold
M
away from the descending manifold of
p_{+}
. In the local coordinate system in
Y_{\textrm{out}}\cap X_{\textrm{out}}
, we will assume that
X_{\textrm{out}}
is given by

\{x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}\leq\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\textrm{out}}^{2},y_{2}\in(\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2,\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}})\}\cap Y_{\textrm{out}}
,
where
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\textrm{out}}\in(0,\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2)
is a real number.
Recall that
y_{1}
is fixed by being on the level set
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, and
y_{2}
changes along
\varpi
.

With respect to the product structure
X_{\textrm{out}}\cong\varpi\times D
,
we write
\partial X_{\textrm{out}}=D_{-}\cup R_{\textrm{tang}}\cup D_{+}
, where
D_{-}=\varpi(\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2)\times D
,
D_{+}=\varpi(1+\varepsilon_{\gamma})\times D
and

R_{\textrm{tang}}=\varpi\times\bd D
. Here
D_{-}
is given by
X_{\textrm{out}}\cap\{y_{2}=\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2\}\cap\{x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}\leq\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\textrm{out}}^{2}\}
.
As
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\textrm{out}}<\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2
we have that
D_{-}\subseteq Y_{\textrm{out}}
.

Choose an auxiliary nonvanishing vector field
\upsilon
on
X_{\textrm{out}}
such that the following hold:

(L-1)

The vector field
\upsilon
is tangent to
\varpi
and to
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
.

(L-2)

The vector field
\upsilon
is transverse to
D_{-}
and to
D_{+}
. Moreover
\upsilon
points into
X_{\textrm{out}}
on
D_{-}
and out of
X_{\textrm{out}}
on
D_{+}
.

(L-3)

The vector field
\upsilon
is tangent to
R_{\textrm{tang}}
.

(L-4)

For any
w\in X_{\textrm{out}}\setminus(D_{+}\cup D_{-})
, the trajectory of
\upsilon
through
w
hits
D_{+}
in the future and hits
D_{-}
in the past.

(L-5)

The time it takes the trajectory of
\upsilon
to go from
D_{-}
to
D_{+}
is equal to
1+\varepsilon_{\gamma}
.

(L-6)

In the local coordinates on
U_{\textrm{out}}\cap X_{\textrm{out}}
, the vector field
\upsilon
is given by
\frac{\partial\phantom{y}}{\partial y_{2}}
.

The existence of such a
\upsilon
is clear because
X_{\textrm{out}}
is homeomorphic to a disc. Notice that
(L-6)
is compatible with

(L-1)
—
(L-5)
.
Condition
(L-5)
can be obtained after all the other conditions by rescaling.
The flow of
\upsilon
allows us to extend the coordinate system from
U_{\textrm{out}}\cap X_{\textrm{out}}

to the whole of
X_{\textrm{out}}
.

Take a point
u\in X_{\textrm{out}}
and let
\theta
be the trajectory of
\upsilon
through
u
. Parameterise
\theta
as
\theta(s)
so
that
\theta(\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2)=w\in D_{-}
and
\theta^{\prime}(s)=\upsilon_{\theta(s)}
for all
s\in[\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2,1+\varepsilon_{\gamma})
.
Then, identifying
w
with its expression in coordinates in
U_{\textrm{out}}
, define the coordinates of
u
to be
w+(0,\ldots,0,0,s_{u})
, where
s_{u}

is the unique value of
s\in[\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}},1+\varepsilon_{\gamma})
such that
\theta(s_{u})=u
.

We conclude this subsection by making an assumption,
whose rôle is to simplify the formulae that will appear in the sequel.

Lemma V.1.2
.

By possibly altering the vector field
\xi
below the level set
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, we may assume that
the connected component of
M\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})\cap X_{\textrm{out}}
containing
\varpi(1)
is given by the set of equations
x_{1}=0
,
y_{2}=1
and

y_{1}=x_{1}^{2}+\dots+x_{h+1}^{2}-x_{h+2}^{2}-\ldots-x_{n}^{2}
.
It follows in particular that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})\cap X_{\textrm{out}}
is contained in
\{y_{2}\leq 1\}
.

Sketch of proof.

We know that
M\cap X_{\textrm{out}}\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})

is an
(n-1)
–dimensional
disc containing
(0,\ldots,0,0,1)
and also the equations

x_{1}=0,y_{2}=1
,
y_{1}=x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{h+1}^{2}-x_{h+2}^{2}-\dots-x_{n}^{2}
define an
(n-1)
–dimensional disc in
X_{1}
containing
(0,\ldots,0,0,1)
. These
two discs are isotopic,
so by an isotopy of
M
we can achieve that they coincide.
Such an isotopy forces a change in
\xi
below the level set of
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, by the Isotopy Insertion Lemma
II.5.8
; compare Section
V.1.2
.
∎

V.1.5.
Flowing the guiding curve to different level sets.

Recall that we defined
\Xi_{s}
to be the flow of
\xi
and define

\overline{Z}_{\textrm{out}}:=\bigcup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\Xi_{s}(\overline{X}_{\textrm{out}})\cap F^{-1}[c_{\textrm{bot}},c_{\textrm{top}}],\ \ Z_{\textrm{out}}=\Int\overline{Z}_{\textrm{out}}.

Then set

V_{\textrm{out}}:=U_{\textrm{out}}\cup Z_{\textrm{out}}.

p_{2}
\varpi
M
Z_{\textrm{out}}
U_{\textrm{out}}

Figure 38.
The sets
Z_{\textrm{out}}
and
U_{\textrm{out}}
.

Using the flow of
\xi
, we can extend the coordinate system from
U_{\textrm{out}}\cup X_{\textrm{out}}
to
V_{\textrm{out}}
. Note that the fact that
\varpi(1)
does not belong
to the descending membrane of
p_{+}
implies that
Z_{\textrm{out}}
is a product
X_{\textrm{out}}\times(c_{\textrm{bot}},c_{\textrm{top}})

and the vector field
\xi
does not vanish on
Z_{\textrm{out}}
.

Remark
.

This is a place where we assume that there are no critical points of
F
other than
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. Otherwise, we should strive
to show that there are no trajectories connecting
\overline{X}_{\textrm{out}}
with a critical point.

Recall that
U_{\textrm{out}}
is a neighbourhood of the critical point
p_{+}
, whereas
X_{\textrm{out}}
, a neighbourhood of the part of
\varpi
that is outside
U_{\textrm{out}}
, is a subset of the level set
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
; see Figure
38
.

We want to extend our coordinate system to
Z_{\textrm{out}}
.
The extension on
Z_{\textrm{out}}
is given by the requirement that
the vector field
\xi
is given by

(V.1.5)

(-x_{1},\ldots,-x_{h+1},x_{h+2},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2},0).

The procedure for extending the coordinate system is analogous to extending the coordinate system to
X_{\textrm{out}}
using the
auxiliary vector field
\upsilon
,
which was described above.

Our aim is to show that in the new coordinate system
F
is given by

-x_{1}^{2}-\dots-x_{h+1}^{2}+x_{h+2}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2}+y_{1}
(up to a constant).
In order to obtain this condition and (
V.1.5
) at the same time, we need to rescale
\xi

by a positive factor. The procedure is as follows.

Suppose
\phi\colon Z_{\textrm{out}}\to(0,\infty)
is a smooth function
and consider the vector field
\phi\xi
.
Take a point
u\in Z_{\textrm{out}}
.
Suppose there is a trajectory
\theta_{\phi}
of
\phi\cdot\xi
such that
\theta_{\phi}(0)=u^{\prime}\in X_{\textrm{out}}
. Suppose

u^{\prime}
has coordinates
(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{2})
.
For
s\geq 0
, we set the coordinates of
\theta_{\phi}(s)
to be

(x_{1}^{\phi},\dots,y_{2}^{\phi})
, where

(V.1.6)

\begin{split}x_{i}^{\phi}&=e^{-s}x^{\prime}_{i}\textrm{ for }i\leq h+1,\ \ x_{i}^{\phi}=e^{s}x^{\prime}_{i}\textrm{ for }i>h+1,\\
y_{1}^{\phi}&=y^{\prime}_{2}\tan(sy^{\prime}_{2}+\arctan(y^{\prime}_{1}/y^{\prime}_{2})),\ \ y_{2}^{\phi}=y_{2}^{\prime}.\end{split}

For simplicity, we do not write explicitly the dependence of

x_{1}^{\phi},\dots,y_{2}^{\phi}
on the variable
s
.
In these coordinates, the vector field
\phi\xi
has the form

(V.1.7)

(-x_{1}^{\phi},\ldots,-x_{h+1}^{\phi},x_{h+2}^{\phi},\ldots,x_{n}^{\phi},(y_{1}^{\phi})^{2}+(y_{2}^{\phi})^{2},0).

To justify this, one must think of
(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{2})
as constants, and differentiate
x_{i}^{\phi}
and
y_{i}^{\phi}
with respect to the variable
s
. This yields:

\displaystyle\frac{{d}x_{i}^{\phi}}{{d}s}

\displaystyle=\frac{{d}}{{d}s}(-e^{-s}x_{i}^{\prime})=-x_{i}^{\phi},\text{ for }i\leq h+1,

\displaystyle\frac{{d}x_{i}^{\phi}}{{d}s}

\displaystyle=\frac{{d}}{{d}s}(e^{s}x_{i}^{\prime})=x_{i}^{\phi}\text{ for }i>h+1,

\displaystyle\frac{{d}y_{1}^{\phi}}{{d}s}

\displaystyle=(y_{2}^{\prime})^{2}\sec^{2}\big(sy_{2}^{\prime}+\arctan(y_{1}^{\prime}/y_{2}^{\prime})\big)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(y_{2}^{\prime})^{2}\big(1+\tan^{2}\big(sy_{2}^{\prime}+\arctan(y_{1}^{\prime}/y_{2}^{\prime})\big)\big)=(y_{2}^{\prime})^{2}+(y_{1}^{\prime})^{2}.

Also note that at
s=0
we recover
x_{i}^{\phi}=x_{i}^{\prime}
and
y_{i}^{\phi}=y_{i}^{\prime}
. Therefore the path
s\mapsto(x_{1}^{\phi},\dots,x_{n}^{\phi},y_{1}^{\phi},y_{2}^{\phi})

is the trajectory of the vector field (
V.1.5
), but it is also, by the definition, the trajectory
\theta_{\phi}(s)
of
\phi\xi
. Therefore, indeed,
\phi\xi

is given by (
V.1.5
).

The next result adjusts the function
\phi
in such a way that
F
has the desired form in the new coordinates.

Lemma V.1.3
.

There exists a unique choice of smooth function
\phi\colon Z_{\textrm{out}}\to(0,\infty)
such that, in the coordinate system
(x_{1}^{\phi},\dots,y_{2}^{\phi})
,
F
has the form

(V.1.8)

F=-(x_{1}^{\phi})^{2}-\ldots-(x_{h+1}^{\phi})^{2}+(x_{h+2}^{\phi})^{2}+\dots+(x_{n}^{\phi})^{2}+(y_{1}^{\phi})+c_{+},

where we recall that
c_{+}=F(p_{+})
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, whenever we talk of a trajectory of a vector field (usually
\xi
or
\phi\xi
), we mean
the part of the trajectory that stays in
Z_{\textrm{out}}
.

Choose a point
u^{\prime}\in X_{\textrm{out}}
, whose coordinates are

x_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{n}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}
. Notice that
y_{2}^{\prime}\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{out}}/2>0
. Define
an abstract function
\nu\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\nu(s)=-e^{-2s}({x^{\prime}_{1}}^{2}+\ldots+{x^{\prime}_{h+1}}^{2})+e^{2s}({x^{\prime}_{h+2}}^{2}+\ldots+{x^{\prime}_{n}}^{2})+y_{2}^{\prime}\tan(s{y_{2}^{\prime}}+\arctan(y_{1}^{\prime}/y_{2}^{\prime})).

Observe that this is always an increasing function.

Let
\theta
be the trajectory of
\xi
such that
\theta(0)=u^{\prime}
.
The functions
s\mapsto F(\theta(s))
and
s\mapsto\nu(s)
are
both smooth, strictly increasing functions of a real positive argument that agree at
s=0
and
\nu
is unbounded. By one-dimensional
inverse function theorem, there exists an increasing function
\psi\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\psi(0)=0
such
that

(V.1.9)

F(\theta(\psi(s)))=\nu(s).

Note that the function
\psi
depends implicitly on the choice of
u^{\prime}
. The formula

(V.1.10)

\phi(\theta(\psi(s)))=\frac{d}{ds}\psi(s)

defines the value of
\phi
on the trajectory of
\xi
through
u^{\prime}
.
Different choices of initial points
u^{\prime}
allow us
to define
\phi
on the whole of
Z_{\textrm{out}}
. With this definition, a straightforward argument involving
smooth dependence of solutions on intial conditions, shows that
\phi
is a smooth function on the whole of
Z_{\textrm{out}}
.

We claim that with the choice of
\phi
from (
V.1.10
)

(V.1.11)

\theta_{\phi}(s)=\theta(\psi(s)).

To see this, notice that the two functions are equal at
s=0
. We calculate the differentials of both sides. By the definition of
\theta_{\phi}
:

(V.1.12)

\frac{d}{ds}\theta_{\phi}(s)=\phi(\theta_{\phi}(s))\xi(\theta_{\phi}(s))

On the other hand:

(V.1.13)

\begin{split}\frac{d}{ds}\theta(\psi(s))=&\frac{d}{ds}\psi(s)\theta^{\prime}_{s}(\psi(s))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}\phi(\theta(\psi(s)))\theta^{\prime}(\psi(s))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(**)}}{{=}}\\
&=\phi(\theta(\psi(s)))\xi(\theta(\psi(s))).\end{split}

Here (*) is precisely (
V.1.10
), whereas (**) holds because
s\mapsto\theta
is the trajectory of
\xi
.
Therefore the two functions,
\theta_{\phi}(s)
and
\theta(\psi(s))
satisfy
the same differental equation

(V.1.14)

\frac{d}{ds}y(s)=\phi(y(s))\xi(y(s))

with the same initial condition.
Hence, they are equal. Note that the differential equation (
V.1.14
) is exactly
the flow of the vector field
\phi\xi
.

Combining (
V.1.9
) with (
V.1.11
) we see that

F(\theta_{\phi}(s))=\nu(s).

With the choice of coordinate system as in (
V.1.6
) we note that
F
has the desired form.
∎

By construction, the function
\phi
is uniquely determined on
Z_{\textrm{out}}
, in particular
\phi\equiv 1
on
U_{\textrm{out}}\cap X_{\textrm{out}}
.
We extend
\phi
by
1
to the whole of
X_{\textrm{out}}
and to a smooth positive function on the whole of
\Omega
(which we take equal to
1
away from
a small neighbourhood of
Z_{\textrm{out}}\cap X_{\textrm{out}}
). We rescale now
\xi
to
\phi\xi
; this preserves all the membranes.
We also drop superscript
\phi
from the coordinates
x_{1},\dots,y_{2}
.
By Lemma
V.1.3

the function
F
has the form (
V.1.8
) on the whole of
Z_{\textrm{out}}
.

We have now completed the definition of the set
V_{\textrm{out}}
and the coordinate
system on it. In what follows, we will refer to
V_{\textrm{out}}
as the
outer shell
.

V.1.6.
Properties of the outer shell

Before we pass to the construction of
V_{\textrm{mid}}
and
V_{\textrm{inn}}
, we sum up the construction of
V_{\textrm{out}}
.
It is an open subset of
\Omega
, endowed with a coordinate system
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2})
, such that the following holds.

(V-1)

The vector field
\xi
is given by
(-x_{1},\ldots,-x_{h+1},x_{h+2},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2},0)
. In particular

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})
is given by
\{x_{h+2}=\ldots=x_{n}=0,y_{2}=0,y_{1}\leq 0\}
.

(V-2)

The manifold
M
is given by
\{x_{1}=0,y_{2}=1\}\cup\{y_{1}=0,y_{2}=0\}
. The second component contains the critical
point
p_{+}
with coordinates
(0,\ldots,0)
.

(V-3)

The function
F
is given by
-x_{1}^{2}-\ldots-x_{h+1}^{2}+x_{h+2}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2}+y_{1}+c_{+}
.

(V-4)

The set
V_{\textrm{out}}
is a grim neighbourhood of
p_{+}
; in particular the entrance set of
V_{\textrm{out}}
is contained
in
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
and the exit set belongs to
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{top}})
.

V.2.
The middle and the inner shells

Y_{\textrm{out}}
V_{out}
V_{\textrm{mid}}
D_{0}
D_{1}
D_{2}
D_{m}
D_{4}
D_{3}
C_{\textrm{safe}}
\mathbb{M}_{a}(p_{2})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})

Figure 39.
The middle shell.

V.2.1.
Definining the middle shell

The middle and inner shells
V_{\textrm{mid}}
and
V_{\textrm{inn}}
are constructed as open subsets satisfying
\overline{V}_{\textrm{inn}}\subseteq V_{\textrm{mid}}
,
V_{\textrm{mid}}\subseteq V_{\textrm{out}}
.
The objective of defining
V_{\textrm{inn}}
and
V_{\textrm{mid}}

is that the finger move will be done inside
V_{\textrm{inn}}
, and the vector field
\xi
will be changed inside
V_{\textrm{mid}}
. The coordinate system
was defined on a larger set
V_{\textrm{out}}
so that we can control all the trajectories of
\xi
reaching
p_{+}
.

Set

V_{\textrm{mid}}:=\{(x,y)\in V_{\textrm{out}}\colon|x_{1}|<\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}\};

see Figure
39
.
The main objective will be to fix the quantity
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
. Before we do this, let us denote.

\overline{Y}_{\textrm{mid}}:=\overline{V}_{\textrm{mid}}\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}}),\ \ Y_{\textrm{mid}}=\Int Y_{\textrm{mid}},

where the interior is taken in the level set
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
; see Figure
40
.

Z_{\textrm{out}}
Y_{\textrm{mid}}
Y_{\textrm{out}}
\mathbb{M}_{d}(p_{2})
M

Figure 40.
The set
Y_{\textrm{mid}}:=V_{\textrm{mid}}\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
.

As a first step towards adjusting the value of
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
, we prove the following result.

Lemma V.2.1
.

There exists
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}^{0}>0
such that if
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}<\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}^{0}
, the middle set
the set
Y_{\textrm{mid}}
is disjoint from the discs
D_{i}
if
i\neq m
; see Figure
40
.

Proof.

By (
V.1.2
),
the disc
D_{i}
is contained in
\{x_{1}=s_{i,1}\}
, and for
i\neq m
,
s_{i,1}\neq 0
. We take
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}<|s_{i,1}|
for all
i\neq m
.
∎

The set
V_{\textrm{mid}}
is definitely not a grim neighbourhood of
V_{\textrm{mid}}\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
, because it does
not contain the whole of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{d}}(p_{+})\cap F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
. However,
if we restrict to the subspace
\{x_{1}=0\}
, then
V_{\textrm{mid}}
turns out to be a grim neighbourhood of
p_{+}
.
We record this property for future use.

Lemma V.2.2
.

The set
V_{\textrm{mid}}\cap\{x_{1}=0\}
is a grim neighbourhood of
p_{+}
in
V_{\textrm{out}}\cap\{x_{1}=0\}
.

Proof.

By definition we have
V_{\textrm{mid}}\cap\{x_{1}=0\}=V_{\textrm{out}}\cap\{x_{1}=0\}
. The set
V_{\textrm{out}}
is the grim neighbourhood of
p_{+}
, see
(V-4)
.
The hypersurface
\{x_{1}=0\}
is preserved by
\xi
. The statement follows quickly.
∎

V.2.2.
Trajectories starting from
D_{i}

The choice of
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
is determined by the control of trajectories starting at
D_{i}
and coming close to
p_{+}
. We will consider
two subsets
C_{\textrm{safe}}
and
C_{\textrm{vs}}
(‘vs’ standing for ‘very safe’) and shrink
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
in such a way that any trajectory from
D_{i}
enters
V_{\textrm{mid}}
through
C_{\textrm{vs}}

and, if it exits
C_{\textrm{safe}}
later on, it does it above the level set
c_{+}
. The finger move will not alter the vector field outside
of
V_{\textrm{mid}}\setminus C_{\textrm{safe}}
. This means that the finger move will not create any new trajectories from
D_{i}
to
p_{+}
; compare Theorem
V.4.1
.

To begin with, let
\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}},\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}
be two positive numbers with
\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}>\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}
. Define

(V.2.1)

\displaystyle C_{\textrm{safe}}

\displaystyle=\{x_{2}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}+y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}^{2},x_{1}\in[-\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}},\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}]\}

(V.2.2)

\displaystyle C_{\textrm{vs}}

\displaystyle=\{x_{2}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}+y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2},x_{1}\in[-\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}},\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}]\}.

The purpose of Subsection
V.2.2
is to prove the following two results.
The first one tells, that we can find a sufficiently thin neighbourhood of
\{x_{1}=0\}
(controlled by the parameter
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
)
a neighbourhood
C_{\textrm{vs}}
of the critical point, such that if a trajectory starts from a point
D_{i}
and hits
V_{\textrm{mid}}
,
then either it hits
C_{\textrm{vs}}
(which is unavoidable, since there are trajectories of
\xi
from
D_{i}
to
p_{+}
),
or it enters
V_{\textrm{mid}}
at the level set, where it has no chance to hit
p_{+}
afterwards.

Lemma V.2.3
.

There exists
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}^{0}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}<\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}^{0}
there exists
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{0}>0
with the property that if
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}<\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{0}
, then the following holds.

If
w_{1}\in D_{i}
,
i\neq m
and the trajectory of
\xi
through
w_{1}

enters
V_{\textrm{mid}}
through
w_{2}\in\partial V_{\textrm{mid}}

(
we require that
w_{2}
is the point of the first entrance of the trajectory to
V_{\textrm{mid}}
after it hits
w_{1})
, then:

(a)

either
w_{2}\in C_{\textrm{vs}}
;

(b)

or
F(w_{2})>c_{+}.

Proof.

Choose a point
w_{1}\in D_{i}
. Write its coordinates as

w_{1}=(s_{i,1},\ldots,s_{i,h+1},x_{h+2},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1,1},y_{2}),

where

(V.2.3)

y_{1,1}=c_{\textrm{bot}}-c_{+}+s_{i,1}^{2}+\cdots+s_{i,h+1}^{2}-x_{h+2}^{2}-\cdots x_{n}^{2};

see (
V.1.2
) and (
V.1.3
).
Denote

\Delta_{-}=s_{i,1}^{2}+\cdots+s_{i,h+1}^{2},\ \ \Delta_{+}=x_{h+2}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}.

Note that
\Delta_{-}
is fixed for all the points in
D_{i}
, while
\Delta_{+}
can vary. There is
a uniform upper bound on
\Delta_{-}
and
\Delta_{+}
on the whole of
D_{0}\cup\dots\cup D_{r}\setminus D_{m}
.

Let
\gamma(s)
be the trajectory of
\xi
such that
\gamma(0)=w_{1}
.
If
\gamma
leads out of
V_{\textrm{out}}
without
hitting
V_{\textrm{mid}}
, then, by
(V-4)
, it leads out at the level set
c_{\textrm{top}}
, so it never reaches
p_{+}
; in that case there
is nothing to prove. So assume
\gamma
reaches
V_{\textrm{mid}}
and let

t_{\textrm{hit}}
be the time of the first entrance of
\gamma
into
V_{\textrm{mid}}
. Set
w_{2}=\gamma(t_{\textrm{hit}})\in\partial\overline{V_{\textrm{mid}}}
.

Given
(V-1)
, the coordinates of
w_{2}
are calculated as follows,

(V.2.4)

w_{2}=\gamma(t_{\textrm{hit}})=(e^{-t_{\textrm{hit}}}s_{i,1},\ldots,e^{-t_{\textrm{hit}}}s_{i,h+1},e^{t_{\textrm{hit}}}x_{i,h+2},\ldots,e^{t_{\textrm{hit}}}x_{i,n},y_{1}(t_{\textrm{hit}}),y_{2}).

where
y_{1}(t_{\textrm{hit}})
can also be determined, compare (
V.1.6
), but we do not use the explicit formula here.

By the definition of
V_{\textrm{mid}}
, the
x_{1}
-coordinate of
w_{2}
is

\pm\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
. Therefore

t_{\textrm{hit}}=\ln\frac{|s_{i,1}|}{\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}}.

Note that by making
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
sufficiently small, we can make
t_{\textrm{hit}}
as large as we please.

Using (
V.2.4
) and
(V-3)
, we compute

(V.2.5)

F(w_{2})=-e^{-2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{-}+e^{2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{+}+y_{1}(t_{\textrm{hit}})+c_{+}.

In the following estimate we recall
t_{\textrm{hit}}
is a function of
w_{1}
, but
t_{\textrm{hit}}
can be made uniformly large for all
w_{1}\in D_{i}
(and for all
indices
i\neq m
) by choosing sufficiently small

\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}

Lemma V.2.4
.

For any
\delta>0
there exists
t_{0}>0
such that for every
w_{1}\in D_{i}
, if
t_{\textrm{hit}}=t_{\textrm{hit}}(w_{1})>t_{0}
, then
y_{1}(t_{\textrm{hit}})>-\delta-\Delta_{+}
.

Proof.

The proof relies on explicit calculations. While we could use the formula as in (
V.1.6
) for
y_{1}
,
we prefer to use more legible, but more rough, estimates.
Let
\upsilon(s)
be the
y_{1}
-coordinate of the point
\gamma(s)
. Note that
\upsilon(s)
satisfies:

(V.2.6)

\begin{split}\frac{d}{ds}\upsilon&=\upsilon^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\\
\upsilon(0)&=c_{\textrm{bot}}-c_{+}+\Delta_{-}-\Delta_{+}.\end{split}

The first equation holds because the
y_{1}
coordinate of
\xi
is
y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}
. The second holds by (
V.2.3
).
We have
\frac{d}{ds}\upsilon(s)\geq\upsilon^{2}
. Let
\upsilon_{0}(s)
be the solution to the ODE
\frac{d}{ds}\upsilon_{0}(s)=\upsilon_{0}^{2}
with initial condition
\upsilon_{0}(0)=\upsilon(0).

That is,

\upsilon_{0}(s)=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\upsilon(0)}-s}.

Gronwall’s inequality from the classical theory of ODEs implies that
\upsilon(s)\geq\upsilon_{0}(s)
for
s\geq 0
, so

\upsilon(t_{\textrm{hit}})\geq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\upsilon(0)}-t_{\textrm{hit}}}.

Now
\frac{1}{\frac{1}{c_{\textrm{bot}}-c_{+}+\Delta_{-}-\Delta_{+}}-t_{\textrm{hit}}}
tends to zero as
t_{\textrm{hit}}\to\infty
. Hence, for sufficiently large
t_{\textrm{hit}}
we have
\upsilon(t_{\textrm{hit}})\geq-\delta-\Delta_{+}
.
∎

Resuming the proof of Lemma
V.2.3
,
choose
\delta<\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/10
and adjust
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
according to Lemma
V.2.4
. Recall that
\Delta_{-}
is constant on
D_{i}
and, making
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
smaller if necessary,
choose
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
in such a way that
e^{-2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{-}<\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/10
as well.
Then (
V.2.5
) implies that

(V.2.7)

\displaystyle F(w_{2})=-e^{-2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{-}+e^{2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{+}+y_{1}(t_{\textrm{hit}})+c_{+}

\displaystyle\geq-e^{-2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{-}+e^{2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{+}-\delta-\Delta_{+}+c_{+}

\displaystyle\geq(e^{2t_{\textrm{hit}}}-1)\Delta_{+}+c_{+}-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/5.

Suppose
w_{2}\notin C_{\textrm{vs}}
.
Recall that
C_{\textrm{vs}}
was given by (
V.2.2
), that is

|x_{1}|\leq\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
and

x_{2}^{2}+\dots+y_{2}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}
.
If
w_{2}\notin C_{\textrm{vs}}
, by
(
V.2.4
), we obtain
the following conditions:

(V.2.8)

\left|e^{-t_{\textrm{hit}}}s_{i,1}\right|>\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}},\textrm{ or }e^{-2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{-}+e^{2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{+}+y_{1}(t_{\textrm{hit}})^{2}+y_{2}^{2}>\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}.

However, we know that
w_{2}
is on the boundary of
V_{\textrm{mid}}
,
so
|e^{-t_{\textrm{hit}}}s_{i,1}|=\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
. Therefore, the second inequality
in (
V.2.8
) has to hold.
There are four terms on the left-hand side of the second inequality,
so at least one of them is greater than one fourth of the right-hand side.
We will list relevant cases and try to show that either
there is an immediate contradiction (using the fact that
t_{\textrm{hit}}
is large), or that
F(w_{2})>c_{+}
.
These cases are:

•

e^{-2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{-}>\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/4
. This is impossible if
t_{\textrm{hit}}>s_{0}
for some fixed
s_{0}
, which can be made uniform
for all
w_{1}
.

•

e^{2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{+}>\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/4
. Then
(e^{2t_{\textrm{hit}}}-1)\Delta_{+}>\frac{2}{9}\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}
if
t_{\textrm{hit}}
is large,
and so by (
V.2.7
) we conclude that
F(w_{2})>c_{+}
.

•

y_{1}(t_{\textrm{hit}})<-\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/4}=-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}/2
. But then by Lemma
V.2.4
we infer that
-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}/2>-\delta-\Delta_{+}>-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/10-\Delta_{+}
, which implies that
\Delta_{+}\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}/2-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/10
. This
is greater than
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/4
if
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}
is small. We reduce to the previous case.

•

y_{1}(t_{\textrm{hit}})>\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/4}=\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}/2
and
e^{-2t_{\textrm{hit}}}\Delta_{-}<\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/4
.
Then by (
V.2.5
) we obtain that
F(w_{2})\geq c_{+}+\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}/2-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/10>c_{+}
, again provided
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}
is small.

•

y_{2}^{2}(t_{\textrm{hit}})\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/4
. The
y_{2}
-coordinate is constant along the trajectory of
\xi
, hence the
y_{1}
coordinate of
\gamma(s)

satisfies the inequality

\frac{d}{ds}\upsilon(s)\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/4
(compare (
V.2.6
))
hence
\upsilon(t_{\textrm{hit}})\geq\upsilon(0)+t_{\textrm{hit}}\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}/4
. With
t_{\textrm{hit}}
sufficiently large we arrive
at the previous case, that is
y_{1}(t_{\textrm{hit}})>\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}/2
.

We have assumed that
w_{2}
is not contained in
C_{\textrm{vs}}
, and have shown that
F(w_{2})>c_{+}
, as desired.
∎

The second result after Lemma
V.2.3
also adjusts the value of
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
, but with respect to another parameter.
We fix a neighbourhood
C_{\textrm{safe}}
as above, whose size is controlled by a parameter
\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}
. We prove we can choose
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
small enough
that if a trajectory enters
C_{\textrm{vs}}
then on exiting the larger set
C_{\textrm{safe}}
, it is at a level set above
p_{+}
, so that it has
no chances to reach
p_{+}
in the future.

Lemma V.2.5
.

For any
\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}>0
, there exists
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{0}>0
such that if
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}<\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{0}
, then
if a trajectory of
\xi
enters
V_{\textrm{mid}}
through
w_{2}\in C_{\textrm{vs}}
, and exits
C_{\textrm{safe}}
at a point
w_{3}
,
then
F(w_{3})>c_{+}
.

Proof.

We let the coordinates of
w_{2}
be

w_{2}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},y_{2}).

We also set

\Delta_{-}=x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{h+1}^{2},\ \Delta_{+}=x_{h+2}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}.

As
w_{2}\in C_{\textrm{vs}}
we have that

(V.2.9)

\Delta_{-},\,\Delta_{+},\,y_{1}^{2},\,y_{2}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2},\ \ |x_{1}|<\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}.

The trajectory of
\xi
through
w_{2}
leaves
C_{\textrm{safe}}
.
We let
s_{0}
be the time after which this trajectory leaves
C_{\textrm{safe}}
for
the first time and let
w_{3}
be the point at which this trajectory leaves
C_{\textrm{safe}}
.
The coordinates of
w_{3}
are clearly

(V.2.10)

w_{3}=(e^{-s_{0}}x_{1},\ldots,e^{-s_{0}}x_{h+1},e^{s_{0}}x_{h+2},\ldots,e^{s_{0}}x_{n},\widetilde{y}_{1},y_{2}).

Here,
\widetilde{y}_{1}
is the
y_{1}
-coordinate of
w_{3}
.
We have
\widetilde{y}_{1}\geq y_{1}
and
y_{1}\geq-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}
by (
V.2.9
), so

(V.2.11)

\widetilde{y_{1}}\geq-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}.

By property
(V-3)
combined with (
V.2.10
):

(V.2.12)

F(w_{3})-c_{+}=e^{2s_{0}}\Delta_{+}-e^{-2s_{0}}\Delta_{-}+\widetilde{y}_{1}.

In (
V.2.1
)
C_{\textrm{safe}}
is given by two conditions:
|x_{1}|\leq\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
,
x_{2}^{2}+\dots+\dots+y_{2}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}^{2}
.
As
w_{3}\in\partial C_{\textrm{safe}}
, one of the two inequalities must be equality.
We argue as in the proof of Lemma
V.2.3
: either the first inequality is an equality, or one of the four terms

x_{2}^{2}+\dots+x_{h+1}^{2}
,
x_{h+2}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2}
,
y_{1}^{2}
,
y_{2}^{2}
at
w_{3}
should be greater or equal than
\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}^{2}/4
.
The coordinates of
w_{3}
are given by (
V.2.10
), so
w_{3}\in\partial C_{\textrm{safe}}
implies that one of the following holds.

(a)

e^{-2s_{0}}\Delta_{-}\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}^{2}/4
;

(b)

e^{2s_{0}}\Delta_{+}\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}^{2}/4
;

(c)

\widetilde{y}_{1}\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}/2
;

(d)

\widetilde{y}_{1}\geq-\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}/2
;

(e)

|y_{2}|\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}/2
;

(f)

|e^{-2s_{0}}x_{1}|\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
.

We now show that among these possibilities some are self-contradictory (given (
V.2.9
)),
the others lead to
F(w_{3})>c_{+}
.

•

Case (a) is impossible, because
\Delta_{-}<\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}
; compare (
V.2.9
).

•

If (b) holds, then in (
V.2.12
) we use that
\Delta_{-}\leq\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}
,
e^{s_{0}}>1
and (
V.2.11
)
to get
F(w_{3})-c_{+}\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}^{2}-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}
. If
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}
is small enough compared with
\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}
, we have
F(w_{3})-c_{+}>0
.

•

In case (c) we have
F(w_{3})-c_{+}\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}-e^{-2s_{0}}\Delta_{-}\geq\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}-\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}^{2}
. This difference is positive
if
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}
is sufficiently small.

•

Case (d) contradicts (
V.2.11
).

•

Case (e) cannot occur, because
y_{2}
is constant on the trajectory, so we must have
|y_{2}|\leq\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}
.

•

Finally, we discuss case (f). Note that
|x_{1}|<\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
by (
V.2.9
). As
s_{0}>0
,
we have that
|e^{-2s_{0}}x_{1}|<\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
.

We have shown that
F(w_{3})>c_{+}
, so the lemma is proved.
∎

Condition V.2.6
.

The value of
\varepsilon_{\textrm{fng}}
is chosen in such a way that the
statements of Lemmas
V.2.1
and
V.2.3
hold.

The values of
\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}
and
\varepsilon_{\textrm{vs}}

will be adjusted in later subsections according to Lemmas
V.2.3
and
V.2.5
.

V.2.3.
The inner shell
V_{\textrm{inn}}

We will also define the set
V_{\textrm{inn}}
. First introduce the following piece of notation.
For every
\alpha\in[0,1]
we denote

(V.2.13)

c_{-\alpha}=c_{+}+(c_{\textrm{bot}}-c_{+})\alpha\textrm{ and }c_{+\alpha}=c_{+}+(c_{\textrm{top}}-c_{+})\alpha.

For
\varepsilon_{R}>0
consider a cylinder

(V.2.14)

V_{\textrm{inn}}(\varepsilon_{R})=\{x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{R}^{2},y_{1}\in[c_{-1/2}-c_{+},c_{+1/2}-c_{+}],y_{2}\in[-\varepsilon_{R},1+\varepsilon_{R}]\}\subseteq V_{\textrm{out}}.

Lemma V.2.7
.

For
\varepsilon_{R}
small enough, the interior of
V_{\textrm{inn}}(\varepsilon_{R})
is a subset of
V_{\textrm{mid}}
.

Proof.

The intersection of all of the
V_{\textrm{inn}}(\varepsilon_{R})
is the 2-dimensional rectangle
(0,\dots,0,t,s)
,
t\in[c_{-1/2}-c_{+},c_{+1/2}-c_{+}]
,
s\in[0,1]
. This
rectangle belongs to
V_{\textrm{mid}}
and we can choose a neighbourhood
U_{\textrm{fng}}
of the rectangle contained in
V_{\textrm{mid}}
. For
\varepsilon_{R_{0}}
sufficiently small,
the interior of
V_{\textrm{inn}}(\varepsilon_{R_{0}})
(interior in
F^{-1}[c_{-1/2},c_{+1/2}]
) is contained in
U_{\textrm{fng}}
. Hence, for
\varepsilon_{R}=\varepsilon_{R_{0}}/2
,
the whole of
V_{\textrm{inn}}(\varepsilon_{R})
belongs to
V_{\textrm{mid}}
.
∎

Condition V.2.8
.

From now on set
\varepsilon_{R}
in such a way that

V_{\textrm{inn}}:=V_{\textrm{inn}}(\varepsilon_{R})

is contained in
V_{\textrm{mid}}
; compare Figure
41
.

V_{\textrm{out}}
V_{\textrm{mid}}
V_{\textrm{inn}}
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{bot}})
F^{-1}(c_{\textrm{top}})
F^{-1}(c_{-1/2})
F^{-1}(c_{1/2})

Figure 41.
The position of the sets
V_{\textrm{inn}}
,

and

presented in a two-dimensional schematic. The dotted lines represent the trajectories
of
\xi
connecting
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
.

The purpose of the sets
V_{\textrm{inn}},V_{\textrm{mid}}
and
V_{\textrm{out}}
will be as follows.

•

V_{\textrm{inn}}
is the set where the finger move is conducted.

•

V_{\textrm{mid}}
is the set where the vector field
\xi
is changed after the finger move, so that it is still a grim vector field of some function.

•

V_{\textrm{out}}
is the set where we control the trajectories of the vector field
\xi
.

V.3.
The finger move

Having defined a suitable neighbourhood of the guiding curve in Subsection
V.1
, we
pass to an explicit description of
the finger move in Subsection
V.3.1
, that is, of the isotopy
G_{1+\tau}
. Note that we assume that
G_{\tau}\equiv G
for

\tau\in[0,1]
.
In Subsection
V.3.2
we show how to change the vector field
\xi
to a new vector field
\widehat{\xi}
.
In Subsection
V.3.3
we study the critical points of
\widehat{\xi}
and show that
\partial_{\widehat{\xi}}F\geq 0
,
which shows that
\widehat{\xi}
is a good candidate for a grim vector field.
It turns out that
\widehat{\xi}
vanishes on the newly created sphere of double points. In fact, the function
F
is not an immersed Morse function on
G_{2}(M)

(it has non-isolated critical points on the second stratum after the finger move).
Therefore, we define an explicit perturbation of both
F
and

\widehat{\xi}
near that sphere of double points. This is done
in Subsection
V.3.4
, where we define the path
F_{\tau}
for
\tau\in[0,1]
and a perturbation of
\widehat{\xi}
, denoted by
\overline{\xi}
.
Property
(FM-1)
of Finger Move Theorem is proved in Subsection
V.3.4
, and
(FM-2)
follows immediately from the construction
of
F_{\tau}
.
The key property of
\widehat{\xi}
, namely
(FM-3)
, is proved in Section
V.4
.

V.3.1.
Explicit description of the finger move

As described in Section
VI.3
via an explicit example, we will start at the level
c_{+1/4}
.
We will push
M
along the guiding curve
\varpi
.
The length of the push will vary as the height varies.

By
(V-2)
, the intersection of
M
with
V_{\textrm{out}}
consists of two connected components. The component containing
p_{+}
is

M_{p_{+}}:=M\cap U_{\textrm{out}}.

The second component belongs to
\{y_{2}=1\}
. We consider its part contained in
V_{\textrm{inn}}
.

M_{\textrm{orig}}=M\cap V_{\textrm{inn}}\cap\{y_{2}=1\}.

Now we define the finger move, that is, the path
G_{1+\tau}

of immersions such that
G_{1}=G
.
Choose
u\in N
. If
G(u)\notin M_{\textrm{orig}}
, we set
G_{1+\tau}(u)=G(u)
for all
\tau\in[0,1]
.
If
G(u)=(x_{1},\dots,y_{2})\in M_{\textrm{orig}}
, we set

(V.3.1)

G_{1+\tau}(u):=(x_{1},\dots,y_{1},(1-\tau)y_{2}+\tau\mathfrak{s}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1}))\in V_{\textrm{inn}},

\tau\in[0,1]
, for a function
\mathfrak{s}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}

which we will define shortly. Note that as
F
does not depend on the
y_{2}
coordinate, see property
(V-3)
, we immediately obtain.

Lemma V.3.1
.

The composition
F\circ G_{1+\tau}
is equal to
F\circ G
.

By definition, the finger move replaces the original part of
M
,
M_{\textrm{orig}}
by

(V.3.2)

M_{\textrm{fng}}:=\{x_{1}=0,y_{2}=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1})\},

that is to say
G_{2}(N)=(M\setminus M_{\textrm{orig}})\cup M_{\textrm{fng}}
.
We define

M_{\textrm{new}}=G_{2}(N)
.
To complete the construction, we need to define
\mathfrak{s}
. Set

(V.3.3)

\mathfrak{s}(x_{1},\dots,y_{1})=\mathfrak{a}(y_{1})\min\{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2},\varepsilon_{R}^{2}\}+\mathfrak{b}(y_{1}),

where
\mathfrak{a}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
and
\mathfrak{b}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are two functions such that

(V.3.4)

\mathfrak{a}(y_{1})\varepsilon_{R}^{2}+\mathfrak{b}(y_{1})\equiv 1.

The function
\mathfrak{a}
is nonnegative. When all
x_{i}=0
, the function is
\mathfrak{b}(y_{1})
.
Moreover we require that:

Condition V.3.2
.

(A-1)

\mathfrak{b}\equiv 1
(and so
\mathfrak{a}\equiv 0
) for
y_{1}\notin[c_{-1/4}-c_{+},c_{+1/4}-c_{+}]
;

(A-2)

\mathfrak{b}
has a minimum at
y_{1}=0
, it is non-increasing for
y_{1}>0
and non-decreasing for
y_{1}<0
;

(A-3)

\mathfrak{b}(0)<0
and
|\mathfrak{b}(0)|<\varepsilon_{R}
, so that
M_{\textrm{fng}}\subseteq V_{\textrm{inn}}
;

(A-4)

\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}(y)\neq 0
whenever
\mathfrak{b}(y)=0
.

A graph of an exemplary function for
\mathfrak{b}
is presented in Figure
42
. A schematic of the finger move is depicted in Figure
43
.

y_{1}
\mathfrak{b}(y_{1})

c_{\textrm{top}}

\mathfrak{b}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Remark
.

As the reader may have noticed,
M_{\textrm{new}}
has corners along the common boundary of
M_{\textrm{fng}}
and
M_{\textrm{orig}}
.
These corners can be avoided by taking a smooth approximation of
\min(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+\ldots+x_{n}^{2},\varepsilon_{R}^{2})
in (
V.3.2
). We will not do that explicitly because
it would make the exposition even more complicated.

Note that
M_{\textrm{new}}
has extra double points. In fact, we have the following result towards proving property
(FM-6)

Lemma V.3.3
.

The map
G_{1+\tau}
is an immersion and it is regular for all values of
\tau
except for a single value of
\tau
.

Proof.

Since
G_{1}
is an immersion, and
G_{\tau}
changes only the coordinate
y_{2}
, which was constant on
M_{\textrm{orig}}
,
it is clear that
G_{\tau}
is an immersion.
In fact, an easy calculation shows that the double point set of
G_{\tau}(M_{\textrm{orig}})\cap M_{p_{+}}

intersect along the sphere
x_{1}=y_{1}=y_{2}=0
and
\{x_{2}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2}=\varepsilon_{R}^{2}-\frac{1}{\tau\mathfrak{a}(0)}\}
.
The only
\tau
for which the immersion is not regular, is when the radius of the sphere is zero, that is
\tau=\varepsilon_{R}^{2}/\mathfrak{a}(0)
.
∎

From this lemma we deduce that

M_{p_{+}}\cap M_{\textrm{fng}}
is given by

(V.3.5)

\Sigma=\{x_{2}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2}=\varepsilon_{\Sigma}^{2}\}\cap\{x_{1}=y_{1}=y_{2}=0\},

where

(V.3.6)

\varepsilon_{\Sigma}^{2}=-\frac{\mathfrak{b}(0)}{\mathfrak{a}(0)}.

The double point set is an
(n-2)
-dimensional sphere.
Note that the two branches of
M
intersect transversely.

The last observation in this subsection prove
(FM-6)
.

Lemma V.3.4
.

The components
M_{\textrm{orig}}
and
M_{p_{+}}
of
M\cap V_{\textrm{out}}
belong to the same connected component of
M
.

Proof.

The part
M_{p_{+}}
is a subset of the connected component of
M
containing
p_{+}
. Note that
p_{+}
and
p_{-}

are connected by a single trajectory of
\xi
, hence
M_{p_{+}}
belongs to the same connected component of
M
as
p_{-}
.
The intersection of the ascending membrane
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{a}}(p_{-})
with
M
belongs to the same connected component of
M
as
p_{-}
.
The endpoint of the guiding curve
\varpi(1)
belongs to the same connected component of
M
as
p_{-}
, because it belongs
to the ascending membrane. But
M_{\textrm{orig}}
contains
\varpi(1)
by construction.
∎

V.3.2.
Construction of
\widehat{\xi}

The next goal is to modify the vector field
\xi
to obtain a vector field
\widehat{\xi}
, which will be perturbed to
\overline{\xi}
later on.
The definition of
\widehat{\xi}
is more important, because it will already have the property
(FM-3)
from Theorem
IV.4.13
– one fewer trajectory between
p_{-}
and
p_{+}
. Note that

(V.3.7)

\partial_{\xi}\mathfrak{s}(x_{1},\ldots,y_{2})=0\textrm{ if }x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+\ldots+x_{n}^{2}\geq\varepsilon_{R}^{2},

which follows immediately from (
V.3.3
), since then
\mathfrak{s}
is constant.
It is easy to see that the vector field

(V.3.8)

\widetilde{\xi}:=(-x_{1},\ldots,-x_{h+1},x_{h+2},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2},\partial_{\xi}\mathfrak{s})=\xi+\partial_{\xi}\mathfrak{s}\frac{\partial\phantom{y}}{\partial y_{2}}

is tangent to
M_{\textrm{fng}}
.
Moreover, by
(V-3)
we have
\frac{\partial\phantom{y}}{\partial y_{2}}F=0
, hence

(V.3.9)

\partial_{\widetilde{\xi}}F=\partial_{\xi}F

everywhere on
V_{\textrm{out}}
.

The two vector fields
\xi
and
\widetilde{\xi}
do not match on
M_{\textrm{fng}}\cap M_{p_{+}}
. To rectify this, we will interpolate between
\xi
and
\widetilde{\xi}
, to obtain a third vector field, which will be
\widehat{\xi}
.
The most naive approach, that is, to define the new vector field
\widehat{\xi}

as a linear combination of
\xi
and
\widetilde{\xi}
multiplied by two functions vanishing
on
M_{\textrm{fng}}
and
M_{p_{+}}
respectively, does not quite work, because a nontrivial linear combination of
\xi
and
\widetilde{\xi}
does not have
to be tangent to the stratum
M_{\textrm{fng}}\cap M_{p_{+}}
.

Let
e_{1}
be the vector field

(V.3.10)

e_{1}:=(0,x_{2},\ldots,x_{n},0,0).

We claim that

(V.3.11)

\partial_{e_{1}}\mathfrak{s}>0

everywhere on
\Sigma
. To see this,
note that if
x_{1}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{R}^{2}
(which holds
on
\Sigma
), then

\partial_{e_{1}}\mathfrak{s}=2(x_{2}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2})\mathfrak{a}(y_{1})+\mathfrak{b}(y_{1}).

On
\Sigma
,
x_{2}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2}=\varepsilon_{\Sigma}^{2}
, so
\partial_{e_{1}}\mathfrak{s}=2\varepsilon_{\Sigma}^{2}\mathfrak{a}(0)+\mathfrak{b}(0)
. By (
V.3.6
),
we obtain
\partial_{e_{1}}\mathfrak{s}=\varepsilon_{\Sigma}^{2}\mathfrak{a}(0)>0
.

.

The latter is possible because of (

).

Now we adjust
\varepsilon_{\textrm{safe}}
to satisfy the assumptions of Lemma

and by the following condition:

Condition V.3.6
.

C_{\textrm{safe}}

, the closures of

are disjoint, and
C_{\textrm{safe}}

M_{\textrm{fng}}
.

Set
\rho_{\textrm{fng}}\colon V_{\textrm{mid}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
to be the square of the distance to
M_{\textrm{fng}}
, and set
\rho_{+}\colon V_{\textrm{mid}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

to be the square of the distance to
M_{p_{+}}
. Finally, choose two bump functions
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\colon\Omega\to[0,1]
, where
\phi_{1}
is a function
supported on
V_{\textrm{mid}}
, equal to
1
on
W
, and
\phi_{2}
is a function supported in
W
and equal to
1
on
W^{\prime}

In
W
, define the decomposition of the tangent bundle

T\Omega
into
E_{\textrm{tan}}
,
E_{1}=\sspan\{\frac{\partial\phantom{y}}{\partial y_{1}},(\partial_{e_{1}}\mathfrak{s})\frac{\partial\phantom{y}}{\partial y_{2}}+e_{1}\}
and
E_{2}=\sspan\{e_{1},\frac{\partial\phantom{x}}{\partial x_{1}}\}
.
By Condition
V.3.5
,
E_{1}\cap E_{2}=0
.
The subbundle
E_{\textrm{tan}}
can be defined as the orthogonal complement of
E_{1}
in
\sspan\{\frac{\partial\phantom{x}}{\partial x_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial\phantom{x}}{\partial x_{n}}\}
.
Recall that we are still using the metric for which the coordinate vectors
\frac{\partial\phantom{x}}{\partial x_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial\phantom{y}}{\partial y_{2}}
are
orthonormal.

The bundle
E_{\textrm{tan}}
is orthogonal to
E_{1}\oplus E_{2}
,
but
E_{1}
is not necessarily orthogonal to
E_{2}
.
By definition

(E_{\textrm{tan}}\oplus E_{2})|_{M_{p_{+}}\cap W}
is the tangent bundle to
M_{p_{+}}\cap W
, and
(E_{\textrm{tan}}\oplus E_{1})|_{M_{\textrm{fng}}\cap W}
is the tangent bundle to
M_{\textrm{fng}}\cap W
.
Moreover,

E_{\textrm{tan}}
restricted to
M_{p_{+}}\cap M_{\textrm{fng}}
is the tangent bundle to
M_{p_{+}}\cap M_{\textrm{fng}}
.

According to the decomposition
T\Omega=E_{\textrm{tan}}\oplus E_{1}\oplus E_{2}

we write
\xi=\xi_{\textrm{tan}}+\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}
and
\widetilde{\xi}=\widetilde{\xi}_{\textrm{tan}}+\widetilde{\xi}_{1}+\widetilde{\xi}_{2}
on
W
. Notice that
\widetilde{\xi}-\xi
is parallel to
\frac{\partial\phantom{y}}{\partial y_{2}}
,
which belongs to
E_{1}\oplus E_{2}
, hence
\widetilde{\xi}_{\textrm{tan}}=\xi_{\textrm{tan}}
in
W
.
Set

\Upsilon=\bigg(\frac{\rho_{\textrm{fng}}}{\rho_{\textrm{fng}}+\rho_{+}}\bigg)^{\phi_{3}}.

Then
\Upsilon\equiv 1
on
C_{\textrm{safe}}
. Also
\Upsilon\equiv 0
on the whole of
M_{\textrm{fng}}
and
\Upsilon\equiv 1
on the whole of
M_{p_{+}}
(
\rho_{+}=0
there); see
Figure
45
. The function
\Upsilon
is not well-defined on
M_{\textrm{fng}}\cap M_{p_{+}}
, but, as we will see below, this does not affect the correctness
of the definition of
\widehat{\xi}
.
The interpolated vector field is given by:

(V.3.12)

\widehat{\xi}=\xi^{a}+\xi^{b}+\xi^{c}+\xi^{d},

where:

\displaystyle\xi^{a}

\displaystyle=(1-\phi_{1})\xi

\displaystyle\xi^{b}

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}\left(\Upsilon\xi_{\textrm{tan}}+(1-\Upsilon)\widetilde{\xi}_{\textrm{tan}}\right)

\displaystyle\xi^{c}

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(1-\phi_{2})\left(\Upsilon(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})+(1-\Upsilon)(\widetilde{\xi}_{1}+\widetilde{\xi}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\xi^{d}

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\left(\rho_{+}\widetilde{\xi}+\rho_{\textrm{fng}}\xi\right).

C_{\textrm{safe}}
\phi_{3}\equiv 0,\ \Upsilon=1
p_{2}

Figure
44
revisited. We indicate the values of
\rho_{\textrm{fng}}
,
\rho_{+}
and
\Upsilon
.

The form of
\widehat{\xi}
in different regions is given in Lemma
V.3.8
and Lemma
V.3.10
.

Remark
.

Notice that
\xi_{\textrm{tan}}
,
\widetilde{\xi}_{\textrm{tan}}
, and
\xi_{i},\widetilde{\xi}_{i}
for
i=1,2
, are defined only on
W
. However, outside
W
the vector field
\widehat{\xi}

depends only on the sum
\xi_{\textrm{tan}}+\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}
and
\widetilde{\xi}_{\textrm{tan}}+\widetilde{\xi}_{1}+\widetilde{\xi}_{2}
. Therefore, to define
\widehat{\xi}
everywhere on
V_{\textrm{mid}}
, extend
\xi_{i}
,
\widetilde{\xi}_{i}
over
V_{\textrm{mid}}
in such a way that
\xi_{\textrm{tan}}+\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}=\xi
and
\widetilde{\xi}_{\textrm{tan}}+\widetilde{\xi}_{1}+\widetilde{\xi}_{2}=\widetilde{\xi}
, and then
\widehat{\xi}

will not depend on the extension.

One can think of
\widehat{\xi}
on
M_{p_{+}}
as taking the vector field
\xi
and slowing it in the transverse direction to
M_{p_{+}}

as the trajectory draws near
M_{p_{+}}
.

In the following series of lemmas we explain the formulae for
\widehat{\xi}
and show that
\widehat{\xi}
is
tangent to
M_{\textrm{fng}}
and to
M_{p_{+}}
.

Lemma V.3.7
.

If
w\notin V_{\textrm{mid}}
, then
\widehat{\xi}(w)=\xi(w)
.

Proof.

We have
\phi_{1}(w)=0
, so
\xi^{a}=\xi
and
\xi^{b}=\xi^{c}=\xi^{d}=0
.
∎

Lemma V.3.8
.

If
w\in V_{\textrm{mid}}\setminus V_{\textrm{inn}}
, then
\widehat{\xi}
is a linear combination of
\xi
and
\widetilde{\xi}
; more precisely

(V.3.13)

\widehat{\xi}=((1-\phi_{1})+\phi_{1}\Upsilon)\xi+\phi_{1}(1-\Upsilon)\widetilde{\xi}.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\phi_{2}\equiv 0
on
V_{\textrm{mid}}\setminus V_{\textrm{inn}}
.
∎

Lemma V.3.9
.

If
w\in V_{\textrm{mid}}\setminus V_{\textrm{inn}}
and
w\in M_{\textrm{fng}}
, then
\widehat{\xi}
is tangent to
M_{\textrm{fng}}
; more precisely

\widehat{\xi}=\xi
on
M_{\textrm{fng}}\cap(V_{\textrm{mid}}\setminus V_{\textrm{inn}})
.

Proof.

On
M_{\textrm{fng}}\setminus V_{\textrm{inn}}
we have
x_{2}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2}\geq\varepsilon_{R}^{2}
. Therefore, by the definition
of
\widetilde{\xi}
we have
\widetilde{\xi}=\xi
. We conclude by (
V.3.13
).
∎

Lemma V.3.10
.

Suppose
w\in V_{\textrm{inn}}\setminus W
. Then
\widehat{\xi}(w)=\Upsilon\xi(w)+(1-\Upsilon)\widetilde{\xi}(w)
. In particular,
\widehat{\xi}(w)=\xi(w)
if
w\in M_{p_{+}}
and
\widehat{\xi}(w)=\widetilde{\xi}(w)

if
w\in M_{\textrm{fng}}
.

Proof.

We have
\phi_{1}\equiv 1
and
\phi_{2}\equiv 0
, hence
\xi^{a}=\xi^{d}=0
and so

\widehat{\xi}=\xi^{b}+\xi^{c}=\Upsilon\xi+(1-\Upsilon)\widetilde{\xi}.

The statement follows, since
\Upsilon(w)=0
if
w\in M_{\textrm{fng}}
and
\Upsilon(w)=1
if
w\in M_{p_{+}}
.
∎

As
C_{\textrm{safe}}\subseteq V_{\textrm{inn}}\setminus W
and
\Upsilon\equiv 1
on
C_{\textrm{safe}}
we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary V.3.11
.

On
C_{\textrm{safe}}
we have
\widehat{\xi}=\xi
.

Lemma V.3.12
.

Suppose
w\in W\setminus W^{\prime}
. Then
\widehat{\xi}
is a nonnegative linear combination of
\xi
,
\widetilde{\xi}
and
\xi_{\textrm{tan}}
, more precisely

(V.3.14)

\widehat{\xi}=(1-\phi_{2})(\Upsilon\xi+(1-\Upsilon)\widetilde{\xi})+\phi_{2}(\rho_{+}\widetilde{\xi}+\rho_{\textrm{fng}}\xi+\xi_{\textrm{tan}}).

Proof.

We use the fact that
\xi_{\textrm{tan}}=\widetilde{\xi}_{\textrm{tan}}
in
W
.
∎

Lemma V.3.13
.

Suppose
w\in W\setminus W^{\prime}
. If
w\in M_{p_{+}}
, then
\widehat{\xi}(w)
is tangent to
M_{p_{+}}
, and if
w\in M_{\textrm{fng}}
, then
\widehat{\xi}(w)
is tangent to
M_{\textrm{fng}}
.

Proof.

This follows from (
V.3.14
). If
w\in M_{p_{+}}
, then
\Upsilon(w)=1
and
\rho_{+}(w)=0
, while if
w\in M_{\textrm{fng}}
, then
\Upsilon(w)=0
and
\rho_{\textrm{fng}}(w)=0
. The vector field
\xi_{\textrm{tan}}

is tangent both to
M_{p_{+}}
and to
M_{\textrm{fng}}
.
∎

Lemma V.3.14
.

If
w\in W^{\prime}
then
\xi(w)
is still given by (
V.3.14
), but the formula simplifies further to

(V.3.15)

\widehat{\xi}(w)=\xi_{\textrm{tan}}(w)+\rho_{+}\widetilde{\xi}_{1}(w)+\rho_{\textrm{fng}}\xi_{2}(w).

In particular
\widehat{\xi}
is well-defined (even if
\Upsilon
is not) and
\widehat{\xi}
is tangent to
M_{\textrm{fng}}
,
M_{p_{+}}
and
M_{\textrm{fng}}\cap M_{p_{+}}
.

Proof.

We have
\phi_{1}(w)=\phi_{2}(w)=1
, so
\xi^{a}=\xi^{c}=0
. Moreover
\xi_{\textrm{tan}}=\widetilde{\xi}_{\textrm{tan}}
, hence
\xi^{b}=\xi_{\textrm{tan}}
. The formula for
\xi^{d}

simplifies to
\xi^{d}=\rho_{+}\widetilde{\xi}_{1}+\rho_{\textrm{fng}}\xi_{2}
. Hence we obtain (
V.3.15
) and from this formula the tangency of
\widehat{\xi}

follows immediately.
∎

Combining the above lemmas we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary V.3.15
.

The vector field
\widehat{\xi}
is tangent to
M_{p_{+}}
,
M_{\textrm{fng}}
and
M_{p_{+}}\cap M_{\textrm{fng}}
.

V_{\textrm{mid}}
V_{\textrm{inn}}
M_{p_{2}}
M_{\textrm{fng}}
W
W^{\prime}
W
W^{\prime}
C_{\textrm{safe}}
p_{2}
\widehat{\xi}=\xi
Lemma
V.3.7
\widehat{\xi}\in\textrm{span}(\xi,\widetilde{\xi})
Lemma
V.3.8
\widehat{\xi}=\Upsilon\xi+(1-\Upsilon)\widetilde{\xi}
Lemma
V.3.10
\widehat{\xi}=(1-\phi_{2})(\Upsilon\xi+(1-\Upsilon)\widetilde{\xi})+\phi_{2}(\rho_{2}\widetilde{\xi}+\rho_{\textrm{fng}}\xi+\xi_{\textrm{tan}})
Lemma
V.3.12
\widehat{\xi}(w)=\xi_{\textrm{tan}}(w)+\rho_{2}\widetilde{\xi}_{1}(w)+\rho_{\textrm{fng}}\xi_{2}(w)
Lemma
V.3.14
\widehat{\xi}=\xi
Corollary
V.3.11
